“Jeremy, lad, you behold two hungry men waiting to sample the art of the best cook in the Borton Hundreds. Have you the wherewithal, or must we go back to your wife?”

“I has, I surely has,” was the answer. “Be pleased to be seated, kind sirs, and Jerry Tusser will have your meat ready before ye have rightly eased your legs. This way, Master, this way.”

He led them to a pit where a fire burned between three stones and a kettle bubbled. Plates of coarse earthenware were brought from some hiding-place, and in five minutes Alastair was supping with an iron spoon as savoury a stew as he had ever eaten. The fruits of Jeremy’s snares were in it, and the fruits of Jeremy’s old fowling piece, and it was flavoured with herbs whose merits the world has forgotten. The hot meal quickened his vigour, and he was on his feet before Midwinter had done, like a dog eager to be on the road again.

===Chinese===

仲冬在一根树干上坐下。

“杰里米小子，你瞧，两条饿汉子正等着领教波顿百户区（Borton Hundreds）第一名厨的手艺呢。家伙什儿齐备么，还是我们得折回找你老婆去？”

“有的，俺管保有，”回答是这么一句。“两位好老爷请坐，杰里·塔瑟（Jerry Tusser）保管二位腿还没坐舒坦，肉就得了。这边请，老爷，这边请。”

他领两人到一个坑边，三块石头间烧着火，一口锅咕嘟作响。粗陶盘子从什么藏处取了出来，不出五分钟，阿拉斯泰尔已握着铁匙，喝上生平数得着的一锅好炖肉。锅里有着杰里米套索的出产，也有着杰里米那杆老火铳的出产，调味的香草，好处早被世人忘了。热腾腾一顿饭下肚，他的精力愈发旺了，仲冬还没吃完他已站起身来，像条急着想重新上路的狗。

===Original===

He heard the man speak low to Midwinter. “Dook o’ Kingston’s horse,” he heard and a hand was jerked northward.

In the afternoon the way lay across more open country, which Midwinter seemed to know like the palm of his hand, for he made points for some ridge or treetop, and yet was never held up by brook or fence or dwelling. The air had grown sharper, clouds were banking in the east, and a wind was moaning in the tops of the high trees. Alastair seemed to have been restored to the clean world of his youth, after long absence among courts and cities. He noted the woodcock flitting between the bracken and the leafless boughs, and the mallards silently flighting from mere to stubble. A wedge of geese moving south made him turn his face skyward, and a little later he heard a wild whistle, and saw far up in the heavens a line of swans. His bodily strength was great as ever, but he had ceased to exult in it, and was ready to observe and meditate.

A highway cut the forest, and the two behind a bush of box watched a company of riders jingle down it. They were rustic fellows, poor horsemen most of them, mounted on every variety of beast, and at the head rode a smarter youth, with brand-new holsters out of which peeped the butts of ancient pistols.

“Recruits for the Duke of Kingston,” Midwinter whispered. “They rendezvous at Nottingham, I hear. Think you they will make a good match of it with your Highland claymores?”

===Chinese===

他听见那汉子低声对仲冬说话。“金斯顿公爵家的马儿，”他听见这么一句，一只手朝北面猛地一扬。

午后，路途横穿更为开阔的乡野。这片地方仲冬熟悉得如同自己的掌纹，他认准一道山梁或一株树顶作目标，却从不曾被溪流、栅栏或人家拦住。空气愈见凛冽，云在东方成堆积聚，高树的梢头有风呜咽。仿佛在宫廷与城市间久客之后，阿拉斯泰尔被送回了他少年那个洁净的世界。他留意到丘鹬在蕨丛与秃枝间翻飞，绿头鸭从水泽悄然飞往茬地。一列南去的大雁排成楔形，引得他仰起脸，不多时他又听见一声野唳，看见极高的天上一行天鹅。

一条大路切开森林，两人躲在一丛黄杨后面，看一队骑手叮叮当当地顺路而下。都是些乡野汉子，大半骑术蹩脚，胯下什么牲口都有；队首是个齐整些的青年，鞍上挂着崭新的枪套，套里露出古旧手枪的枪柄。

“金斯顿公爵（Duke of Kingston）招的新兵，”仲冬低声道。“听说他们在诺丁汉（Nottingham）集结。您说，他们配不配同你们高地人的阔剑较量一场？”

===Original===

Night fell when they were still in the open, and Midwinter, after halting for a second to take his bearings, led the way to a wood which seemed to flow in and out of a shallow vale.

“The night will be cold, Captain Maclean, and a wise man takes comfort when he can find it. I could find you twenty lodgings, but we will take the warmest.”

The woodland path ended in a road which seemed to be the avenue to a great house. It was soon very dark, and Alastair heard the rustling of animals which revived some ancestral knowledge, for he could distinguish the different noises which were rabbit, badger, stoat and deer. Down the avenue Midwinter led unconcernedly, and then turned off to a group of buildings which might have been stables. He bade Alastair wait while he went forward, and after some delay returned with a man who carried a lantern. The fellow, seen in the dim light, was from his dress an upper servant, and his bearing was in the extreme respectful. He bowed to Alastair, and led them through a gate into a garden, where their feet rang on flagged stones and rustled against box borders. A mass loomed up on the left which proved to be a great mansion.

The servant admitted them by a side door, and led them to a room, where he lit a dozen candles from his lantern, and revealed a panelled octagonal chamber hung with full-length portraits of forbidding gentlemen. There he left them, and when he returned it was with an elderly butler in undress, who bowed with the same deferent decorum.

===Chinese===

暮色降临时两人还在旷野里，仲冬停了一瞬辨认方位，便领路向一片树林走去，那林子仿佛在一条浅谷间流进流出。

“今夜会很冷，麦克莱恩上尉，聪明人遇上安适处便不放过。我能给您找出二十处宿头，咱们却要挑最暖和的一处。”

林间小径的尽头接上一条大路，看那气象，像是通向一座大宅的林荫道。天很快就黑透了，阿拉斯泰尔听见各种动物窸窣的声音，唤醒了他血脉里某种远祖的知识，因为他能分辨出哪些响动是兔、是獾、是鼬、是鹿。仲冬满不在乎地沿林荫道前行，随后拐向一片建筑，那像是马厩。他让阿拉斯泰尔稍候，自己上前去，隔了好一阵才带着一个提灯笼的人回来。暗淡灯光下看得出，那人从衣着是个上等仆役，举止恭敬到了极处。他向阿拉斯泰尔躬身，领着两人穿过一道门进了园子，脚下在石板上踏出脆响，蹭过黄杨镶边沙沙作响。左首黑压压一大团，走近了原来是一座巨宅。

仆役引两人从一道边门入内，带进一个房间，用灯笼点亮了一打蜡烛，照出一间镶板的八角形屋子，四壁挂着神情冷峻的绅士们的全身画像。他撂下两人走了，回来时带着一位衣冠未整的老管家，那管家以同样恭谨的礼数躬身行礼。

===Original===

“His lordship has gone since yesterday into Yorkshire, sir,” he informed Midwinter. “I will have the usual rooms made ready for you at once, and you can sup in my lord’s cabinet which is adjacent.”

The two travellers soon found themselves warming their feet before a bright fire, while some thousands of volumes in calf and vellum looked down on them from the walls. They supped royally, but Alastair was too drowsy for talk, and his body had scarcely touched the sheets of his bed before he was asleep. He was woke before dawn, shaved and dressed by the butler, and given breakfast﻿—with China tea in place of beer﻿—in the same cabinet. It was still dark when the first servant of the night before conducted them out of the house by the same side door, led them across the shadowy park, and through a gate in the wall ushered them out to a dusky common, where trees in the creeping light stood up like gibbets. Midwinter led off at a trot, and at a trot they crossed the common and put more than one little valley behind them, so that when day dawned fully there was no sign in all the landscape of their night’s lodging.

“Whose was the house?” Alastair asked, and was told﻿—“We name no names in Old England.”

===Chinese===

“老爷从昨天起去了约克郡（Yorkshire），先生，”他禀告仲冬。“我这就叫人把惯常住的那几间屋子收拾出来，二位可以在我老爷的书房里用晚饭，就在隔壁。”

两位旅人不久便坐在明亮的炉火前烤脚，墙上几千卷小牛皮和犊皮纸装帧的书俯视着他们。晚饭吃得丰美如王侯，但阿拉斯泰尔困得懒得说话，身子刚沾床单便睡熟了。天亮之前他被叫醒，由管家伺候着修面穿衣，仍在那间书房用了早餐——以中国茶代了啤酒。天还黑着，昨夜那个仆役便领他们从同一道边门出了宅子，穿过朦影幢幢的苑囿，从墙上一道门引他们出到一片昏昧的公地上，晨光爬动之处，树木像绞刑架一样立着。仲冬撒开小跑领路，两人一路小跑穿过公地，把不止一道小谷甩在身后，待到天光大亮，满目山川里已全然看不出他们昨夜的宿处。

“那是谁家的宅子？”阿拉斯泰尔问，得到的回答是——“在老英格兰，我们不提名字。”

===Original===

The second day was to Alastair like the first for joy in the movement of travel, but the weather had grown bitterly cold and unfallen snow was heavy in the leaden sky. The distances were still clear, and though all the morning the road seemed to lie in hollows and dales, yet he had glimpses in the north of high blue ridges. Other signs told him that he was nearing the hills. The streams ceased to be links of sluggish pools, and chattered in rapids. He saw a water ouzel with its white cravat flash from the cover of a stone bridge. A flock of plovers which circled over one heath proved to be not green but golden. He told this to Midwinter, who nodded and pointed to a speck in the sky.

“There is better proof,” he said.

The bird dropped closer to earth, and showed itself as neither sparrow-hawk nor kestrel, but merlin.

“We are nearing the hills,” he said, “but Brightwell is far up the long valleys. We will not reach it before tomorrow night.”

===Chinese===

第二天，在阿拉斯泰尔看来，行路的欢快一如昨日，只是天气冷得砭骨，铅灰的天空中未落之雪沉沉欲坠。远景依然澄澈，虽然一上午道路似乎总躺在洼地与谷地里，他却已望得见北方青蓝的高高山脊。别的征象也告诉他，山快到了。溪流不再是一串滞缓的水潭，而是在急滩上絮絮滔滔。他看见一只系着白围脖的河乌，从一座石桥的掩蔽处闪出。一群在一方荒原上盘旋的鸻鸟，看清楚不是绿的，是金的。他把这说给仲冬听，仲冬点头，指着天空里的一个黑点。

“有更确凿的凭证，”他说。

那鸟俯冲得离地近了些，显出它既非雀鹰也非红隼，而是一只灰背隼。

“我们离山近了，”他说，“但布莱特韦尔还远在长长的谷地深处。明晚之前是到不了的。”

===Original===

Just at the darkening the first snow fell. They were descending a steep boulder-strewn ridge to a stream of some size, which swirled in icy grey pools. Above them hung a tree-crowned hill now dim with night, and ere they reached the cover on its crest the flakes were thick about them. Midwinter grunted, and broke into a trot along the ridge. “Ill weather,” he croaked, “and a harder bed than yestereen. We’ll have to make shift with tinkler’s fare. They told me at Harrowden that Job Lee’s pack were in the Quarters Wood, and Job has some notion of hospitality. Job it must be, for the snow is fairly come.”

In a broad coombe on the sheltered side of the ridge they came presently on a roaring fire of roots with three tents beside it, so placed that they were free alike from wind and smoke. The snow was falling hard, and beginning to drift, when Midwinter strode into the glow, and the man he called Job Lee﻿—a long man with untied hair brushing his shoulders and a waistcoat of dyed deerskin﻿—took his right hand between both of his and carried it to his lips. The newcomers shook themselves like dogs and were allotted one of the tents, thereby ousting two sleeping children who staggered to the hospitality of their father’s bed. They supped off roast hare and strong ale, and slept till the wintry sun had climbed the Derbyshire hills and lit a world all virgin-white.

“The Almighty has sent a skid for our legs,” Midwinter muttered as he watched the wet logs hiss in Job Lee’s morning fire. “We can travel slow, for the roads will be heavy for my lady.” So they did not start till the forenoon was well advanced, and as soon as possible exchanged the clogged and slippery hillside for a valley road. A wayside inn gave them a scrag of boiled mutton for dinner, and thereafter they took a shortcut over a ridge of hill to reach the dale at whose head lay the house of Brightwell. On the summit they halted to reconnoitre, for the highway was visible there for many miles.

Just below them at the road side, where a tributary way branched off, stood an inn of some pretensions, whose sign was deciphered by Alastair’s hawk eyes as a couchant stag. Fresh snow was massing on the horizon, but for the moment the air was diamond clear. There had been little traffic on the road since morning and that only foot passengers, with one horse’s tracks coming down the valley. These tracks did not pass the door, therefore the horseman must be within. There were no signs of a coach’s wheels, so Lady Norreys had not yet arrived. He lifted his eyes and looked down the stream. There, a mile or so distant, moved a dark cluster, a coach apparently and attendant riders.

The snow was on them again and Alastair bowed his head to the blast. “They will lie at that inn,” said Midwinter. “Brightwell is half a dozen miles on, and the road is dangerous. You will, of course, join them. I will accompany you to the door and leave you, for I have business in Sherwood that cannot wait.”

Again Alastair peered through the snow. He saw a man come out of the inn door as in a great hurry, mount a waiting horse, and clatter off up the vale﻿—a tall man in a horseman’s cloak with a high collar. Then a little later came the vanguard of the approaching party to bespeak quarters. The two men watched till the coach came abreast the door, and a slender hooded figure stepped from it. Then they began to make their way down the hillside.

===Chinese===

天刚擦黑，头一场雪落了下来。两人正沿一道乱石嶙峋的陡脊下到一条不小的溪边，溪水在冰冷的灰色水潭里打着旋。头顶上悬着一座戴树冠的山，此刻已隐入夜色，还没等他们爬到山顶林木的掩蔽处，雪片已密密围住了两人。仲冬咕哝一声，顺着山脊撒开小跑。“坏天气，”他哑着嗓子说，“床铺也要比昨夜硬。只好将就一顿补锅匠的饭食了。哈罗登（Harrowden）那边的人告诉我，乔布·李（Job Lee）的一伙在夸特斯林（Quarters Wood），乔布是颇懂些待客之道的。只能是乔布家了，雪是当真下起来了。”

在山脊背风一面的一道宽谷里，两人不久便望见一堆树根烧得毕剥作响的大火，旁边支着三座帐篷，摆的位置既避风又避烟。雪下得紧，开始积了，仲冬大步走进火光，被他唤作乔布·李的那人——一个瘦长汉子，披散的头发扫着肩头，穿一件染色的鹿皮马甲——双手捧起仲冬的右手，送到唇边一吻。新来的两人像狗一样抖了抖身上的雪，被安置进其中一座帐篷，为此挤走了两个睡熟的孩子，孩子跌跌撞撞投奔他们父亲的床榻去了。晚饭是烤野兔和烈性麦酒，一觉睡到冬日的太阳爬上德比郡（Derbyshire）的群山，照亮一个素白初绽的世界。

“全能的神给咱们的腿脚使了个绊子，”仲冬望着湿柴在乔布·李的晨火里嘶嘶作响，喃喃道。“我们可以慢些走，路上的泥泞对夫人不利。”于是直到上午过半他们才动身，一得了机会，便撇下又黏又滑的山坡，换上谷中的大路。一家路旁客栈给他们一顿清炖羊颈肉当正餐，随后他们翻过一道山梁抄近路，去那谷地——布莱特韦尔的宅子就坐落在谷地的顶端。到了梁顶两人停下来侦察，因为从那里，大路望出去有好几英里远。

就在他们脚下的路边，一条岔道分出去的地方，立着一家颇有气派的客栈，招牌上的图样被阿拉斯泰尔的鹰眼辨了出来：一头卧伏的牡鹿。天边正在积起新雪，此刻空气却澄澈如钻。从清晨起路上行人寥寥，且都是步行的人，只有一串马蹄印从谷中下来。蹄印没有过那家店门，可见骑手必在店中。没有车轮的辙迹，可见诺里斯夫人尚未抵达。他抬眼顺着溪流望去。约莫一英里外，一团暗影正在移动，像是一辆马车，带着随行的骑手。

雪又扑到两人身上，阿拉斯泰尔顶着风雪低下头。“他们要在那家客栈过夜了，”仲冬说。“布莱特韦尔还在前面六英里，路况凶险。您自然是去同他们会合。我陪您走到店门口便要告辞，我在舍伍德（Sherwood）有一件等不得的事。”

阿拉斯泰尔又透过雪幕张望。他看见一个人急匆匆走出店门，跨上一匹候着的马，嘚嘚地顺谷而上——是个瘦高个，裹着一件高领的骑装斗篷。稍过片刻，那队将至的人马派出了先行者来订房间。两人望着马车挨近店门，一个纤细的戴兜帽的身影从车上跨了下来。于是他们开始向山坡下走去。

## XVII Ordeal of Honour / 十七、荣誉的裁决
===Original===
The night was growing colder, and the moon in her first quarter was sinking among heavy woolpack clouds. The Spainneach’s whisper had been enough for Alastair, who in his sojourn at the Sleeping Deer had made himself familiar with the neighbourhood, after the fashion of a campaigner who may soon have to fight in it. The road led them past the silent hostelry, and then left the vale and struck over a succession of low ridges to another, where a parallel stream of the hills broadened as it neared the lowlands. The men did not spare their horses, and, as the hooves clattered on the bare ribs of rock which crossed the track sparks like wildfire flew behind them.

Alastair’s mood was as dark as the weather. The sight of Claudia, babbling of her lover, had for a moment converted him to Johnson’s view. In a fine impulse of quixotry he had ridden from Brightwell, his purpose vague towards Sir John Norreys but determined in the service of the lady. If her love was pledged irrevocably to a knave and fool, then be it his business to keep the said knave from greater folly, and see that disillusion did not shatter a gentle heart. For a little he felt the glow of self-conscious worth, and the pleasant melancholy which is born of approving self-pity.

It did not last long. Visions of Claudia, dim-eyed, stammering, all russet and snow, returned to ravish his fancy, and the picture of a certain sharp-nosed gentleman to exacerbate his temper. Before God he could not surrender such a darling, he would be no party to flinging such a pearl before swine! His heart grew hot when he thought of Sir John, the mean visage and hedgehog soul. To condone his infamy would be to sin against Heaven﻿—to foster his lady’s blind fondness the task of a pander. Let the truth be told and the devil be shamed, for a wounded heart was better than a slow decay.

===Chinese===
夜色愈来愈冷，一弯上弦月正沉入堆堆羊毛垛般的浓云里。西班牙人的一句耳语，对阿拉斯泰尔已经尽够了——他在睡鹿客栈下榻的那些日子，早把这一带的地势摸得烂熟，正是行伍中人的做派：这种人不久便可能要在此地开战。道路引着他们掠过寂无声息的客店，随后离开谷地，翻过一道接一道的低岗，进入另一道谷——一条平行的山溪在那谷中渐近低地处愈见开阔。两人都不爱惜马力，马蹄踏在横过路面的裸岩脊上，嘚嘚作响，蹄下迸出的火星如火燎一般飞在身后。

阿拉斯泰尔的心绪同这天气一般阴沉。克劳迪娅絮絮叨叨念着情郎的那副景象，有一时竟把他劝到了约翰逊的看法上。乘着一阵堂吉诃德式的侠气冲动，他纵马离开了布莱特韦尔——对约翰·诺里斯爵士，他所图还模模糊糊；为那位夫人效力，却是打定了主意的。倘若她的爱情已不可挽回地许给了一个恶棍兼蠢货，那么他的本分，便是拦住这恶棍，别教他闯下更大的蠢祸，并留意别让幻灭击碎一颗温柔的心。有一小会儿，他尝到了自觉高尚的暖意，还有那种由自我怜惜、自我嘉许而生出的惬意忧郁。

这份情怀没有维持多久。克劳迪娅的幻影——泪眼婆娑、讷讷无语、一身栗衣映着雪色——又回来攫住他的想象，而某位尖鼻子先生的形影，则回来给他的火气火上浇油。他在上帝面前起誓，他交不出这样一位可人儿，他绝不做同谋，把这样一颗明珠丢到猪跟前！一想到约翰爵士，想到那副猥琐的面孔、刺猬般的灵魂，他的心便烧起来。宽纵他的丑行，便是获罪于天；姑息夫人的盲目痴恋，便是拉皮条的营生。让真相说出口来，教魔鬼蒙羞去吧——一颗受了伤的心，总好过慢慢的溃烂。

===Original===
Presently his mind had swung round to a new resolution. He would go straight to Derby to the Prince, which was his direct soldierly duty. He knew the road; the next left-hand turning would lead him there before morning. He was already weeks, months late; he dared not tarry another hour, for he alone knew the truth about the West, and that truth might determine the Prince’s strategy. True, His Highness was at Derby now, and the Rubicon had been doubtless crossed, but in so great a matter no precaution could be omitted. At that very moment Lochiel, with his letter in his hand, might be looking in vain for the man who had named Derby as the trysting-place.﻿ ﻿… He would sweep southward with the Army to conquest, and then in their hour of triumph would root Sir John from his traitor’s kennel. The man must fight on his challenge, and he had no doubt as to the issue of that fight.

But would he? Would he not disappear overseas, taking with him his wife under some false story? If she were deceived in one matter, she might be deceived in others.﻿ ﻿… No, by Heaven, there was no way of it but the one. The fox must be found before he reached his earth, and brought to account at a sword’s point. Stone dead had no fellow.

The crossroads lay before them where was the turning to Derby.

===Chinese===
不多时，他的心思又荡到一个新的决意上。他该径直去德比投奔王子殿下，这才是他直截了当的军人本分。路他认得；下一个左手岔口，天亮前便能引他到那里。他已迟了几个星期、几个月，再不敢多耽搁一个钟头，因为西边的实情唯有他一人知晓，而那实情或可决定王子的方略。诚然，殿下此刻已在德比，卢比孔河想必也已渡过；但如此大事，任何谨慎都不嫌多余。说不定此时此刻，洛希尔正拿着他的书信，徒然寻访那个指名德比为相会之地的人。……他要随大军横扫南下，直取胜利；然后在得胜的时辰，把约翰爵士从他叛徒的狗窠里连根刨出。那人接到他的战书，非应战不可；而那一战的胜负，他是毫无悬念的。

可他肯应战么？他岂不会编一套假话，携着妻子遁往海外？她既在一件事上受了欺瞒，便也可能在别的事上受欺瞒。……不，上天在上，此事只有一条路可走。必须趁这狐狸还没钻回洞里把他逮住，用剑尖同他清算。唯有死透了的人，才断得了后患。

通往德比的岔路口，已在他们眼前。

===Original===
“There lies the Prince,” said Alastair, his head over his left shoulder. “My duty is to ride forthwith thither. I could breakfast in the camp.”

Johnson, though lacking a riding-coat, had grown warm with the exercise, and both he and his mount were blowing.

“You would not falter in your most honourable resolve?” he puffed.

Alastair clapped spurs to his beast. “No,” he said, “I am resolved before all things to find Sir John Norreys. But when I find him I will kill him.”

He heard a gasp which was more than Mr. Johnson’s chronic shortness of breath. As he cantered forward the slower and heavier beast of his companion was forced alongside of him, and a hand clutched his arm.

===Chinese===
「王子殿下就在那边，」阿拉斯泰尔说，头朝左肩后望去。「我的职责是即刻纵马前去。赶得上在营中用早餐。」

约翰逊虽没有骑马大衣，一场奔驰下来身上倒暖了，只是人同坐骑都喘个不住。

「您那至为光荣的决意，不至动摇吧？」他喘着气说。

阿拉斯泰尔双刺一磕马腹。「不动摇，」他说，「我矢志不移的头一件大事，便是找到约翰·诺里斯爵士。不过找到他时，我要杀了他。」

他听见一声抽气，那分明不止是约翰逊先生惯常的气短。他催马小跑向前，同伴那匹迟缓沉重的坐骑也被逼着赶了上来，一只手攥住了他的手臂。

===Original===
“I beseech you, sir,” said a tragic voice, “I pray you, in God’s name, to turn aside to Derby.”

“I will first meet Sir John,” was the reply and the hand was shaken off.

“But he will be safe at your hands?”

“That is as God may direct,” said Alastair.

===Chinese===
「我恳求您，先生，」一个悲怆的声音说，「看在上帝分上，我求您，转道去德比吧。」

「我要先见约翰爵士，」他如此作答，那只手被甩脱了。

「可他在您手里性命无虞？」

「那要看上帝怎么裁夺，」阿拉斯泰尔说。

===Original===
His resolution being now fixed, his spirits rose. He let thoughts of Claudia flush his mind with their sweet radiance. He pictured her as he had last seen her﻿—the light from the candles making her slim white neck below the rosy nightcap take on the bloom of a peach, and the leaping flames of the hearth chequering the shadow of the bed-curtain. He saw her dim eyes, heard her melting voice, felt the warm vigour of her body as she cowered beside him in the dark of the Flambury galleries. Too young for wife, too old for child, but the ripe age for comrade﻿—and such a comrade, for there was a boy’s gallantry in her eyes and something of a child’s confident fearlessness. He did not hear the groans of Mr. Johnson pounding dismally behind him, or the shuddering cry of owls from the woods. The world was a quiet place to him where a soft voice was speaking, the thick darkness was all aglow with happy pictures. The man’s soul was enraptured by his dreams. He found himself suddenly laughing to think how new and strange was this mood of his. Hitherto he had kept women at arms’ length, and set his heart on policy and war, till he had earned the repute of one to be trusted and courted, but one already at thirty middle-aged. Lord! but there had been a melting of icebergs! And like a stab came the thought of yet another molten iceberg﻿—Sir John﻿—of the sharp nose and the high coat collar! Alastair cried out like a man in pain.

They rode into Milford half an hour after midnight. There was no light in any house, and the inn was a black wall. But the door of the yard was open, and a hostler, ascending to his bed in the hayloft, accepted a shilling for his news. A man had ridden through Milford that night. He had not seen him, but he had heard the clatter as he was bedding the post horses that had come in late from Marlock. How long ago? Not more than an hour, maybe less, and the fellow checked his memory with a string of minute proofs.

Alastair swung his horse’s head back to the road. “Courage, my friend,” he cried. “We are gaining on him. We shall overtake him before morning.”

===Chinese===
决意既定，他的兴致高涨起来。他任由关于克劳迪娅的思绪，以其甜美的光辉涨满心田。他描画着她最后别时的模样——烛光把她玫瑰色睡帽下纤细白皙的脖颈映出桃子般的红晕，炉膛里跳动的火焰把床帷的影子映得明暗成格。他看见她泪眼朦胧，听见她柔肠欲化的声音，触到她偎在他身边、在弗兰伯里画廊的黑暗里瑟缩时那温热的生气。做妻子太年轻，做孩子又太老，做同志却正当妙龄——而且是这样一位同志：她眼里有少年的英侠之气，还有几分孩童那种笃定的无畏。约翰逊先生在他身后悲声颠簸的呻吟，林中鸱鸮战栗的啼叫，他都听不见了。世界于他是一处安静的所在，一个柔和的声音正在那里说话；浓密的黑暗处处燃着幸福的图景。这人的灵魂沉醉在他的梦里。想到自己这份心绪何等新奇陌生，他忽然独自笑出声来。此前他一向同女人保持一臂之遥，一心扑在权谋与战事上，直博得个堪信托、可结纳的令誉——只是年方三十，人已俨然中年。天哪！这真是冰山消融了！随即如遭一剑，他想起还有另一座消融的冰山——约翰爵士——那尖鼻子，那高竖的大衣领！阿拉斯泰尔像伤者似的叫出了声。

午夜过后半小时，他们驰入米尔福德。家家不见灯火，客栈是一堵黑墙。但院门敞着，一个正要上干草棚睡觉的马夫，收下一个先令，卖出了他的消息。当夜有一人骑马穿米尔福德而过。他没有见着那人，只是在给几匹从马洛克晚归的驿马铺草时，听见了嘚嘚的蹄声。多久以前？至多一个钟头，兴许还不到；那汉子还扳着指头，拿出一连串细碎的凭据来核对自己的记性。

阿拉斯泰尔拨转马头回到路上。「振作些，我的朋友，」他喊道，「我们追近他了。天亮前准能赶上。」

===Original===
Again Johnson caught his arm. “Bethink you, sir,” he stammered. “You ride on an errand of murder.”

“Nay,” was the answer, “of love.”

But the next miles were over roads like ploughlands, and the rain blew up from the southwest and set the teeth chattering of the cloakless Mr. Johnson. The night was very dark and the road seemed to pass no villages, for not a light appeared in the wastes of wet ling and fern and plashing woods. The track could be discerned well enough, for it was the only possible route through the rugged land, and happily for the riders there were no crossways. No other traveller met them or was overtaken﻿—which, thought Alastair, was natural, for with the Prince at Derby the flight of the timid would be to the south, and not north or west into the enemy’s country.

===Chinese===
约翰逊又一把抓住他的手臂。「您想想啊，先生，」他结结巴巴地说。「您这是策马去办一桩杀人的差事。」

「不然，」回答是，「是为爱。」

可接下来好几里路，路面竟如翻耕过的田地，西南方又刮来了雨，冻得没穿大衣的约翰逊先生牙齿打战。夜黑得很，一路似乎不经过村庄，因为潮湿的石楠荒草、蕨丛和雨声淅沥的树林之间，不见一点灯火。小道倒还辨认得出——它是这崎岖乡野间唯一可通的路径，而且托天之福，没有岔路。再没有别的旅人与他们相逢，或被他们赶上——阿拉斯泰尔想，这也是自然的：王子既在德比，胆小之辈的逃窜必是奔南，谁会往北、往西逃进敌方的地界里去。

===Original===
Long before dawn he was far beyond the countryside of which he had any knowledge. He had been given Ernshawbank by the Spainneach as the second point to make for, and had assumed that there, if not before, he would fall in with Sir John. Yet when he came to a village about cockcrow, and learned from a sleepy carter that it was Ernshawbank, he did not find his quarry. But at the inn he had news of him. A man answering his description had knocked up the landlord two hours before, drunk a gill of brandy, eaten a crust, and bought for a guinea the said landlord’s cocked grey beaver, new a month ago at Leek Fair. Two hours! The man was gaining on him! It appeared that he had ridden the path for lower Dovedale, as if he were making for Staffordshire and Trentside.

The two breakfasted at an ale house below Thorp Cloud, when a grey December morning was breaking over the leafless vale and the swollen waters of Dove. Their man had been seen, riding hard, with a face blue from cold and wet, and his fine clothes pitifully draggled with the rain. He had crossed the river, and was therefore bound for Staffordshire, and not Nottinghamshire, as Alastair had at first guessed. A minute’s reflection convinced him of the reason. Sir John was specially concerned with cutting off the help coming to the Prince from the West, and therefore went to join those, like the Duke of Kingston, who were on that flank, rather than the army which lay between Derby and London. The reflection gave him acute uneasiness. Nottinghamshire was distant, so there was a chance to overtake the fugitive on the way. But, as it now was, any hour might see the man in sanctuary. The next village might hold a patrol of the Duke’s.﻿ ﻿… He cut short the meal, which Mr. Johnson had scarcely tasted, and the two were again on their weary beasts pounding up the steep lanes towards Ershalton and my lord Shrewsbury’s great house.

The mist cleared, a wintry sun shone, and the sky was mottled with patches of watery blue. Mr. Johnson’s teeth began to chatter so violently that Alastair swung round and regarded him.

===Chinese===
天亮之前许久，他早已驰出自己认得的乡野，进入全然陌生的地界。西班牙人指点他，第二个目标是恩肖班克；他原以为就算此前追不上，到了那里总能撞见约翰爵士。然而鸡鸣时分他进了一座村子，从一个睡眼惺忪的车夫口中得知这就是恩肖班克，却没有寻到他的猎物。不过在客栈里，他得到了那人的消息。两个钟头前，有个与他形貌相符的人敲门叫醒了店主人，喝了一及耳白兰地，啃了一块干面包皮，又花一几尼买下店主那顶灰色海狸皮三角帽——一个月前在利克集市上还是新货。两个钟头！那人反把距离拉大了！看情形，他走的是通往达夫谷下游的小路，像是取道斯塔福德郡、直奔特伦特河而去。

两人在索普克劳德山下的一家麦酒店用了早餐，其时十二月的灰色清晨正破晓在秃枝的山谷与达夫河暴涨的浊流之上。他们追的人有人望见过：骑得很凶，一张脸冻得湿冷发青，讲究的衣裳被雨水浇得狼狈不堪。他已经渡了河，因此是取道斯塔福德郡，而不是阿拉斯泰尔起初猜想的诺丁汉郡。略一思忖，他便明白了缘故。约翰爵士的心事尤其在于斩断西方驰援王子的人马，因此他要去投金斯顿公爵那一类西翼的大员，而不是屯在德比与伦敦之间的大军。这一层想明白，他心里陡然不安起来。诺丁汉郡路途遥远，路上还有望追上亡命者；如今这情势，随便哪个时辰，那人都可能已置身官军翼下。下一座村子，说不定就驻着公爵的一队巡骑。……他草草结束了这顿约翰逊先生几乎没沾唇的饭，两人重新跨上疲惫的坐骑，沿着陡峭的巷道一路轰响，朝厄沙尔顿、朝什鲁斯伯里老爷的宏伟大宅赶去。

雾散了，冬日的太阳照了出来，天上斑斑点点，缀着水色洗淡的蓝。约翰逊先生的牙齿打战打得那样厉害，阿拉斯泰尔不由得回过头来打量他。

===Original===
“You will without doubt catch an ague, sir,” he said, and at the next presentable inn he insisted on his toasting his small-clothes before the kitchen fire, drinking a jorum of hot rum, and borrowing a coat of the landlord’s till his own was dry. For suddenly the panic of hurry was gone out of Alastair, and he saw this business as something predestined and ultimate. Fate was moving the pieces, and her iron fingers did not fumble. If it was written that he and Sir John should meet, then stronger powers than he would set the stage. He was amazed at his own calm.

The rum made his companion drowsy, and as they continued on the road he ceased to groan, and at the next halting-place did not stare at him with plaintive hangdog eyes. As for Alastair he found that his mind had changed again and that all his resolution was fluid.

His hatred of the pursued was ebbing, indeed had almost vanished, for with the sense of fatality which was growing upon him he saw the man as no better than a pawn; a thing as impersonal as sticks and stones. All the actors of the piece﻿—Kyd, Norreys, the Spoonbills, Edom, the sullen Johnson, grew in his picture small and stiff like marionettes, and Claudia alone had the warmth of life. Once more she filled the stage of his memory, but it was not the russet and pearl of her and her witching eyes that held him now, but a tragic muse who appealed from the brink of chasms. She implored his pity on all she loved, on the casket where she had hid her heart.

===Chinese===
「您这准要染上寒热病了，先生，」他说。到了下一家还算像样的客栈，他执意要同伴把贴身衣裤凑在厨房炉火前烘着，灌下一大盅热朗姆酒，再向店主借一件上衣，等自己的衣裳干了再换。因为阿拉斯泰尔心头那股火燎火急的慌乱，忽然间消散了；他把这桩事看作某种前定的、终局的东西。命运正在拨动棋子，她那铁指头从不含糊。倘若他与约翰爵士注定要相逢，自有比他更强的力量来搭这座台。他惊讶于自己的平静。

朗姆酒使他的同伴昏昏欲睡，上路之后，那人不再呻吟了；到下一个歇脚处，也不再用哀愁而负疚的眼睛望着他了。至于阿拉斯泰尔，他发觉自己的心思又变了，先前的一切决意都成了流质。

他对被追者的仇恨正在退潮，其实已近于消散；随着那愈来愈重的不由自主之感，他看那人不过是枚棋子，一件同棍棒石块一样无情之物。这出戏里的所有角色——基德、诺里斯、篦鹭会、以东、阴沉的约翰逊——在他的图景里都缩得又小又僵，像一具具提线木偶，唯有克劳迪娅带着生命的暖意。她又一次占满了他记忆的舞台，但此刻攫住他的，已不是她的栗衣珠色和那双蛊人的眼睛，而是一位悲剧的缪斯，正从深渊边缘发出呼吁。她恳求他怜惜她所爱的一切，怜惜那只她藏起自己心儿的匣子。

===Original===
With a start he recognised that this casket was no other than Sir John Norreys.

He might shatter it and rescue the heart, but how would the precious thing fare in the shattering? Her eyes rose before him with their infinite surrender. Was Johnson right and was she of the race of women that give once in life and then utterly and forever? If so, his errand was not to succour, but to slay. His sword would not cut the bonds of youth and innocence, it would pierce their heart.

He forced his mind to reconstruct the three occasions when she had faced him﻿—not for his delectation, but to satisfy a newborn anxiety. He saw her at Flambury, a girl afire with zeal and daring, sexless as a child, and yet always in her sweet stumbling phrases harping on her dear Sir John. He saw her in the Brown Room at the Sleeping Deer, a tender muse of memories, but imperious towards dishonour, one whose slim grace might be brittle but would not bend. Last he saw her set up in the great bed at Brightwell, one arm round the neck of Duchess Kitty, the other stretched towards him in that woman’s appeal which had held him from Derby and the path of duty.

===Chinese===
他悚然一惊，认出这只匣子不是别的，正是约翰·诺里斯爵士。

他可以把匣子砸碎，救出那颗心；可在这粉碎之中，那件珍宝又如何自处呢？她那双眼睛带着无限的委曲顺从，浮现在他眼前。约翰逊是对的吗？她当真属于那种女人——一生只付出一次，一旦付出便倾其所有、至死不渝？若果真如此，他此行便不是救苦，而是杀伐。他的剑斩不断青春与天真的锁链，只会刺穿它们的心。

他强使自己的心思，一寸寸重造她与他相对的三次光景——不为赏心悦目，而是要安抚一重新生的忧惧。他看见她在弗兰伯里，一个为热忱与胆气所燃的姑娘，像孩童一样不分男女，却总在她那甜美的、跌跌撞撞的话语里，念着她亲爱的约翰爵士。他看见她在睡鹿客栈的棕室里，一位温存的追忆的缪斯，却对羞辱威严凛然——她那纤秀的风致也许易折，却决不肯弯。最后他看见她在布莱特韦尔的大床上支起身来，一条胳膊搂着基蒂公爵夫人的脖颈，另一条朝他伸来，带着那种属于女人的恳求——正是这恳求，把他从德比、从职责的道路上挽住了。

===Original===
There is that in hard riding and hard weather which refines a man’s spirit, purging it of its grosser humours. The passion of the small hours had gone utterly from Alastair, and instead his soul was filled with a tempestuous affection, not of a lover but of a kinsman and protector. The child must at all costs be sheltered from sorrow, and if she pined for her toy it must be found for her, its cracks mended and its paint refurbished. His mood was now the same as Johnson’s, his resolution the same. He felt an odd pleasure in this access of tenderness, but he was conscious, too, that the pleasure was like a thin drift of flowers over dark mires of longing and sorrow. For his world had been tumbled down, and all the castles he had built. He had always been homeless, but now he was a thousandfold more an outlaw, for the one thing on earth he desired was behind him and not before him, and he was fleeing from hope.

In the afternoon the rain descended again and the road passed over a wide heath, which had been blackened by some autumn fire so that the shores of its leaden pools were like charcoal, and skeleton coverts shook their charred branches in the wind. The scene was a desolation, but he viewed it with calm eyes, for a strange peace was creeping into his soul. He turned in the saddle, and saw six yards behind him Johnson jogging wearily along, his heavy shoulders bowed and his eyes fixed dully on his horse’s neck. The man must be near the limits of his strength, he thought.﻿ ﻿… Once again he had one of his sudden premonitions. Sir John Norreys was close at hand, for he had not yet stopped for a meal and he had now been on the road for twelve hours. The conviction grew upon him, and made him urge his tired beast to a better pace. Somewhere just in front was the meeting-place where the ordeal was appointed which should decree the fate of two souls.﻿ ﻿…

The drizzle changed into half a gale, and scouring blasts shut out the landscape. There came a moment’s clearing, and lo! before him lay a bare space in the heath, where another road entered from the west to join the highway. At their meeting, set in a grove of hornbeams, stood an inn.

===Chinese===
剧烈的骑行与严酷的天气里自有一种东西，能提炼人的性灵，涤除其中较为粗浊的体液。凌晨时分那股激情已从阿拉斯泰尔身上褪得干干净净，代之充满他灵魂的，是一种狂放的深情——不是情人的深情，是亲族与护卫者的深情。这孩子必须不惜一切代价护她免于忧伤；她若恋着她的玩偶，就得替她寻回来，把裂缝粘好，把漆色补新。他此刻的心境与约翰逊相同，决意也相同。他对这阵涌上来的柔情感到一种奇异的快慰，但同时也自觉，这快慰好比薄薄一层飘落的花，覆在渴慕与哀伤的黑泥沼上。因为他的世界已经坍塌，他亲手筑起的一切楼堡都已坍塌。他一向无家可归，如今更是千倍地成了亡命之人——世上他唯一渴望的东西在他身后，不在他身前，他是在逃离希望。

午后雨又落下来，道路穿过一片辽阔的荒原。荒原曾被秋火燎过，焦黑一片，铅灰色水塘的塘岸宛如炭条，烧成骨架的灌木林在风里摇着焦黑的枝条。满目荒凉，他却以平静的眼光看它，因为一种奇异的安宁正潜入他的灵魂。他在鞍上回头，看见六码之后约翰逊疲沓地颠着步子，沉重的双肩佝偻，两眼呆呆盯住马颈。这人怕是近于力竭了，他想。……他又一次生出那种突如其来的预感。约翰·诺里斯爵士近在咫尺，因为他还没停下用过饭，而已在路上跑了十二个钟头。这确信在他心里愈长愈牢，催着他逼那匹疲惫的牲口再快些。就在前方某处，便是那约好的会面之地——那场裁决已在那里约定，将裁断两个灵魂的命运。……

细雨转成了半场狂风，横扫的疾飙把景物遮了个严实。忽有一霎风开——看哪！他面前现出荒原上一块空地，一条岔路自西而来，在那里汇入大道。两路相接之处，一片鹅耳枥树丛中，立着一座客栈。

===Original===
It was a small place, ancient, long and low, and the signboard could not be read in the dim weather. But beneath it, new-painted, was an open eye. He checked his horse, and turned to the door, for he knew with utter certainty that he had reached his destination.

He dropped from the saddle, and since there was no stable-lad in sight, he tied the reins to a ring in the wall. Then he pushed open the door and descended a step into the inn kitchen. A man was busy about the hearth, a grizzled elderly fellow in leathern small-clothes. In front of the fire a fine coat hung drying on two chairs, and a pair of sodden boots steamed beside the log basket.

The innkeeper looked up, and something in the quiet eyes and weatherworn face awoke in Alastair a recollection. He had not seen the face before, but he had seen its like.

===Chinese===
那是小小的一处房子，古旧，长而低矮，天色昏冥中看不清招牌上的字。但招牌底下，新漆着一只睁开的眼睛。他勒住马，朝店门转去——他知道得千真万确，自己到了目的地。

他翻身下鞍，四下不见马厩小厮，便把缰绳拴在墙上的铁环里。随后他推开店门，走下一级台阶，进了客栈的厨房。一个男人正在炉边忙碌，是个花白头发的老者，穿一条皮质的贴身裤。火前两把椅子上摊着烘一件讲究的上衣，柴篮旁边一双湿透的靴子正冒着热气。

店主抬起头来，那双沉静的眼睛和那张饱经风霜的脸上，有什么东西在阿拉斯泰尔心里唤醒了一段记忆。这张脸他从未见过，但他见过同类的脸。

===Original===
“You have a guest?” he said.

The man did not answer, and Alastair knew that no word or deed of his would compel an answer, if the man were unwilling.

“You have the sign,” he said. “I, too, am of the Spoonbills. I seek Master Midwinter.”

The innkeeper straightened himself. “He shall be found,” he said. “What message do I carry?”

“Say that he to whom he promised help on Otmoor now claims it. And stay, there are two weary cattle outside. Have them fed and stabled.”

===Chinese===
「店里有客么？」他问。

那人不应声。阿拉斯泰尔知道，这人若不愿开口，自己说什么做什么也逼不出回答。

「您挂着记号，」他说。「我也是篦鹭会的人。我寻仲冬师傅。」

店主直起身来。「定能寻着他，」他说。「要捎什么话？」

「就说，他在奥特沼泽许诺相助的那个人，如今来讨这个情了。慢着——外头有两匹乏透了的牲口，给它们上料入厩。」

===Original===
The man turned to go, but Alastair checked him.

“You have a guest?” he asked.

“He is now upstairs at food,” was the answer given readily. “He feeds in his shirt, for he is all mucked and moiled with the roads.”

“I have business with him, I and my friend. Let us be alone till Master Midwinter comes.”

The man stood aside to let Johnson stumble in. Then the door was shut, and to Alastair’s ear there was the turning of a key.

===Chinese===
那人转身要走，阿拉斯泰尔却止住了他。

「店里有客么？」他又问。

「他这会儿在楼上用饭，」这回回答得爽快。「他只穿着衬衫吃饭，一路上泥里水里，浑身上下糊得不成样子。」

「我和我的朋友有话同他讲。在仲冬师傅来之前，让我们单独待着。」

那人侧身让约翰逊跌跌撞撞地进来。随后门关上了，阿拉斯泰尔耳中听见钥匙拧动的一响。

===Original===
Johnson’s great figure seemed broken with weariness. He staggered across the uneven stone floor, and rolled into a grandfather’s chair which stood to the left of the fire. Then he caught sight of the coat drying in the glow and recognised it. Into his face, grey with fatigue, came a sudden panic. “It is his,” he cried. “He is here.” He lifted his head and seemed to listen like a stag at pause. Then he flung himself from the chair, and rushed on Alastair, who was staring abstractedly at the blaze. “You will not harm him,” he cried. “You will not break my lady’s heart. Sooner, sir, I will choke you with my own hands.”

His voice was the scream of an animal in pain, his skin was livid, his eyes were hot coals. Alastair, taken by surprise, was all but swung off his feet by the fury of the assault. One great arm was round his waist, one hand was clutching his throat. The two staggered back, upsetting the chair before the fire; the hand at the throat was shaken off, and in a second they were at wrestling-grips in the centre of the floor.

Both men were weary, and one was lately recovered of a sickness. This latter, too, was the lighter, and for a moment Alastair found himself helpless in a grip which crushed in his sides and stopped his breath. But Johnson’s passion was like the spouting of a volcano and soon died down. The fiery vigour went out of his clutch, but it remained a compelling thing, holding the young man a close prisoner.

The noise of the scuffle had alarmed the gentleman above. The stairs ran up in a steep flight direct from the kitchen, and as Alastair looked from below his antagonist’s elbow, he saw a white face peer beneath the low roof of the stairway, and a little further down three-quarters of the length of a sword blade. He was exerting the power of his younger arms against the dead strength of Johnson, but all the while his eyes were held by this new apparition. It was something clad only in shirt and breeches and rough borrowed stockings, but the face was unmistakable and the haggard eyes.

===Chinese===
约翰逊魁伟的身躯像被疲惫压垮了。他踉跄着穿过坎坷的石板地，一屁股陷进炉火左边一把老式高背椅里。随即他瞥见火光中烘着的那件上衣，认了出来。他那张困乏灰败的脸上，陡然掠过一阵惊惶。「是他的，」他叫道。「他在这儿。」他抬起头，像一头驻足谛听的牡鹿。随后他从椅子里猛地跃起，直扑阿拉斯泰尔——后者正恍恍惚惚盯着炉火。「您不能伤害他，」他喊道。「您不能踩碎我夫人的心。真到那一步，先生，我宁可亲手掐死您。」

他的声音是受伤野兽的哀嚎，皮肤青灰，双眼是两块烧红的炭。阿拉斯泰尔猝不及防，被这一扑之猛几乎掀翻在地。一条粗壮的胳膊箍住他的腰，一只手掐住他的咽喉。两人踉跄后退，撞翻了火前的椅子；掐喉的手被甩脱，转眼间两人已扭作一团，在厨房正中角力相持。

两人都已困乏，其中一个还是大病新愈。这位又是较轻的一个，有一瞬间，阿拉斯泰尔在那钳制里全然无措——那钳制挤压着他的两肋，闭住了他的呼吸。但约翰逊的激情像火山喷发，不久便息了。那双手上烈火般的狠劲熄了，却仍旧是一副不容挣脱的钳锁，把这年轻人牢牢囚作近身的囚徒。

厮打声惊动了楼上的那位绅士。一道楼梯从厨房里直直陡陡通上去；阿拉斯泰尔从对手的臂肘下仰望，看见一张白脸从楼梯低矮的顶棚下探出来，再往下几级，又探出四分之三截剑身。他一面用年轻的双臂对抗着约翰逊死沉沉的蛮力，一面目光却全被这新现的幽灵勾住。那是个只穿着衬衫、马裤和借来的粗劣长袜的身影，可那张脸错不了，那双憔悴的眼睛也错不了。

===Original===
The apparition descended another step, and now Alastair saw the hand which grasped the sword. Fear was in the man’s face, and then a deeper terror, for he had recognised one of the combatants. There was perplexity there, too, for he was puzzled at the sight, and after that a spasm of hope. He hesitated for a second till he grasped the situation. Then he shouted something which may have been an encouragement to Johnson, and leaped the remaining steps on to the kitchen floor.

Johnson had not seen him, for his head was turned the other way and his sight and hearing were dimmed by his fury. The man in underclothes danced round the wrestlers, babbling strangely. “Hold him!” he cried. “Hold him, and I’ll finish him!” His blade was shortened for a thrust, but the movement of the wrestlers frustrated him. He made a pass, but it only grazed the collar of Alastair’s coat.

Then he found a better chance, and again his arm was shortened. A hot quiver went through Alastair’s shoulder, for a rapier had pinked the flesh and had cut into the flapping pouch of Johnson’s coat.

===Chinese===
那身影又下了一级，这下阿拉斯泰尔看清了握剑的那只手。那人脸上有惧色，继而是一重更深的恐怖——他认出了角力二人中的一个。脸上又有惶惑，因为这景象叫他摸不着头脑；随后又掠过一阵希望的痉挛。他迟疑了一秒，直到弄清了局势。随后他喊了一句什么——大概是给约翰逊助威——便一跃跳过剩下的梯级，落在厨房的地上。

约翰逊没有看见他，因为他的头扭向另一边，视与听都被怒火烧得昏昧了。那个只穿内衣的人围着两个角力者打转，语无伦次地乱嚷。「按住他！」他喊。「按住他，我来结果他！」他的剑已收短作突刺之势，但两人翻滚的身子搅得他无从下手。他递出一剑，只擦过阿拉斯泰尔的上衣领子。

随后他寻到一个更好的机会，手臂又收短蓄势。一阵火辣的颤痛穿过阿拉斯泰尔的肩头——细剑点破了皮肉，又顺势划进约翰逊外衣那只翻飞的口袋。

===Original===
It may have been Alastair’s cry, or the fierce shout of the man in underclothes, but Johnson awoke suddenly to what was happening. He saw a white face with fiery eyes, he saw the rapier drawn back for a new thrust with blood on its point.﻿ ﻿… With a shudder he loosened his grip and let Alastair go free.

“I have done murder,” he cried, and staggered across the floor till he fell against the dresser. His hands were at his eyes and he was shaken with a passion of sobbing.

The two remaining faced each other, one in his stocking-soles, dancing like a crazy thing in the glow of the wood-fire, triumph in his small eyes. Alastair, dazed and shaken, was striving to draw his blade, which, owing to the struggle, had become entangled in the skirts of his riding-coat. The other, awaking to the new position of affairs, pressed on him wildly till he gave ground.﻿ ﻿… And then he halted, for a blade had crossed his.

Both men had light travelling-swords, which in a well-matched duello should have met with the tinkle of thin ice in a glass. But now there was the jar and whine of metal harshly used, for the one lunged recklessly, and the other stood on a grim defensive, parrying with a straight arm a point as disorderly as wildfire. Sir John Norreys had the skill in fence of an ordinary English squire, learned from an Oxford *maître d’escrime* and polished by a lesson or two in Covent Garden﻿—an art no better than ignorance when faced with one perfected by Gérard and d’Aubigny, and tested in twenty affairs against the best blades of France.

===Chinese===
也许是阿拉斯泰尔的那声痛呼，也许是那内衣人凶狠的呼喝——约翰逊霎时醒悟了眼前的事。他看见一张白脸，眼里喷着火；看见细剑已撤回蓄势，剑尖上带着血。……他一阵战栗，松开双手，放阿拉斯泰尔脱身。

「我成了杀人的凶手了，」他叫道，踉跄着穿过地板，直到跌在碗橱上。他双手掩住眼睛，抽抽噎噎，浑身发颤。

剩下的两人面对面站定：一个只穿着袜底，在柴火的光焰里疯子也似地蹦跳，小小的眼睛里盛满得意。阿拉斯泰尔又昏又晃，正奋力拔剑——方才一番缠斗，剑刃绞进了骑马大衣的下摆。对手看清了局面之新变，发疯般向他进逼，逼得他步步后退。……可那人忽然停住了，因为一柄剑已横挡在他的剑上。

两人用的都是轻便的旅行佩剑；若是势均力敌的决斗，剑与剑相触，本该如杯中薄冰相碰，叮然一声脆响。可此刻只有金铁受虐的震响与尖啸，因为一方不顾死活地直刺，另一方则立于冷峻的守势，直着一条手臂，招架那野火般散乱无章的剑尖。约翰·诺里斯爵士的剑术，是一个寻常英格兰乡绅的剑术——从牛津的一位击剑教师（maître d'escrime）学来，又在考文特花园经一两课点拨——这门功夫，遇上经热拉尔与多比尼锤炼至善、又在二十场决斗里对过法兰西最佳剑手的剑术，比无知强不了多少。

===Original===
Alastair’s wound was a mere scratch, and at this clearing of issues his wits had recovered and his strength returned. As he fought, his eyes did not leave the other’s face. He saw its chalky pallor, where the freckles showed like the scars of smallpox, the sharp arrogant nose, the weak mouth with the mean lines around it, the quick, hard eyes now beginning to waver from their first fury. The man meant to kill him, and as he realised this, the atmosphere of the duello fled, and it was again the old combat à outrance of his clan﻿—his left hand reached instinctively for the auxiliary dagger which should have hung at his belt. And then he laughed, for whatever his enemy’s purposes, success was not likely to follow them.

The scene had to Alastair the spectral unreality of a dream. The kitchen was hushed save for the fall of ashes on the hearth, the strained sobbing of Johnson, and the rasp of the blades. The face of Sir John Norreys was a mirror in which he read his own predominance. The eyes lost their heat, the pupils contracted till they were two shining beads in the dead white of the skin, the wild lunging grew wilder, the breath came in short gasps. But the face was a mirror, too, in which he read something of the future. If his resolution to spare the man had not been already taken, it must now have become irrevocable. This was a child, a stripling, who confronted him, a mere amateur of vice, a thing which to slay would have been no better than common murder. Pity for the man, even a strange kindness stole into Alastair’s soul. He wondered how he could ever have hated anything so crude and weak.

He smiled again, and at that smile all the terrors of death crowded into the other’s face. He seemed to nerve himself for a last effort, steadied the fury of his lunges and aimed a more skilful thrust in tierce. Alastair had a mind to end the farce. His parry beat up the other’s blade, and by an easy device of the schools he twitched the sword from his hand so that it clattered at Johnson’s feet.

===Chinese===
阿拉斯泰尔的伤不过是擦破一点皮；战局一清，他的神智便回来了，气力也回来了。他一面交剑，一面目光须臾不离对方那张脸。他看见那白垩般的苍白，苍白里雀斑点点，如出水痘的疤；那尖细傲慢的鼻子；那软弱的嘴，四围刻着猥琐的细纹；那对又急又硬的眼睛，初时的凶焰已开始摇曳。这人是要他的命；他一意识到这个，决斗的空气便遁走了，这又成了他氏族那种古老的殊死之搏（combat à outrance）——他的左手本能地伸向本应悬在腰间的副手短剑。随即他笑了：不论这仇人打的什么主意，那主意都断难得逞。

这幕景象在阿拉斯泰尔眼里，有一种梦魇般鬼影幢幢的不真实。厨房里悄然无声，只有炉膛里炉灰的坠落、约翰逊压抑的啜泣，和双剑的磨响。约翰·诺里斯爵士的脸是一面镜子，他从中照见自己的优势。那双眼失去了热度，瞳孔缩成两粒亮珠，嵌在死白的皮肤上；狂乱的突刺愈发狂乱，呼吸短促地一口口抽。但这张脸也是一面镜子，他从中照见几分未来。倘若饶这人性命的决意此前尚未拿定，此刻也必是无可挽回地定了。与他相对的是个孩子，一个毛头少年，恶行里的半吊子——杀这样一个东西，与寻常的凶杀没有两样。对这人的怜悯，甚至一种奇异的善意，悄悄潜入阿拉斯泰尔的灵魂。他诧异自己竟恨过这样一件粗劣而软弱的东西。

他又笑了笑；这一笑，死亡的百般恐怖便一齐涌上对方的脸。那人似乎强打精神作最后一搏，稳住狂乱的突刺，照第三姿势（tierce）递出一记更为老练的突刺。阿拉斯泰尔有心收场这场闹剧。他一格荡开对方剑身，顺势用剑馆里一记寻常手法，把剑从那人手里拧脱，当啷一声落在约翰逊脚边。

===Original===
Sir John Norreys stood stock-still for an instant, his mouth working like a child about to weep. Then some share of manhood returned to him. He drew himself straight, swallowed what may have been a sob, and let his arms drop by his side.

“I am at your mercy,” he stammered. “What do you purpose with me?”

Alastair returned his sword to its sheath. “I purpose to save your life,” he said, “and if God be merciful, your soul.”

He stripped off his riding-coat. “Take this,” he said. “It is wintry weather, and may serve till your own garments are dry. It is ill talking unclad, Sir John, and we have much to say to each other.”

===Chinese===
约翰·诺里斯爵士僵立了一瞬，嘴抽动着，像要哭的孩子。随后几分男子的气概回到了他身上。他挺直身子，咽下一口也许是呜咽的东西，双臂垂在身侧。

「我听凭发落，」他讷讷地说。「您打算怎么处置我？」

阿拉斯泰尔还剑入鞘。「我打算救你的性命，」他说，「若上帝慈悲，也救你的灵魂。」

他脱下自己的骑马大衣。「披上这个，」他说。「天寒，凑合到你自己的衣裳干了再说。赤身说话总是不便，约翰爵士，我们要谈的话还多着呢。」

===Original===
Johnson had risen, and his face was heavy with an emotion which might have been sorrow or joy. He stood with arm upraised like a priest blessing his flock. “Now to Angels and Archangels and all the Company of Heaven,” he cried, and then he stopped, for the door opened softly and closed again.

It was Midwinter that entered. His shoulders filled the doorway, and his eyes constrained all three to a tense silence. He walked to the fireplace, picking up Norreys’s sword, which he bent into a half hoop against the jamb of the chimney. As his quiet gaze fell on the company it seemed to exercise a peaceful mastery which made the weapon in his hand a mere trinket.

“You have summoned me, Captain Maclean,” he said. “I am here to make good my promise. Show me how I can serve you.”

“We are constituted a court of honour,” said Alastair. “We seek your counsel.”

===Chinese===
约翰逊已经站起身，脸上压着一种沉重的情绪，也许是悲，也许是喜。他举着一条手臂站着，像祝福羊群的神父。「愿偕天使与总领天使，并天上全会众——」他高声念道，随即停住了，因为门轻轻开了，又轻轻关上。

进来的是仲冬。他的双肩堵满了门洞，他的目光迫得三人一齐紧绷绷地噤了声。他走到壁炉前，拾起诺里斯的剑，抵着烟囱的壁柱，把它拗成一个半环。他沉静的目光落到众人身上时，仿佛施行着一种平和的统御，教他手中那件兵器只成了一件小玩意儿。

「您召唤了我，麦克莱恩上尉，」他说。「我来践我的诺言。请吩咐我如何效劳。」

「我们组成了一个荣誉法庭，」阿拉斯泰尔说。「我们求您指教。」

===Original===
He turned to Norreys.

“You are not two months married, Sir John. How many years have you to your age?”

The man answered like an automaton. “I am in my twenty-third,” he said. He was looking alternately to his antagonist and to Midwinter, still with the bewilderment of a dull child.

“Since when have you meddled in politics?”

“Since scarce two years.”

===Chinese===
他转向诺里斯。

「你成婚还不满两个月，约翰爵士。你今年多少岁了？」

那人像自动木偶一样作答。「我正当第二十三个年头，」他说。他的目光在对手与仲冬之间来回游移，依旧带着迟钝孩童的那种惶惑。

「你从几时起插手政事的？」

「还不到两年。」

===Original===
“You were drawn to the Prince’s side﻿—by what? Was it family tradition?”

“No, damme, my father was a Hanover man when he lived. I turned Jacobite to please Claudie. There was no welcome at Chastlecote unless a man wore the white rose.”

“And how came you into your recent business?”

“ ’Twas Kyd’s doing.﻿ ﻿… No, curse it, I won’t shelter behind another, for I did it of my own free will. But ’twas Kyd showed me a way of improving my fortunes, for he knew I cared not a straw who had the governing of the land.”

===Chinese===
「你被牵到王子殿下一边——凭什么？是家门的旧传统么？」

「不是，见鬼，我父亲在世时是汉诺威王朝的人。我当雅各布党人，是为了讨克劳迪欢心。在查斯特科特，男人若不佩白玫瑰，便不受欢迎。」

「那你又是怎么落到眼下的营生里的？」

「是基德引的路。……不，去他的，我不躲到别人背后去，这事是我自己情愿干的。不过是基德给我指了一条进财的路子，因为他知道，谁来治理这个国家，我是半个子儿也不在乎的。」

===Original===
“And you were happy in the service?”

The baronet’s face had lost its childishness, and had grown sullen.

“I was content.” Then he broke out. “Rot him, I was not content﻿—not of late. I thought the Prince and his adventure was but a Scotch craziness. But now, with him in the heart of England I have been devilish anxious.”

“For your own safety? Or was there perhaps another reason?”

===Chinese===
「那你在这种差事里当得快活么？」

从男爵脸上那份孩子气褪尽了，换上一副阴沉。

「我没什么不满。」随即他发作了。「叫他去烂吧，我才不满——近来早就不满了。我原以为王子殿下和他那番事业，不过是苏格兰人的一阵疯劲。可如今他打进了英格兰的腹心，我焦心得要命。」

「是为你自家的身家性命？还是另有缘故？」

===Original===
Sir John’s pale face flushed. “Let that be. Put it that I feared for my neck and my estate.”

Alastair turned smiling to the others. “I begin to detect the rudiments of honesty﻿ ﻿… I am going to unriddle your thoughts, Sir John. You were beginning to wonder how your wife would regard your courses. Had the Prince shipwrecked beyond the Border, she would never have known of them, and the Rising would have been between you only a sad pleasing memory. But now she must learn the truth, and you are afraid. Why? She is a lady of fortune, but you did not marry her for her fortune.”

“My God, no,” he cried. “I loved her most damnably, and I ever shall.”

===Chinese===
约翰爵士苍白的脸涨红了。「那个就不必提了。就算我怕掉脑袋、怕丢家业吧。」

阿拉斯泰尔含笑转向众人。「我渐渐嗅出几分诚实的苗头了……我来替你解破心事吧，约翰爵士。你开始犯嘀咕：你夫人会怎样看待你的行径。倘若王子殿下在边境以北翻了船，她永远无从知晓这一切，这场举事于你们夫妻之间，便只是一段凄然可念的回忆。可如今她必然要得知真相，于是你怕了。为什么？她是位有家财的小姐，可你娶她，并非贪图她的家财。」

「上帝作证，不是，」他叫道。「我爱她爱得要命，如今依然，永远依然。」

===Original===
“And she loves you?”

The flush grew deeper. “She is but a child. She has scarcely seen another man. I think she loves me.”

“So you have betrayed the Prince’s cause, because it did not touch you deep and you favoured it only because of a lady’s eyes. But the Prince looks like succeeding, Sir John. He is now south of Derby on the road to London, and his enemies do not abide him. What do you purpose in that event? Have you the purchase at his Court to get your misdoings overlooked?”

“I trusted to Kyd.”

===Chinese===
「她也爱你么？」

红晕涨得更深。「她不过是个孩子。她几乎没见过别的男人。我想她是爱我的。」

「如此说来，你背叛了王子的大业，因为它不曾深触你的心，你偏向它，只不过为了一双姑娘的眼睛。可是王子殿下眼看要成了，约翰爵士。他此刻已在德比以南，正取道直趋伦敦，他的仇敌望风不敢撄其锋。事若至此，你打算怎么办？你在他的宫廷里，可有那么大的情面，叫你的种种劣迹一笔勾销？」

「我原指望基德。」

===Original===
“Vain trust. Last night, after you left us so hastily, Kyd was stripped to the bare bone.”

“Was he sent to the Prince?” the man asked sharply.

“No. We preferred to administer our own justice, as we will do with you. But he is gone into a long exile.”

“Is he dead?﻿ ﻿… You promised me my life.”

===Chinese===
「指望落了空。昨夜你匆匆走后，基德已被剥得只剩一把骨头。」

「他被打发到王子殿下那里去了么？」那人厉声问。

「没有。我们宁可亲自行使我们自己的正义，对你也将一样。不过他已去作漫长的流放了。」

「他死了么？……你们应过饶我一命。」

===Original===
“He lives, as you shall live. Sir John, I will be frank with you. You are a youth whom vanity and greed have brought deep into the mire. I would get you out of it﻿—not for your own sake, but for that of a lady whom you love, I think, and who most assuredly loves you. Your besetting sin is avarice. Well, let it be exercised upon your estates and not upon the fortunes of better men. I have a notion that you may grow with good luck into a very decent sort of man﻿—not much of a fellow at heart, perhaps, but reputable and reputed﻿—at any rate enough to satisfy the love-blinded eyes of your lady. Do you assent?”

The baronet reddened again at the contemptuous kindliness of Alastair’s words.

“I have no choice,” he said gruffly.

“Then it is the sentence of this court that you retire to your estates and live there without moving outside your park pale.”

===Chinese===
「他活着，你也将活着。约翰爵士，我对你开诚布公。你是个被虚荣与贪心拖进泥沼深处的年轻人。我要把你拽出来——不是为你自己，是为一位你爱着、而她千真万确爱着你的夫人。你缠身之罪是贪财。好，那就把这份贪心使在自己的庄产上，别去打比你良善之人家财的主意。依我看，运气若好，你也许能长成一个相当像样的人——论心地或许算不得什么人物，但有脸面，有声名——无论如何，尽够满足你夫人那双被爱情迷瞎的眼睛了。你可应承？」

听着阿拉斯泰尔这番带着轻蔑的好意，从男爵又红了脸。

「我别无选择，」他生硬地说。

「那么本庭宣判：你退回你的田庄去，住在那里，园墙一步不出。」

===Original===
“Alone?”

“Alone. Your wife has gone into Wiltshire with her Grace of Queensberry. You will stay at Weston till she returns to you, and that date depends upon the posture of affairs in the country. You will give me your oath to meddle no more in politics. And for the safety of your person and the due observance of your promise you will be given an escort on your journey south.”

“Will you send Highlanders into Oxfordshire?” was the astonished question.

Midwinter answered. “Nay, young sir, you will have the bodyguard of Old England.”

===Chinese===
「独自一人？」

「独自一人。你夫人已随昆斯伯里公爵夫人去了威尔特郡。你就待在韦斯顿，等她回到你身边；至于那一天，要看国中局势而定。你要向我立誓，从此不再插手政事。为保你的人身、也为你切实守约，你南下途中将有一队护卫同行。」

「你们要派高地人进牛津郡么？」那声音里满是惊愕。

仲冬答话。「不然，年轻人，护送你的，是『老英格兰』的卫队。」

===Original===
Sir John stared at Midwinter and saw something in that face which made him avert his gaze. He suddenly shivered, and a different look came into his eyes. “You have been merciful to me, sirs,” he said, “merciful beyond my deserts. I owe you more than I can repay.”

“You owe it to your wife, sir,” Alastair broke in. “Cherish her dearly and let that be your atonement.﻿ ﻿… If you will take my advice, you will snatch a little sleep, for you have been moss-trooping for a round of the clock.”

As the baronet’s bare shanks disappeared up the stairway Alastair turned wearily to the others. A haze seemed to cloud his eyes, and the crackle of logs on the hearth sounded in his ears like the noise of the sea.

“You were right,” he told Johnson. “There’s the makings of a sober husband in that man. No hero, but she may be trusted to gild her idol. I think she will be happy.”

===Chinese===
约翰爵士凝视仲冬，看见那张脸上有什么东西，教他把目光挪开了。他突然打了个寒噤，眼里换了一种神色。「诸位对我实在仁慈，」他说，「仁慈得远过我的罪愆。我欠下诸位还不清的恩情。」

「这份情是欠你夫人的，先生，」阿拉斯泰尔插言。「好生珍爱她，就算你的赎罪。……你若肯听我的劝，就抓紧睡一会儿——学沼地马匪的样子奔命，你已经奔了整整一昼夜了。」

从男爵光裸的小腿消失在楼梯上头，阿拉斯泰尔疲惫地转向众人。一层雾仿佛蒙上他的眼睛，炉膛里柴火的爆响在他耳中，声声如海涛。

「你说得对，」他对约翰逊说。「那人身上有成个本分丈夫的坯子。算不得英雄，不过给她那位偶像镀金的事，尽管放心交给她。我想她会幸福的。」

===Original===
“You have behaved as a good Christian should.” Mr. Johnson was still shaking as if from the ague. “Had I been in your case, I do not think I would have shown so just a mind.”

“Call it philosophy, which makes a man know what it is not in his power to gain,” Alastair laughed. “I think I have learned the trick of it from you.”

He swayed and caught Midwinter’s shoulder. “Forgive me, old friend. I have been riding for forty hours, and have fought and argued in between, and before that I rose off a sickbed.﻿ ﻿… But I must on to Derby. Get a fresh horse, my brave one.”

Midwinter drew him to an armchair, and seemed to fumble with his hands for a second or two at his brow. When Johnson looked again Alastair was asleep, while the other dressed roughly the hole in his shoulder made by Sir John’s sword.

“*Festina lente*, Mr. Johnson. I can provide fresh beasts, but not fresh legs for the riders. The pair of you will sleep for five hours and then sup, for Derby is a far cry and an ill road, and if you start as you are you will founder in the first slough.”

===Chinese===
「你的行事，合乎一个好基督徒的本分。」约翰逊先生还在发抖，像寒热病未退。「换了我处你的地位，我想我未必显得出这样一副公正的心肠。」

「就称它作哲学吧——哲学教人明白，何者非人力所能求，」阿拉斯泰尔笑道。「这门诀窍，我想正是跟你学的。」

他晃了晃，一把扶住仲冬的肩。「恕我失态，老朋友。我已经骑了四十个钟头，中间还打了一场、论了一场，在那之前，又是从病床上爬起来的。……可我必须赶到德比去。给我备一匹新马，我的好汉。」

仲冬把他拉到一张扶手椅上，双手似乎在他额前虚虚拂了一两下。约翰逊再看时，阿拉斯泰尔已经睡着，而仲冬正粗粗地包裹他肩上那个被约翰爵士的剑戳出的窟窿。

「急事缓办（Festina lente），约翰逊先生。新牲口我备得出，骑马人的新腿可备不出。你们两位先睡五个钟头，然后吃晚饭——德比远着呢，路也坏，照现在这副样子动身，头一道泥沼里就得趴下。」

## Postscript / 跋

===Original===
Thus far Mr. Derwent’s papers.

With the farewell on the Cumberland moor Alastair Maclean is lost to us in the mist. Of the nature of Ramoth-Gilead let history tell; it is too sad a tale for the romancer. But one is relieved to know that he did not fall at Culloden, or swing like so many on Haribee outside the walls of Carlisle. For the Editor has been so fortunate as to discover a further document, after a second search among Mr. Derwent’s archives, a document in the handwriting of Mr. Samuel Johnson himself; and there seems to be the strongest presumption that it was addressed to Alastair at some town in France, for there is a mention of hospitality shown one Alan Maclean who had crossed the Channel with a message and was on the eve of returning. There is no superscription, the letter begins “My dear Sir,” and the end is lost; but since it is headed “Gough Square,” and contains a reference to the writer’s beginning work on his great dictionary, the date may be conjectured to be 1748. Unfortunately the paper is much torn and discoloured, and only one passage can be given with any certainty of correctness. I transcribe it as a memorial of a friendship which was to colour the thoughts of a great man to his dying day and which, we may be assured, left an impress no less indelible upon the mind of the young Highlander.

===Chinese===
德温特先生的文稿，到此为止。

随着坎伯兰荒沼上那一场话别，阿拉斯泰尔·麦克莱恩便隐入雾中，从此杳无踪迹。至于基列的拉末究竟有怎样的结局，且让史书去叙述吧；那段故事太过悲惨，不宜出诸传奇小说家之笔。但令人宽慰的是，我们知道他既没有阵亡于卡洛登，也没有像许多人那样，在卡莱尔城墙外的哈里比绞架上晃荡。因为编者有幸在第二次搜检德温特先生的档案之后，又发现了一件文书——一件出自塞缪尔·约翰逊先生本人手笔的文书；而且种种迹象几乎可以断定，这封信是写给当时身在法国某城的阿拉斯泰尔的，因为信中提到曾款待过一位阿兰·麦克莱恩——此人携使命渡海而来，正待启程归返。信上没有具收件人的名址，开头是「亲爱的先生」，结尾部分已经佚失；但由于信首写着「高夫广场」，信中又提及写信人已着手编纂他那部大辞典，其日期约可推测为 1748 年。可惜纸页破损多处、色泽黯败，只有一段文字能够确切无疑地抄录出来。我把它迻录于此，作为一段友谊的纪念——这段友谊将濡染一位伟人的思想，直至其临终之日；而我们可以确信，它在那位年轻高地人心上留下的印痕，同样永不磨灭。

===Original===
> “… I send by your kinsman the second moiety of the loan which you made me at our last meeting, for I assume that, like so many of your race and politics now in France, you are somewhat in straits for money. I do assure you that I can well afford to make the repayment, for I have concluded a profitable arrangement with the booksellers for the publication of an English dictionary, and have already received a considerable sum in advance.﻿ ﻿…
> “I will confess to you, my dear sir, that often in moments of leisure and in quiet places, my memory traverses our brief Odyssey, and I am moved again with fear and hope and the sadness of renunciation. You say, and I welcome your generosity, that from me you acquired something of philosophy; from you I am bound to reply that I learned weighty lessons in the conduct of our mortal life. You taught me that a man can be gay and yet most resolute, and that a Christian is not less capable of fortitude than an ancient Stoic. The recollection of that which we encountered together lives in me to warm my heart when it is cold, and to restore in dark seasons my trust in my fellow men. The end was a proof, if proof were wanted, of the vanity of human wishes, but sorrow does not imply failure, and my memory of it will not fade till the hour of death and the day of judgment.﻿ ﻿…
> “I have been at some pains to collect from my friends in Oxford news of my lady N﻿—﻿—. You will rejoice to hear that she does well. Her husband, who has now a better name in the shire, is an ensample of marital decorum and treats her kindly, and she has been lately blessed with a male child. That, I am confident, is the tidings which you desire to hear, for your affection for that lady has long been purged of any taint of selfishness, and you can rejoice in her welfare as in that of a sister. But I do not forget that you have buried your heart in that monument to domestic felicity. Our Master did not place us in this world to win even honest happiness, but to shape and purify our immortal souls, and sorrow must be the companion of the noblest endeavour. Like the shepherd in Virgil you grew at last acquainted with Love, and found him a native of the rocks.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
> 「……托你的族人捎去那笔款项的下半数——上次会面时你借与我的那一笔；因为我想，你同如今流寓法国的许多贵同族人与同党一样，手头想必有些拮据。我敢向你担保，这笔款子我是还得起的：我已与书商们谈妥一桩有利可图的安排，出版一部英语辞典，且已预收了一笔可观的款项。……
> 「亲爱的先生，我愿向你坦白：每每闲暇之时、清幽之地，我的记忆便重历我们那段短短的奥德赛之旅，恐惧、希望与舍弃的哀愁又再度使我动容。你说，从我这里你得到了几分哲学——你的厚意我欣然领受；而我必须回答：从你那里，我学得了安度此生的重大功课。你教会我，一个人尽可以快活，同时又最是坚毅；一个基督徒的坚忍，并不逊于古代的斯多噶哲人。我们共同阅历的种种，存留在我的心间，在我心冷时予它温暖，在晦暗的时节恢复我对同胞的信任。那结局，倘若还需要证明，正是人类愿望之虚妄的明证；然而悲哀并不意味着失败，我对它的记忆将永不消褪，直到死亡的时辰、审判的日子。……
> 「我曾颇费周章，从牛津的友人处搜集 N——夫人的消息。你听了必定欢喜：她一切都好。她的丈夫如今在郡中名声较佳，堪称婚姻美德的典范，待她温存；她近来又喜获一子。我确信，这正是你渴望听到的音讯，因为你对那位夫人的深情久已涤净一切自私的痕迹，你能为她的安康欢喜，如同为一位姊妹一般。但我未曾忘记，你已把心埋进了那座家室幸福的纪念碑。我们的主安置我们于这个世上，原不是为了赢取哪怕无愧于心的幸福，而是为了砥砺、净化我们不朽的灵魂；悲哀，必是最高贵的奋斗的同伴。你终于像维吉尔笔下的牧人那样认识了爱神，发现他原是顽岩间土生之物。……”

## Preface / 序言

===Original===
By the Editor

Last year my friend, Mr. Sebastian Derwent, on becoming senior partner of the reputable firm of solicitors which bears his name, instituted a very drastic clearing out of cupboards and shelves in the old house in Lincoln’s Inn Fields. Among a mass of derelict papers﻿—cancelled deeds, mouldy files of correspondence, copies of pleadings in cases long ago forgotten﻿—there was one little bundle which mystified him, since it had no apparent relation to the practice of the law. He summoned me to dinner, and, with our chairs drawn up to a bright fire and a decanter of his famous brown sherry between us, we discussed its antecedents.

First there was a document of three quarto pages, which appeared to be a fair copy in a scrivener’s hand. It started and finished abruptly, so we judged it to be a portion of a larger work. Then came a long ill-written manuscript, partly in a little volume of which the clasp and lock had been broken, and partly on loose paper which seemed to have been torn from the beginnings and ends of printed books. The paper had no watermark that we could discover, but its quality suggested the eighteenth century. Last there was a bundle of letters in various hands, all neatly docketed and dated. Mr. Derwent entrusted me with the papers, for certain words and phrases in the quarto sheets had stirred my interest. After considerable study I discovered that the packet contained a story, obscure in parts, but capable of being told with some pretence of continuity.
===Chinese===
——编者

去年，我的朋友塞巴斯蒂安·德温特（Sebastian Derwent）先生，在他那间以己姓冠名、声誉素著的律师事务所里升任资深合伙人，便对林肯律师学院广场那所老宅里的橱柜书架来了一次大刀阔斧的清仓。在一大堆废旧文契之中——已作废的契据、霉烂的往来信札案卷、早已无人记得的讼案的诉状副本——有一小捆东西令他百思不解，因为它与律师的营生显然毫无干系。他请我去吃饭，两把椅子拉近明亮的炉火，中间摆上一细颈瓶他那种有名的褐色雪利酒，我们便细细考究起这捆文件的来历。

头一件是三页四开本的文稿，看笔迹是抄写员誊清的工整副本。它起首突兀，煞尾也突兀，我们便断定这是一部更大著作的一部分。其次是一份字迹潦草的长篇手稿，一部分写在一小册本子里，册子的搭扣和锁扣俱已损坏；一部分写在散页上，那些纸似乎是从一些印本书籍的卷首卷尾撕下来的。纸上寻不出我们能发现的任何水印，但纸质看来像是十八世纪的东西。最后是一束书信，出自各个不同的手笔，都整整齐齐编了签条、注了日期。德温特先生把这几件文书托付给我，因为那几页四开纸上的某些字句引起了我的兴味。经过相当一番研读，我发现这包东西里藏着一段故事，虽有些地方晦暗不明，却还能勉强连缀成篇、叙述出来。

===Original===
First for the matter copied by the amanuensis. It was clearly a fragment, intended by the compiler to form part of an introduction to the work. On first reading it I rubbed my eyes and tasted the joy of the discoverer, for I believed that I had stumbled upon an unknown manuscript of Mr. James Boswell, written apparently after the publication of his *Life of Johnson*, and designed for a supplementary volume, which, Dr. Johnson being dead, he felt at liberty to compile. On reflection I grew less certain. The thing was undoubtedly the work of an intimate friend of the Great Lexicographer, but, though there were mannerisms of style and thought which suggested Mr. Boswell, I did not feel able to claim his authorship with any confidence. It might be the production of one or other of the Wartons, or of Sir Robert Chambers, or of some Oxford friend of Johnson whose name has not come down to us. Mr. Derwent at my request explored the records of his firm, which extended back for the better part of a century, but could find no evidence that it had ever done business for any member of the family of Auchinleck. Nevertheless I incline to attribute the thing to Mr. Boswell, for he alone of Johnson’s circle was likely to have the eager interest in Scotland which the manuscript reveals, and the dates do not conflict with what we know of his movements.

Here, at all events, is the text of it:
===Chinese===
先说抄写员誊录的那部分。它分明是个残篇，编者本意是拿它充当全书导言的一部分。初读之下，我揉了揉眼睛，尝到了发现者的欢喜，因为我自以为无意间觅得了詹姆斯·鲍斯韦尔（James Boswell）先生一篇不为人知的手稿——看样子写于他的《约翰逊传》（Life of Johnson）出版之后，是为一部补编而作；约翰逊博士既已辞世，他自觉尽可放手编纂。细细一想，我又不敢那样笃定。这东西无疑出自那位大辞书家某位密友之手，然而虽有种种笔调与思路上的习气令人想到鲍斯韦尔先生，我却不能信心十足地把著作权归到他名下。它或许出自沃顿兄弟中的这一位或那一位，或许出自罗伯特·钱伯斯爵士，或许出自约翰逊的某位牛津友人，而其姓名已不曾传到我们手里。应我之请，德温特先生翻检了事务所的旧档——那些档案上溯近一个世纪——却查不出本所曾为奥金莱克家族任何人经手过事务的凭据。话虽如此，我仍倾向于把此稿归诸鲍斯韦尔先生，因为约翰逊的朋友圈子里，唯有他一个人才会有手稿中透露的那种对苏格兰的热切兴味，况且稿中的日期也与我们对他行踪的了解并无抵牾。

无论如何，它的文字就在这里：

===Original===
> In the last week of June in the year 1763 Johnson was in Oxford, and I had the honour to accompany him one afternoon to the village of Elsfield, some four miles from the city, on a visit to Mr. Francis Wise, one of the fellows of Trinity College and Radcliffe’s librarian. As I have already mentioned, there were certain episodes in the past life of my illustrious friend as to which I knew nothing, and certain views, nay, I venture to say prejudices, in his mind, for the origin of which I was at a loss to account. In particular I could never receive from him any narrative of his life during the years 1745 and 1746, the years of our last civil war, during which his literary career seems to have been almost totally suspended. When I endeavoured to probe the matter, he answered me with some asperity, so that I feared to embarrass him with further questions. “Sir, I was very poor,” he once said, “and misery has no chronicles.” His reticence on the point was the more vexatious to me, since, though a loyal supporter of the present Monarchy and Constitution, he always revealed a peculiar tenderness towards the unfortunate House of Stuart, and I could not but think that in some episode in his past lay the key to a sentiment which was at variance with his philosophy of government. I was also puzzled to explain to my own mind the reason for his attitude towards Scotland and the Scotch nation, which afforded him matter for constant sarcasms and frequent explosions of wrath. As the world knows, he had a lively interest in the primitive life of the Highlands, and an apparent affection for those parts, but towards the rest of Scotland he maintained a demeanour so critical as to be liable to the reproach of harshness. These prejudices, cherished so habitually that they could not be attributed to mere fits of spleen, surprised me in a man of such preeminent justice and wisdom, and I was driven to think that some early incident in his career must have given them birth; but my curiosity remained unsatisfied, for when I interrogated him, I was met with a sullen silence, if we were alone, and, if company were present, a tempestuous ridicule which covered me with blushes.
===Chinese===
> 1763 年六月的最后一个星期，约翰逊正在牛津。一天下午，我有幸陪他到离城约四英里的埃尔斯菲尔德村（Elsfield），去拜访弗朗西斯·怀斯（Francis Wise）先生——三一学院的研究员、拉德克利夫图书馆的馆长。正如我从前说过的，我这位显赫友人的生平里有几段经历，我全然不知；他心中又有某些见解——恕我放肆说一句，是某些偏见——我一直无从解释其来历。尤其是 1745 和 1746 两年，那是我们最后一次内战的年头，其间他的笔墨生涯似乎几乎全然中断，而他对这两年间的身世，我从来没能从他口中得到过半句叙述。每当我设法探问，他总带着几分厉气回答我，吓得我不敢再用问题去窘他。「先生，我那时穷得很，」他有一回说，「而苦难是没有编年史的。」他在这一点上的缄默尤其叫我苦恼，因为他对当今的王室与宪制虽是忠贞不渝的拥护者，对不幸的斯图亚特王室却总流露出一种特别的温情；我不能不猜想，他过去某段经历里，正藏着这桩与他政见相悖的情愫的钥匙。他对待苏格兰和苏格兰民族的态度，也叫我百思不解——那态度供给他无穷无尽的讥刺资料，又屡屡惹得他勃然大怒。世人都知道，他对高地人的原始生活兴味盎然，对那一带地方也显然怀有感情；可对苏格兰其余的地方，他始终端着一副苛评的架子，难免要落下严酷的诟病。这些偏见他抱得如此习以为常，绝不能归之于一时半刻的意气；在这样一位公正睿智、无出其右的人身上，实在令我诧异。我于是认定，必是他早年际遇中的某桩事故孕育了它们；然而我的好奇心始终不得满足，因为我一问起，若是我们两人独处，他报以阴沉的沉默；若有旁人在座，他便掀起一阵狂风暴雨般的奚落，臊得我满面通红。

===Original===
> On this occasion at Elsfield that happened which whetted my curiosity, but the riddle remained unread till at this late stage of my life, when my revered Master has long been dead, fortune has given the key into my hand. Mr. Francis Wise dwelt in a small ancient manor of Lord North’s, situated on the summit of a hill with a great prospect over the Cherwell valley and beyond it to the Cotswold uplands. We walked thither, and spent the hour before dinner very pleasantly in a fine library, admiring our host’s collection of antiquities and turning the pages of a noble folio wherein he had catalogued the coins in the Bodleian collection. Johnson was in a cheerful humour, the exercise of walking had purified his blood, and at dinner he ate heartily of veal sweetbreads, and drank three or four glasses of Madeira wine. I remember that he commended especially a great ham. “Sir,” he said, “the flesh of the pig is most suitable for Englishmen and Christians. Foreigners love it little, Jews and infidels abhor it.”
===Chinese===
> 这回在埃尔斯菲尔德，偏偏发生了那桩事，把我的好奇磨得更利了；然而谜底始终无人读出，直到我迟暮之年——我敬爱的师长下世已久——命运才把钥匙交到我手里。弗朗西斯·怀斯先生住在诺思勋爵名下一座古老的小庄宅里，宅子坐落在一座山冈顶上，俯瞰切尔韦尔河谷，望过河谷直抵科茨沃尔德高地。我们步行到了那里，在主人家雅致的藏书室里打发饭前那一个钟头，甚是惬意：一面赏看主人的古物收藏，一面翻检一部堂皇的对开本，他在这书里著录了博德利馆藏的钱币。约翰逊兴致很好，一番步行涤清了他的血液；席间他痛痛快快吃了小牛胸腺，喝了三四杯马德拉酒。我记得他对一只大火腿尤为称赏。「先生，」他说，「猪肉最宜于英国人和基督徒。外国人不大爱它，犹太人和异教徒则深恶痛绝。」

===Original===
> When the meal was over we walked in the garden, which was curiously beautified with flowering bushes and lawns adorned with statues and fountains. We assembled for tea in an arbour, constructed after the fashion of a Roman temple, on the edge of a clear pool. Beyond the water there was a sharp declivity, which had been utilised to make a cascade from the pool’s overflow. This descended to a stone tank like an ancient bath, and on each side of the small ravine lines of beeches had been planted. Through the avenue of the trees there was a long vista of meadows in the valley below, extending to the wooded eminence of the Duke of Marlborough’s palace of Blenheim, and beyond to the Cotswold hills. The sun was declining over these hills, and, since the arbour looked to the west, the pool and the cascade were dappled with gold, and pleasant beams escaped through the shade to our refuge.

> Johnson was regaled with tea, while Mr. Wise and I discussed a fresh bottle of wine. It was now that my eminent friend’s demeanour, which had been most genial during dinner, suffered a sudden change. The servant who waited upon us was an honest Oxfordshire rustic with an open countenance and a merry eye. To my surprise I observed Johnson regarding him with extreme disfavour. “Who is that fellow?” he asked when the man had left us. Mr. Wise mentioned his name, and that he was of a family in the village. “His face reminds me of a very evil scoundrel,” was the reply. “A Scotchman,” he added. “But no nation has the monopoly of rogues.”
===Chinese===
> 饭罢我们在园中散步，园子装点得别有情趣：花木扶疏，草坪上缀着雕像和喷泉。我们聚在一座凉亭里用茶，亭子仿罗马神庙的式样筑成，立在一泓清池边上。池水那面是一面陡坡，正好利用池中漫出的水做成一道小瀑布。瀑布泻进一座石槽，形如古时的浴池；小小峡谷的两壁各栽着一列列山毛榉。透过树木的夹道，谷底的草地在下方铺展出长长一幅远景，一直延展到马尔伯勒公爵布伦海姆宫林木葱茏的冈峦，再远处便是科茨沃尔德群山。夕阳正向那群山沉落，凉亭面朝西，池水和瀑布都洒满碎金，宜人的光线穿过浓荫，漏进我们这座栖身的小亭里来。

> 有茶款待了约翰逊，怀斯先生和我则共酌一瓶新开的酒。就在这时，我这位大名鼎鼎的朋友饭席间本是极其和蔼的，神色却陡然一变。侍候我们的仆人是个老实的牛津郡乡下人，面目开朗，眼神快活。岂料我看见约翰逊正用极不痛快的眼色打量他。「那家伙是谁？」等人退下后，他问道。怀斯先生说了那人的名字，又说他是村里一户人家的人。「他这张脸叫我想起一个十分歹毒的恶棍，」他答道，又补上一句，「是个苏格兰人。不过恶棍这门营生，可不是哪一国能独占的。」

===Original===
> After that my friend’s brow remained cloudy, and he stirred restlessly in his chair, as if eager to be gone. Our host talked of the antiquities in the neighbourhood, notably of the White Horse in Berkshire and of a similar primitive relic in Buckinghamshire, but he could elicit no response, though the subject was one to which I knew Johnson’s interest to be deeply pledged. He remained with his chin sunk on his breast, and his eyes moody as if occupied with painful memories. I made anxious inquiries as to his health, but he waved me aside. Once he raised his head, and remained for some time staring across the valley at the declining sun.

> “What are these hills?” he asked.

> Mr. Wise repeated names﻿—Woodstock, Ditchley, Enstone. “The trees on the extreme horizon,” he said, “belong to Wychwood Forest.”

> The words seemed to add to Johnson’s depression. “Is it so?” he murmured. “Verily a strange coincidence. Sir, among these hills, which I now regard, were spent some of the bitterest moments of my life.”
===Chinese===
> 打那以后，我朋友的额头便一直阴云不散，他在椅子里坐立不安，仿佛巴不得立刻走人。主人谈起左近的古物，特别说到伯克郡的白马，还有白金汉郡一处类似的太古遗迹，却引不出他半点回应——虽然我深知，这个题目本是约翰逊兴味所深系。他始终下巴抵在胸前，眼神郁郁，像是被苦痛的往事盘踞了。我焦急地探问他的身子安好，他只挥手叫我别管。有一回他抬起头来，好一阵子呆望着谷那边西沉的太阳。

> 「这些山是什么山？」他问。

> 怀斯先生报出一串名字——伍德斯托克、迪奇利、恩斯通。「天边极处的那片树木，」他说，「属于威奇伍德森林。」

> 这话似乎更添了约翰逊的愁郁。「当真么？」他喃喃道，「确是一桩奇特的巧合。先生，在我此刻眺望的这些山间，消磨过我一生中最辛酸的一些时日。」

===Original===
> He said no more, and I durst not question him, nor did I ever succeed at any later date in drawing him back to the subject. I have a strong recollection of the discomfort of that occasion, for Johnson relapsed into glumness and presently we rose to leave. Mr. Wise, who loved talking and displayed his treasures with the zest of the owner of a raree-show, would have us visit, before going, a Roman altar which, he said, had lately been unearthed on his estate. Johnson viewed it peevishly, and pointed out certain letters in the inscription which seemed fresher than the rest. Mr. Wise confessed that he had himself re-cut these letters, in conformity, as he believed, with the purpose of the original. This threw Johnson into a transport of wrath. “Sir,” he said, “the man who would tamper with an ancient monument, with whatever intentions, is capable of defiling his father’s tomb.” There was no word uttered between us on the walk back to Oxford. Johnson strode at such a pace that I could scarcely keep abreast of him, and I would fain have done as he did on an earlier occasion, and cried *Sufflamina*.1
===Chinese===
> 他再没有多说，我也不敢再问；此后无论到什么时候，我始终没能把他引回这个话题上。那天场合的窘迫，我至今记忆犹新：约翰逊重新堕入阴郁，不多时我们便起身告辞。怀斯先生素来健谈，又带着西洋景摊主夸耀珍品的那股兴头，临行前非要我们先去看一座罗马祭坛不可，说是不久前刚在他的庄园里出土。约翰逊没好气地端详了一阵，指出铭文里有几个字母比其余的显得新。怀斯先生承认，那几个字母是他亲手重新凿刻的，他自信正合原刻的本意。这话登时把约翰逊激得勃然大怒。「先生，」他说，「谁若肯窜改一座古代碑碣，不管存着什么心，谁就有本事去玷污自己父亲的坟墓。」回牛津的一路上，我们彼此没有交谈一句话。约翰逊迈开大步，我几乎跟不上他；我很不得照他早先那回的样子，喊出一声「拦住我」（Sufflamina）。1

===Original===
> The incident which I have recorded has always remained vivid in my memory, but I despaired of unravelling the puzzle, and believed that the clue was buried forever in the grave of the illustrious dead. But, by what I prefer to call Providence rather than Chance, certain papers have lately come into my possession, which enable me to clear up the mystery of that summer evening, to add a new chapter to the life of one of the greatest of mankind, and to portray my dear and revered friend in a part which cannot fail to heighten our conception of the sterling worth of his character.

Thus far the quarto pages. Their author﻿—Mr. Boswell or some other﻿—no doubt intended to explain how he received the further papers, and to cast them into some publishable form. Neither task was performed. The rest of the manuscript, as I have said, was orderly enough, but no editorial care had been given it. I have discovered nothing further about Alastair Maclean save what the narrative records, and my research among the archives of Oxfordshire families has not enabled me to add much to the history of the other figures. But I have put such materials as I had into the form of a tale, which seems of sufficient interest to present to the world. I could wish that Mr. Boswell had lived to perform the task, for I am confident that he would have made a better job of it.
===Chinese===
> 我记下的这段遭遇，始终鲜明地留在我记忆里；可我对解开这个谜早已绝望，只道线索已随那位显赫的亡人永远埋进了坟墓。然而——我宁愿称之为天意，而非机缘——近来有几件文书落到我手中，使我能解开那个夏夜的疑团，为人类最伟大的人物之一的一生添上一章新篇，并把我亲爱敬重的朋友的一个侧面描画出来；这个侧面，必能令我们对他品格的足金成色更添景仰。

四开纸上的文字，止于此。它的作者——鲍斯韦尔先生也好，另有其人也罢——无疑是打算交代那批后续文书如何到手，并把它编成可以付印的样式的。这两桩事他都没有做。手稿的其余部分，如前所述，倒还井然有序，只是不曾受过任何编订的照管。关于阿拉斯泰尔·麦克莱恩，除了叙事本身所载，我再没有考出什么；我对牛津郡各家档案的查考，也没能为其余人物的历史增添多少材料。不过我已把手头仅有的材料写成了这样一篇故事，看来兴味也还足够，可以公之于世了。我惟愿鲍斯韦尔先生能活着做成这件事，因为我深信，他一定做得比我漂亮。

## XIX Ramoth-Gilead / 十九、基列的拉末（Ramoth-Gilead）

===Original===

Three hours’ hard riding should have brought them to the tail of the Highland army, but the horses were still in their stalls when the night fell. For, as he sat by the fire with Johnson, the latches of Alastair’s strength were loosened and it fell from him. The clout on the head, the imperfect convalescence, the seasons of mental conflict and the many hours in the saddle had brought even his tough body to cracking-point. The room swam before his eyes, there was burning pain in his head, and dizziness and nausea made him collapse in his chair. Johnson and the hostess’s son, a half-grown boy, carried him to bed, and all night he was in an ague﻿—the return, perhaps, of the low fever which had followed his wound at Fontenoy. There was a buzzing in his brain which happily prevented thought, and next day, when the fever ebbed, he was so weak that his mind was content to be vacant. By such merciful interposition he escaped the bitterest pangs of reproach which would have followed his realisation of failure.

The first afternoon Johnson sat with him, giving him vinegar and water to sip, and changing the cool cloths on his brow. Alastair was drifting aimlessly on the tide of weakness, seeing faces﻿—Claudia, Kitty of Queensberry, Cornbury, very notably the handsome periwigged head of the King’s Solicitor﻿—like the stone statues in a garden. They had no cognisance of him, and he did not wish to attract their notice, for they belonged to a world that had vanished, and concerned him less than the figures on a stage. By and by his consciousness became clearer, and he was aware of a heartbreak that enveloped him like an atmosphere, a great cloud of grief that must shadow his path forever. And yet there were rifts in it where light as from a spring sky broke through, and he found himself melting at times in a sad tenderness. He had lost tragically, but he had learned that there was more to prize than he had dreamed.

Johnson, his face like a bishop’s, sat at the bed foot, saying nothing, but gazing at the sick man with the eyes of an old friendly dog. When Alastair was able to drink the gruel the hostess produced, the tutor considered that he must assist his recovery by sprightly conversation. But the honest man’s soul had been so harassed in the past days that he found it hard to be jocose. He sprawled in his wooden chair, and the window which faced him revealed sundry rents in his small-clothes and the immense shabbiness of his coat. Alastair on his bed watched the heavy pitted features, the blinking eyes, the perpetually twitching hands with a certainty that never in his days had he seen a man so uncouth or so wholly to be loved; and, as he looked, he seemed to discern that in the broad brow and the noble head which was also to be revered.

===Chinese===

策马疾驰三个钟头，本该把他们送到高地军的殿后队尾，可是夜幕降临时，马儿还拴在厩中。因为阿拉斯泰尔同约翰逊坐在火边时，他周身气力的搭扣竟松开了，整副气力从他身上垮落下去。头上挨的那一记，尚未痊愈的病体，几番内心的交战，加上鞍马劳顿的许多时辰，终于把他强健的躯体逼到了崩断的边缘。房间在他眼前打转，脑袋里烧灼作痛，眩晕与恶心使他瘫倒在椅中。约翰逊和女店主的儿子——一个半大孩子——把他抬上床，他整夜寒热交作——那也许是他丰特努瓦创伤之后低热的回潮。脑中嗡嗡作响，倒好在不许他思想；次日热退，他虚弱至极，心神也乐得一片空白。上苍如此仁慈地居间调停，使他躲过了那最刻毒的自责之痛——一旦意识到自己的失败，那痛楚本是要随之而来的。

头一天下午，约翰逊守在他床边，喂他啜饮醋水，替他更换额上的凉布。阿拉斯泰尔在虚弱的潮水里无目的地漂流，看见一张张面孔——克劳迪娅、昆斯伯里的基蒂、科恩伯里，还有格外分明的那颗扑粉假发之下的漂亮脑袋，国王的副检察长——如同园中的石像。他们并不知道有他，他也不愿招他们注目，因为他们属于一个已经消逝的世界，与他相干之少，还不及戏台上的角色。渐渐地他的神志清楚了些，便觉出一场心碎将他包裹，像一层大气，一大团愁云，注定要把他的前路永远罩在阴影里。然而云中亦有裂罅，春日晴空般的光从那里透进来，他发现自己时时融化在一阵哀伤的温柔里。他输得惨烈，却学会了世上可珍爱的东西，比他梦中所想的更多。

约翰逊坐在床脚，一张脸像主教般慈和，一言不发，只以一条老而友善的狗那样的眼睛，凝望着病人。等到阿拉斯泰尔能喝下女店主端来的麦片糊了，这位塾师便认定，须以轻快的谈兴助他康复。但这个诚实人的灵魂连日来备受煎熬，要诙谐起来竟觉得艰难。他摊手摊脚坐在木椅里，对面的窗子照出他紧身马裤上绽开的几道口子，和他外衣上那份了不起的寒酸。阿拉斯泰尔躺在床上，望着那张麻点斑斑的厚重面孔、眨动不停的眼睛、永远抽搐着的双手，确信平生从未见过一个如此粗陋、却又如此全然可爱的人；而且望着望着，他仿佛还能在那宽阔的额头和高贵的头颅里，辨出一种同样令人起敬的东西。

===Original===

The young man’s gaze having after the fashion of sick folk fixed itself upon one spot, Johnson became conscious of it, and looked down on his disreputable garments with distaste not unmixed with humour.

“My clothes are old and sorry,” he said. “I lament the fact, sir, for I am no lover of negligence in dress. A wise man dare not go underdressed till he is of consequence enough to forbear carrying the badge of his rank upon his back. That is not my case, and I would fain be more decent in my habiliments, which do not properly become even my modest situation in life. But I confess that at the moment I have but two guineas, given me by my dear young lady, and I have destined them for another purpose than haberdashery.”

What this purpose was appeared before the next evening. During the afternoon Johnson disappeared in company with the youth of the inn, and returned at the darkening with a face flushed and triumphant. Alastair, whose strength was reviving, was sitting up when the door opened to admit a deeply self-conscious figure.

===Chinese===

年轻人的目光循着病人的习性凝定在一处，约翰逊觉察了，低头打量自己这身不体面的衣衫，神情厌恶之中不无幽默。

“我的衣裳又旧又不像话，”他说，“先生，我为这件事痛心，因为我素来不喜衣冠不整。一个聪明人不敢穿得寒酸，除非他的身份已经贵重到不必再把地位的徽记背在背上。我尚非此例，我也但愿自己在服饰上能更整齐些——如今这身行头，连我那份寒微的人生境遇都衬不起。但我承认，眼下手头只有两几尼，是那位亲爱的小姐所赠，我已把它们派定了别的用途，不是添置衣着。”

这用途是什么，次日入夜之前便见分晓。下午，约翰逊同客栈那个小伙计一道不见了踪影，暮色四合时才回来，一张脸红扑扑的，得意洋洋。阿拉斯泰尔体力渐复，正坐起身来，只见房门开了，进来一个局促不安得厉害的人影。

===Original===

It was Johnson in a secondhand riding-coat of blue camlet, cut somewhat in the military fashion, and in all likelihood once the property of some dashing yeoman. But that was only half of his new magnificence, for below the riding-coat, beneath his drab coat, and buckled above his waistcoat, was a great belt, and from the belt depended a long scabbard.

“I make you my compliments,” said Alastair. “You have acquired a cloak.”

“Nay, sir, but I have acquired a better thing. I have got me a sword.”

He struggled with his skirts and after some difficulty drew from its sheath a heavy old-fashioned cut-and-thrust blade, of the broadsword type. With it he made a pass or two, and then brought it down in a sweep which narrowly missed the bedpost.

===Chinese===

正是约翰逊，穿一件蓝色的二手羽纱骑装，样式裁得略带军装风味，多半曾属于某位英武的自耕农。但这不过是他新添威仪的一半，因为骑装底下、在那件黄褐色外衣之下、马甲之上，束着一条宽阔的腰带，腰带上垂挂着一个长长的剑鞘。

“我向你道贺，”阿拉斯泰尔说，“你添了一件斗篷。”

“非也，先生，我添的是一件更好的东西。我给自己弄到了一柄剑。”

他与衣裾搏斗了一阵，费了些气力才从鞘中拔出一柄沉重而老式的劈刺两用剑，是阔剑的路数。他拿它比划了一两个剑势，随即大开大阖地挥落下来，险些削中床柱。

===Original===

“Now am I armed against all enemies,” he cried, stamping his foot. “If Polyphemus comes, have at his eye,” and he lunged towards the window.

The mingled solemnity and triumph of his air checked Alastair’s laughter. “This place is somewhat confined for swordplay,” he said. “Put it up, and tell me where you discovered the relic.”

“I purchased it this very afternoon, through the good offices of the lad below. There was an honest or indifferent honest fellow in the neighbourhood who sold me cloak, belt and sword for three half-guineas. It is an excellent weapon, and I trust to you, sir, to give me a lesson or two in its use.”

He flung off the riding-coat, unbuckled the belt and sat himself in his accustomed chair.

===Chinese===
