“如今我武装齐备，不怕一切敌人了，”他跺着脚喊道，“波吕斐摩斯若是来了，就刺他的眼睛，”说着朝窗户一剑刺去。

他那神气庄重与得意交织的模样，止住了阿拉斯泰尔的笑。“这屋子舞起剑来未免局促了些，”他说，“把它收了吧，再告诉我，你在哪儿寻见这件古董的。”

“就在今天下午买的，全亏楼下那小家伙奔走出力。附近有个诚实的——或者说不大老实的——家伙，用三枚半几尼的价钱，把斗篷、腰带连同剑一齐卖给了我。这是一件极好的兵器，我信得过你，先生，还望你指点我一两手用法。”

他甩掉骑装，解下腰带，在自己惯坐的那把椅子上落了座。

===Original===

“Two men are better than one on the roads,” he said, “the more if both are armed. I would consult you, sir, on a point of honour. I have told you that I am reputably, though not highly born, and I have had a gentleman’s education. I am confident that but for a single circumstance, no gentleman need scruple to cross swords with me or to draw his sword by my side. The single circumstance is this﻿—I have reason to believe that a relative suffered death by hanging, though for what cause I do not know, since the man disappeared utterly and his end is only a matter of gossip. Yet I must take the supposition at its worst. Tell me, sir, does that unhappy connection in your view deprive me of the armigerous rights of a gentleman?”

This time Alastair did not forbear to smile.

“Why no, sir. In my own land, the gallows is reckoned an ornament to a pedigree, and it has been the end of many a promising slip of my own house. Indeed it is not unlikely to be the end of me. But why do you ask the question?”

===Chinese===

“路上同行，两个人总比一个人强，”他说，“何况两个都带了兵器。先生，我有一件关乎名誉的事要请教于你。我告诉过你，我出身清白，虽不算高贵，受的却是绅士的教育。我深信，若非一桩变故，任何绅士与我交锋、或与并肩拔剑，都无须有所踌躇。这桩变故是——我有理由相信，我有一位亲眷是受绞刑而死的，所犯何罪我却不知，因为那人从此杳无音讯，他的结局不过是市井闲谈的资料。然而我必须从最坏处着想。请告诉我，先生，依你之见，这层不幸的干连，是否就剥夺了我作为绅士佩剑的权利？”

这一回，阿拉斯泰尔忍不住笑了。

“哪里的话，先生。在我自己的家乡，绞架被算作家谱上的一种装饰，我族中多少前程远大的枝条，都是以它作结的。说实在的，我的下场说不定也是它。可你为何问起这个？”

===Original===

“Because I purpose to go with you to the wars.”

Johnson’s face was as serious as a judge’s, and his dull eyes had kindled with a kind of shamefaced ardour. The young man felt so strong a tide of affection rising in him for this uncouth crusader that he had to do violence to his own inclination in shaping his counsel.

“It cannot be, my dear sir,” he cried. “I honour you, I love you, but I will not permit a futile sacrifice. Had England risen for our Prince, your aid would have been most heartily welcome, but now the war will be in Scotland, and I tell you it is as hopeless as a battle of a single kestrel against a mob of ravens. I fight in it, for that is my trade and duty; I have been bred to war, and it is the quarrel of my house and my race. But for you it is none of these things. You would be a stranger in a foreign strife.﻿ ﻿… Nay, sir, but you must listen to reason. You are a scholar and have your career to make in a far different world. God knows I would welcome your comradeship, for I respect your courage and I love your honest heart, but I cannot suffer you to ride to certain ruin. Gladly I accept your convoy, but you will stop short of Ramoth-Gilead.”

===Chinese===

“因为我打算随你去从军打仗。”

约翰逊的脸肃穆得像一位法官，平日黯淡的眼睛里燃起一种近乎羞怯的热忱。年轻人只觉一股挚爱之潮为这个粗陋的十字军武士涌起，汹涌得他不得不强按自己的心意，来斟酌进言。

“这万万使不得，我亲爱的先生，”他喊道，“我敬你，我爱你，可我不能容许一场无谓的牺牲。倘若英格兰为我们王子起兵，你的相助我们求之不得；可如今战事将在苏格兰，我告诉你，那希望之渺茫，好比一只红隼对一群渡鸦的战斗。我投身其中，因为那是我的本分与职守；我自幼习战，那是我的家族我的种族的公案。对你来说却全然不是这些。你到了那场异邦的争斗里，只是一个外人。……不，先生，你务必听我讲道理。你是个学者，你的前程要在另一个全然不同的世界里建立。天知道我何等欢迎你作伴，我敬重你的勇气，爱你这颗诚实的心，可我不能眼看你骑马奔向注定的毁灭。你的护送我欣然领受，但你须在基列的拉末（Ramoth-Gilead，《列王纪上》中的避难之城）之前止步。”

===Original===

The other’s face was a heavy mask of disappointment. “I must be the judge of my own path,” he said sullenly.

“But you will be guided in that judgment by one who knows better than you the certainties of the road. It is no part of a man’s duty to walk aimlessly to death.”

The last word seemed to make Johnson pause. But he recovered himself.

“I have counted the cost,” he said. “I fear death, God knows, but not more than other men. I will be no stranger in your wars. I will change my name to MacIan, and be as fierce as any Highlander.”

===Chinese===

对方那张脸成了失望的一副重面具。“我自己的路，须由我自己来做裁断。”他悻悻地说。

“但这裁断，你总要听一听深知此路凶险之人指点。漫无目的地走向死亡，可不在人的本分之内。”

最后那个字似乎叫约翰逊迟疑了一下。但他随即定住了神。

“代价我已掂量过了，”他说，“我怕死，天知道，可不见得比旁人更甚。到你们的战事里我不会是个外人。我要改名麦克伊恩，凶悍不输任何高地人。”

===Original===

“It cannot be. What you told Midwinter is the truth. If you are not fitted by nature for Old England, still less are you fitted for our wild long-memoried North. You will go back to London, Mr. Johnson, and some day you will find fortune and happiness. You will marry some day﻿ ﻿…”

At the word Johnson’s face grew very red, and he turned his eyes on the ground and rolled his head with an odd nervous motion.

“I have misled you,” he said. “I have been married these ten years. My dear Tetty is now living in the vicinity of London.﻿ ﻿… I have not written to her for seven weeks. *Mea culpa! Mea maxima culpa!*”

===Chinese===

“使不得。你对仲冬说的那番话是实情。既然天性不合老英格兰，你就更不合我们那片野性而旧忆绵长的北国。你回伦敦去吧，约翰逊先生，有朝一日你会寻得富贵与幸福。你总有一天要娶妻的……”

听到这个字眼，约翰逊的脸涨得通红，他把眼睛垂向地面，带着一种古怪的神经质动作摇起头来。

“我误导了你，”他说，“我成婚已十年了。我亲爱的泰蒂如今就住在伦敦近郊。……我已有七个星期没给她写信了。*Mea culpa! Mea maxima culpa!*（我之罪也！我罪之大莫可名状！）”

===Original===

He put his head in his hands and seemed to be absorbed in a passion of remorse.

“You must surely return to her,” said Alastair gently.

Johnson raised his head. “I would not have you think that I had forgotten her. She has her own small fortune, which suffices for one, though scant enough for two. I earn so little that I am rather an encumbrance than an aid, and she is more prosperous in my absence.”

“Yet she must miss you, and if you fall she will be widowed.”

===Chinese===

他双手抱头，仿佛沉浸在一阵痛悔的激浪里。

“你总该回到她身边去。”阿拉斯泰尔温和地说。

约翰逊抬起头来。“我不要你以为我忘了她。她自己有一小笔财产，一人度日尽够，两人用便拮据了。我挣得太少，与其说是帮手，不如说是拖累，我不在跟前，她反倒宽裕些。”

“可她必定惦念你；你若阵亡，她便要守寡。”

===Original===

“True, true. I have no clearness in the matter. I will seek light in prayer and sleep.” He marched from the room, leaving his new accoutrements lying neglected in a corner.

Next day Alastair was sufficiently recovered to travel, and the two set out shortly after daylight. The woman of the inn, who had been instructed by Midwinter, had counsel to give. The Ashbourne road was too dangerous, for already the pursuit had begun and patrols of Government horse were on the trail of the Highlanders; two gentlemen such as they might be taken for the tail of the rebels and suffer accordingly. She advised that the road should be followed by Chesterfield and the east side of the county, which would avoid the high hills of the Peak and bring them to Manchester and the Lancashire levels by an easier if a longer route. It was agreed that the two should pass as master and man﻿—Mr. Andrew Watson, the coal-merchant of Newcastle, and his secretary.

The secretary, ere they started, drew his sword and fingered it lovingly. “I must tell you,” he whispered to Alastair, “that the reflections of the night have not shaken my purpose. I am still resolved to accompany you to the wars.”

===Chinese===

“是了，是了。此事我心中尚无明见。我要从祈祷和睡眠中寻求光照。”他大步走出房去，任由他那身新得的行头落寞地撂在墙角。

次日，阿拉斯泰尔已恢复得可以上路，天亮不久两人便启程了。受仲冬嘱咐过的客栈妇人有一番指教。阿什本大道太危险，因为追缉已经开始，政府军的骑兵巡队正沿着高地人的踪迹搜索；他们这样两位绅士，很可能被当作叛军的尾巴，随之吃些苦头。她劝他们取道切斯特菲尔德、沿郡境东侧行走，既避开皮克的高山，又可由一条虽绕远却平顺的路，把他们送到曼彻斯特和兰开夏的平川。两人议定，扮作主仆同行——纽卡斯尔的海煤商人安德鲁·沃森先生，携其文书一名。

动身之前，那文书先拔出剑来，爱抚地摩挲着。“我必须告诉你，”他低声对阿拉斯泰尔说，“昨夜的思量并未动摇我的决心。我仍旧打定主意，要陪你一同赴战。”

===Original===

But there was no gusto in his air. All that day among the shallow vales he hardly spoke, and now and then would groan lamentably. The weather was mist and driving rain, and the travellers’ prospect was little beyond the puddles of the road and the wet glistening stone of the roadside dykes. That night they had risen into the hills, where the snow lay in the hollows and at the dyke-backs, and slept at a wretched hovel of a smithy on a bed of bracken. The smith, a fellow with a week’s beard and red-rimmed eyes, gave the news of the place. The Scots, he had heard, had passed Macclesfield the night before, and all day the militia, horsed by the local squires, had been scouting the moors picking up breechless stragglers. He did not appear to suspect his sullen visitors, who proclaimed their hurry to reach Manchester on an errand of trade.

Thereafter to both men the journey was a nightmare. In Manchester, where they slept a night, the mob was burning Charles in effigy and hiccuping “George is magnanimous”﻿—that mob which some weeks before had worn white favours and drunk damnation to Hanover. They saw a few miserable Highlanders, plucked from the tail of the army, in the hands of the town guard, and a mountebank in a booth had got himself up in a parody of a kilt and sang ribaldry to a screaming crowd. They heard, too, of the Government troops hard on the trail, Wade cutting in from the east by the hill roads, Cumberland hastening from the south, Bland’s and Cobham’s regiments already north of the town, mounted yeomen to guard the fords and bridges, and beacons blazing on every hill to raise the country.

===Chinese===

可他神气里全无兴致。那一整天行在浅谷之间，他几乎不说话，还不时发出可怜的呻吟。天气是雾，是斜扑的雨，旅人眼中所见，差不多不出路上的泥潭和路旁沟墙湿漉漉发亮的石头。入夜他们已上到山里，洼地和沟墙背面积着雪，宿在一座铁匠铺的破窝棚里，睡的是蕨草铺的床。铁匠是个胡子一星期没刮、眼圈通红的家伙，说了些地方上的消息。据他所闻，苏格兰人前一夜已过麦克斯菲尔德，本地乡绅们出资备马组建的民团，一整天都在荒原上巡弋，搜捕那些不穿马裤的掉队散兵。他似乎并不疑心这两位郁郁寡欢的客人，他们自称有贸易上的急务，要赶去曼彻斯特。

此后在两人眼里，这趟旅程成了一场噩梦。在曼彻斯特歇宿一夜，只见暴民焚着查理的画像，打着嗝儿高呼“乔治宽宏大量”——正是几个星期前还佩着白色饰结、举杯咒汉诺威遭天谴的那群暴民。他们看见几个从大军尾巴上揪下来的可怜高地人，落在城防队手里；集市棚子里一个卖艺的，把自己装扮成一条滑稽仿制的苏格兰短裙，对着尖叫的人群唱着下流小调。他们还听闻政府军已紧咬踪迹：韦德正从东面山道切入，坎伯兰从南面兼程赶来，布兰德团和科巴姆团已到城北，骑马的自耕农义勇队守着津渡桥梁，座座山头烽火齐燃，要唤起全境。

===Original===

“The Prince must halt and fight,” Alastair told his companion as they rode out of Manchester next morning. “With this hell’s pack after him he will be smothered unless he turn and tear them. Lord George will command the rearguard, and I am positive he will stand at Preston. Ribble ford is the place. You may yet witness a battle, and have the chance of fleshing that blade of yours.”

But when they came to Preston﻿—by circuitous ways, for they had to keep up the pretence of timid travellers, and the main road was too thick with alarums﻿—they found the bridge held by dragoons. Here they were much catechised, and, having given Newcastle as their destination, were warned that the northern roads into Yorkshire were not for travellers and bidden go back to Manchester. The Prince, it seemed, was at Lancaster, and Lord George and the Glengarry men and the Appin Stewarts halfway between that town and Preston.

That night Alastair implored Johnson to return. “We are on the edge of battle,” he told him, “and I beseech you to keep away from what can only bring you ruin.” But the other was obstinate. “I will see you at any rate on the eve of joining your friends,” he said. “We have yet to reach Ramoth-Gilead.”

===Chinese===

“王子殿下必须停下来打一仗，”次晨策马出曼彻斯特时，阿拉斯泰尔对同伴说，“这群地狱的猎犬在后面追赶，他若不回头撕咬，便要被闷死。乔治勋爵将统率后卫，我确信他会在普雷斯顿站住脚。里布尔河的渡口就是那地方。你说不定还能亲眼见一场战役，有你那口剑开荤的机会。”

可是等他们到了普雷斯顿——走的是迂回小径，因为要维持胆小商旅的做派，而大路上风声鹤唳——却见桥梁已由龙骑兵把守。在这里他们被盘问良久，报了纽卡斯尔为目的地后，被警告说通往约克郡的北面道路不是旅人走的，勒令折回曼彻斯特。王子殿下似乎驻在兰开斯特，乔治勋爵同格伦加里的族众、阿平的斯图亚特人，驻在那城与普雷斯顿之间的半路上。

当夜，阿拉斯泰尔恳求约翰逊回去。“我们已经到了战事的边缘，”他告诉他，“我求你远离这只能给你带来毁灭的东西。”可对方固执。“无论如何，我要送你到与朋友们会师的前夜，”他说，“咱们的基列的拉末，还有得走呢。”

===Original===

The Preston dragoons were too busy on their own affairs to give much heed to two prosaic travellers. Alastair and Johnson stole out of the town easily enough next morning, and making a wide circuit to the west joined the Lancaster road near Garstang. To their surprise the highway was almost deserted, and they rode into Lancaster without hindrance. There they found the town in a hubbub, windows shuttered, entries barricaded, the watch making timid patrols about the streets, and one half the people looking anxiously south, the other fearfully north to the Kendal road. The Prince had been there no later than yesterday, and the rearguard had left at dawn. News had come that the Duke of Cumberland was recalled, because of a French landing, and there were some who said that now the Scots would turn south again and ravage their way to London.

The news, which he did not believe, encouraged Alastair to mend his pace. There had been some kind of check in the pursuit, and the Prince might yet cross the Border without a battle. He believed that this would be Lord George’s aim, who knew his army and would not risk it, if he could, in a weary defensive action. The speed of march would therefore be increased, and he must quicken if he would catch them up. The two waited in Lancaster only to snatch a meal, and then set out by the Hornby road, intending to fetch a circuit towards Kendal, where it seemed likely the Prince would lie.

The afternoon was foggy and biting cold, so that Alastair looked for snow and called on Johnson to hurry before the storm broke. But the fall was delayed, and up to the darkening they rode in an icy haze through the confused foothills. The mountains were beginning again, the hills of bent and heather that he knew; the streams swirled in grey rock-rimmed pools, the air had the sour, bleak, yet invigorating tang of his own country. But now he did not welcome it, for it was the earnest of defeat. He was returning after failure. Nay, he was leaving his heart buried in the soft South country, which once he had despised. A wild longing, the perversion of homesickness, filled him for the smoky brown champaigns and the mossy woodlands which now enshrined the jewel of Claudia. He had thought that regrets were put away forever and that he had turned his eyes stonily to a cold future, but he had forgotten that he was young.

===Chinese===

普雷斯顿的龙骑兵自家的事还忙不过来，无暇多理会两个乏味的旅人。次日清晨，阿拉斯泰尔和约翰逊不费什么力气便溜出城去，向西兜了个大圈子，在加斯唐附近接上了去兰开斯特的大道。出乎他们意料，官道几乎空无一人，他们毫无阻碍地骑进了兰开斯特。只见满城惶惶，窗户上了板，巷口设了栅，更夫们在街上怯生生地巡逻，一半人焦急地望着南面，另一半人提心吊胆地望着北面通往肯德尔的路。殿下昨日方离此城，后卫天一亮也已开拔。有消息说坎伯兰公爵因法国人登陆已被召回，还有人说，这下苏格兰人要再折向南，一路掳掠直捣伦敦了。

这消息他并不相信，却鼓舞他加快了脚程。追兵方面想必遇到了某种顿挫，殿下说不定能不经一战便渡过边境。他想这该正是乔治勋爵的用意：他深知自己这支军队，只要能避免，便不肯拿它去打一场疲于奔命的防御战。因此行军的速度必然加快，他要赶上队伍，也必须催马。两人在兰开斯特只匆匆吃了一餐，便取道霍恩比路出发，打算兜一个圈子往肯德尔去——殿下当晚多半会驻在那里。

下午雾重，寒气砭骨，阿拉斯泰尔料要有雪，便催约翰逊赶在风暴之前加紧。可是雪迟迟未落，直到暮色四合，他们都在一层冰冷的雾霭里穿行于杂乱的丘陵之间。山岳又开始了，是他熟识的那些长满山草和石楠的冈峦；溪水在灰色的石缘深潭里打着旋，空气里有他自己故乡那种微酸、荒凉却令人振奋的味道。可如今他不欢迎它了，因为它是战败的信物。他是铩羽而归。不，他是把心埋在了那片他曾经轻视的温柔南国。一种狂野的渴望——乡愁的倒错——攫住了他，使他眷恋那烟霭迷蒙的褐色平野和青苔斑驳的林地，因为克劳迪娅这颗明珠，如今正供奉在那里。他原以为悔恨已永远撇清，自己已把眼睛铁石般转向一个寒冷的未来，却忘了自己还年轻。

===Original===

In the thick weather they came from the lanes into a broader high road, and suddenly found their progress stayed. A knot of troopers bade them halt, and unslung their muskets. They were fellows in green jackets, mounted on shaggy country horses, and they spoke with the accent of the Midlands. Alastair repeated his tale, and was informed that their orders were to let no man pass that road and to take any armed and mounted travellers before the General. He asked their regiment and was told that it was the Rangers, a corps of gentlemen volunteers. The men were cloddish but not unfriendly, and, suspecting that the corps was some raw levy of yokels commanded by some thickskulled squire, Alastair bowed to discretion and bade them show the way to the General’s quarters.

But the moorland farmhouse to which they were led awoke his doubts. The sentries had the trimness of a headquarters guard, and the horses he had a glimpse of in the yard were not the screws or carthorses of the ordinary yeoman. While they waited in the low-ceiled kitchen he had reached the conclusion that in the General he would find some regular officer of Wade’s or Cumberland’s command, and as he bowed his head to enter the parlour he had resolved on his line of conduct.

But he was not prepared for the sight of Oglethorpe; grim, aquiline, neat as a Sunday burgess, who raised his head from a mass of papers, stared for a second and then smiled.

===Chinese===

浓雾之中，他们从小巷转上一条宽阔的大道，忽觉去路被人挡住。一小队骑兵喝令他们站住，卸下火枪。那是些穿绿短上衣的家伙，骑着毛茸茸的乡间马，说话带英格兰中部的口音。阿拉斯泰尔把来历重述一遍，得到的答复是：上峰有令，此路不许任何人通过，凡带兵器骑马的旅人，一概解赴将军面前。他问他们是哪个团，答曰游骑队，一支由绅士们志愿组成的队伍。那些人土气却不恶。阿拉斯泰尔猜想这队伍不过是哪个榆木脑袋乡绅统领的一伙庄稼汉新募之众，便识时务地低了头，请他们引路去将军的驻所。

然而被引到的那座荒原农舍，却叫他起了疑。哨兵有司令部卫队的那股利落劲，院里惊鸿一瞥所见的那几匹马，也绝非寻常自耕农的驽马或拉车的重挽马。在低矮的厨房里等候时，他已断定，这位将军必是韦德或坎伯兰麾下的一名正规军官；及至低头跨进客厅，他已拿定了应答的方针。

然而他万没想到会看见奥格尔索普：严峻，鹰钩鼻，齐整得像做礼拜的市议员。他从一大堆文书里抬起头，盯着看了片刻，随即笑了。

===Original===

“You have brought me a friend, Roger,” he told the young lieutenant. “These gentlemen will be quartered here this night, for the weather is too thick to travel further; likewise they will sup with me.”

When the young man had gone, he held out his hand to Alastair.

“We seem fated to cross each other’s path, Mr. Maclean.”

===Chinese===

“罗杰，你给我带来了一位朋友，”他对那年轻的中尉说，“这两位先生今夜就宿在此地，这样的天气太浓，走不得远路；还有，他们要同我一起用晚饭。”

那年轻人走后，他向阿拉斯泰尔伸出手来。

“我们像是命里注定要狭路相逢，麦克莱恩先生。”

===Original===

“I would present to you my friend, Mr. Samuel Johnson, sir. This is General Oglethorpe.”

Johnson stared at him and then thrust forward a great hand.

“I am honoured, sir, deeply honoured. Every honest man has heard the name.” And he repeated:

> “One, driven by strong benevolence of soul,
 

 “Shall fly like Oglethorpe from pole to pole.”

The General smiled. “Mr. Pope was over-kind to my modest deserts. But, gentlemen, I am in command of a part of His Majesty’s forces, and at this moment we are in the region of war. I must request from you some account of your recent doings and your present purpose. Come forward to the fire, for it is wintry weather. And stay! Your Prince’s steward has been scouring the country for cherry brandy, to which it seems His Highness is partial. But all has not been taken.” He filled two glasses from a decanter at his elbow.

===Chinese===

“先生，请容我为你引见我的朋友塞缪尔·约翰逊先生。这位是奥格尔索普将军。”

约翰逊愣愣地看了看他，随即伸出一只大手。

“我深感荣幸，先生，荣幸之至。每一个正派人都听过这个姓氏。”接着他吟道：

> “有一位，受灵魂强烈的仁善所驱策，

> “将如奥格尔索普，从一极翱翔至另一极。”

将军笑了。“蒲柏先生对我微末的功劳未免过誉。不过，二位，我此刻统率着陛下的一部分军队，正值战区之内。我必须请二位交代一下近来的行止和如今的目的。到火边来吧，这是隆冬天气。且慢！你们王子殿下的管家刚才还在四乡搜购樱桃白兰地，殿下似乎偏好这一口。好在并没有全被搜走。”他从手边的细颈酒瓶里斟满两杯。

===Original===

Looking at the rugged face and the grave kindly eyes, Alastair resolved that it was a case for a full confession. He told of his doings at Brightwell after the meeting with Oglethorpe at the Sleeping Deer, and of the fate of Mr. Nicholas Kyd, but he made no mention of Sir John Norreys. He told of his ride to Derby, and what he had found on the Ashbourne road. It is possible that there was a break in his voice, for Oglethorpe averted his eyes and shook his head.

“I cannot profess to regret a failure which it is my duty to ensure,” he said, “but I can pity a brave man who sees his hopes destroyed. And now, sir? What course do you shape?”

“I must pursue the poor remains of my duty. I go to join my Prince.”

“And it is my business to prevent you!”

===Chinese===

望着那张粗犷的脸和那双庄重而慈和的眼睛，阿拉斯泰尔决意这回须得坦诚相告。他讲述了自睡鹿客栈与奥格尔索普相遇之后在布莱特韦尔的种种作为，以及尼古拉斯·基德先生的下场，只是绝口不提约翰·诺里斯爵士。他讲了德比之行，讲了阿什本大道上的所见。他说到某处，声音或许是哽了一哽，因为奥格尔索普把眼光掉了开去，摇了摇头。

“我分内之事就是要确保这场失败，所以我无法说不遗憾，”他说，“但我能怜悯一位眼见希望破灭的勇士。那么如今呢，先生？你航向何方？”

“我必须去尽我职分中可怜的残余。我要去投我的王子殿下。”

“而阻拦你正是我的职守！”

===Original===

Alastair looked at him composedly. “Nay, sir, I do not think that such can be your duty. It might be Cumberland’s or Wade’s, but not Oglethorpe’s, for you can understand another loyalty than your own, and I do not think you will interfere with mine. I ask only to go back to my own country. I will give you my word that I will not strike a blow in England.”

Again Oglethorpe smiled. “You read my heart with some confidence, sir. If I were to detain you, what would be the charge? You have not yet taken arms against His Majesty. Of your political doings I have no experience: to me you are a gentleman travelling to Scotland, who has on one occasion rendered good service to myself and so to His Majesty. That is all which, as a soldier, I am concerned to know. You will have quarters for the night, and tomorrow, if you desire it, continue your journey. But I must stipulate that the road you follow is not that of the Prince’s march. You will not join his army till it is north of Esk.”

Alastair bowed. “I am content.”

===Chinese===

阿拉斯泰尔神色自若地看着他。“不，先生，我想这不会是你的职守。它或许是坎伯兰的、韦德的，却不会是奥格尔索普的，因为你懂得世上有别于你自己的另一种忠诚，我想你也不会来妨害我的。我只求回到我的祖国。我向你担保，绝不在英格兰动手一击。”

奥格尔索普又笑了。“先生，你对我的心思揣摩得颇有把握。我若扣下你，罪名是什么呢？你还不曾对陛下兴兵。你的政治作为我无从指证：在我眼里，你是一位前往苏格兰的绅士，曾有一次于我本人、亦即于陛下，效过大力。作为一个军人，我要知道的仅此而已。今夜你有一处宿营，明天你若愿意，便可继续赶路。但我有一个条件：你走的路不得与王子殿下的行军路线相合。在他的军队北上渡过埃斯克河之前，你不得前去会合。”

阿拉斯泰尔躬身。“我遵命。”

===Original===

“But your friend,” Oglethorpe continued. “This Mr. Samuel Johnson who quotes so appositely the lines of Mr. Pope. He is an Englishman, and is in another case. I cannot permit Mr. Johnson to cross the Border.”

“He purposes to keep me company,” said Alastair, “till I have joined the Prince.”

“Nay, sir,” cried Johnson. “You have been honest with us, and I will be honest with you. My desire is to join the Prince and fight by my friend’s side.”

Oglethorpe looked at the strange figure, below the skirts of whose old brown coat peeped a scabbard. “You seem,” he said, “to have fulfilled the scriptural injunction ‘He that hath no sword, let him sell his garment and buy one.’ But, sir, it may not be. I would not part two friends before it is necessary, but you will give me your parole that you will not enter Scotland, or I must hold you prisoner and send you to Manchester.”

Johnson turned to Alastair and put a hand on his shoulder.

“It seems that Providence is on your side, my friend, and has intervened to separate us. That was your counsel, but it was never mine.﻿ ﻿… So be it, then.” He walked to the window and seemed to be in trouble with his dingy cravat.

===Chinese===

“可你这位朋友，”奥格尔索普接着说，“这位引蒲柏先生的诗句引得如此贴切的塞缪尔·约翰逊先生。他是英格兰人，情形便不同了。我不能准许约翰逊先生越过边境。”

“他打算陪我同行，”阿拉斯泰尔说，“直到我与王子殿下会合。”

“非也，先生，”约翰逊喊道，“你待我们以诚，我也须待你以诚。我的愿望正是去投王子殿下，在我朋友身旁作战。”

奥格尔索普打量着这个古怪的人物，他那件褐色旧外衣的衣裾底下，露出半截剑鞘。“看来，”他说，“你已经奉行了圣书的训诫：‘没有刀的，要卖衣服买刀。’可是，先生，这使不得。我不想在必要之前拆散一对朋友，但你要向我立下假释，担保不踏入苏格兰，否则我便只好扣押你，解送曼彻斯特。”

约翰逊转向阿拉斯泰尔，把一只手搭上他的肩头。

“看来天意站在你那边，我的朋友，出手来拆散我们了。那是你的主张，从来不是我的。……那么，就这样吧。”他走到窗前，似乎正同他那条晦暗的领巾过不去。

---

===Original===

Next morning when Oglethorpe’s Rangers began their march towards Shap, the two travellers set out by an easterly road, forded the Lune and made for the Eden valley. The rains filled the streams and mosses, and their progress was slow, so that for days they were entangled among the high Cumbrian hills. News of the affair at Clifton, where Lord George beat off Cumberland’s van and saved the retreat, came to them by a packman in a herd’s sheiling on Cross Fell, and after that their journey was clear down the Eden, till the time came to avoid Carlisle and make straight across country for Esk. The last night they lay at an alehouse on the Lyneside, and Alastair counted thirty guineas from his purse.

“With this I think you may reach London,” he told Johnson, and when the latter expostulated, he bade him consider it a loan. “If I fall, it is my bequest to you; and if I live, then we shall assuredly meet again and you can repay me. I would fain make it more, but money is likely to be a scarce commodity in yonder army.”

“You have a duty clear before you,” said the other dismally. “For me, I have none such; I would I had. But I will seek no opiates in a life of barbarism. I am resolved to spend what days the Almighty may still allot me on the broad highway of humanity. When I have found my task I will adhere to it like a soldier.”

===Chinese===

次日清晨，奥格尔索普的游骑队开拔往沙普去，两个旅人则取道向东，涉过卢恩河，向伊登谷行去。雨水灌满了溪流和沼泽，行程迟缓，一连数日他们都在坎布里亚的高山之间辗转。克利夫顿一役的消息——乔治勋爵在那里击退坎伯兰的前锋、保住了撤退之路——是由一个货郎在克罗斯费尔山上一间牧人的夏牧棚里说给他们听的；此后沿伊登河顺流而下，一路通畅，直到该避开卡莱尔、取直路横穿乡野奔埃斯克河的时候。最后一夜，他们宿在莱恩河畔一家麦酒店里，阿拉斯泰尔从钱袋里点出三十几尼。

“有这些钱，我想你到得了伦敦，”他对约翰逊说。对方还要推辞，他便叫他只当这是一笔借贷。“我若阵亡，这是我留给你的遗赠；我若活着，我们必定后会有期，你再还我不迟。我本想多给些，只是在那边的军队里，钱恐怕要成稀罕物了。”

“你面前摆着一条清清楚楚的本分，”对方郁郁地说，“我呢，却没有这样的本分；但愿我有。可我不愿到野蛮人的生涯里去寻麻醉自己的鸦片。我已拿定主意，全能者余下派给我的岁月，我要消磨在人性的康庄大道上。一旦寻得我的职事，我便要像个士兵一样坚守不渝。”

===Original===

Next morning they rode to a ridge beneath which the swollen Esk poured through the haughlands. It was a day of flying squalls, and the great dales of Esk and Annan lay mottled with sun-gleams and purple shadows up to the dark hills, which, chequered with snow, defended the way to the north. Further down Alastair’s quick eye noted a commotion on the river banks, and dark objects bobbing in the stream.

“See,” he cried, “His Highness is crossing. We have steered skilfully, for I enter Scotland by his side.”

“Is that Scotland?” Johnson asked, his shortsighted eyes peering at the wide vista.

“Scotland it is, and somewhere over yon hills lies Ramoth-Gilead.”

===Chinese===

次日清晨，他们骑上一道山脊，涨水的埃斯克河在脊下的河漫滩间奔腾。那是一阵一阵急雨飞掠的日子，埃斯克与安南两大河谷，阳光与紫影斑驳相间，一直铺到黑沉沉的山前——那些山上覆着棋盘般的残雪，扼守住北去的通路。再往下游，阿拉斯泰尔敏锐的眼睛望见两岸人马骚动，黑色的身影在河水中起伏。

“看哪，”他喊道，“殿下正在渡河。我们把舵把得多巧，我恰在他身旁踏进苏格兰。”

“那就是苏格兰么？”约翰逊问道，近视的眼睛竭力望着那片辽阔的景色。

“正是苏格兰。那边的山后某处，便是基列的拉末。”

===Original===

Alastair’s mind had in these last days won a certain peace, and now at the sight of the army something quickened in him that had been dead since the morning on the Ashbourne road. Youth was waking from its winter sleep. The world had become coloured again, barriers were down, roads ran into the future. Hazard seemed only hazard now and not despair. Suddenly came the sound of wild music, as the pipers struck up the air of “Bundle and Go.” The strain rose far and faint and elfin, like a wandering wind, and put fire into his veins.

“That is the march for the road,” Alastair cried. “Now I am for my own country.”

“And I for mine,” said Johnson, but there was no spring in his voice. He rubbed his eyes, peered in the direction of the music, and made as if to unbuckle his sword. Then he thought better of it. “Nay, I will keep the thing to nurse my memory,” he said.

The two men joined hands; and Alastair, in his foreign fashion, kissed the other on the cheek. As they mounted, a shower enveloped them, and the landscape was blotted out, so that the two were isolated in a world of their own.

“We are naked men,” said Johnson. “Each must go up to his own Ramoth-Gilead, but I would that yours and mine had been the same.”

Then he turned his horse and rode slowly southward into the rain.

===Chinese===

这些天来，阿拉斯泰尔的心已赢得某种平静，此刻一见大军，某种自阿什本大道那个早晨以来死去了的东西，在他体内复活过来。青春正从冬眠中醒来。世界重又有了色彩，藩篱倒了，条条道路通向未来。险境如今只是险境，不再是绝望。忽然响起野性的乐声——风笛手们吹起了《捆好行装走》（Bundle and Go）的曲调。那乐声远远升起，细微飘渺如精灵，像一缕游荡的风，把火注入了他的血脉。

“这正是赶路的进行曲，”阿拉斯泰尔喊道，“这下我要回我自己的祖国了。”

“我也回我自己的祖国。”约翰逊说，可他嗓音里没有一点轻快。他揉揉眼睛，朝乐声传来的方向望去，做出要解下佩剑的样子，随即又改了主意。“不，这东西我要留着，喂养我的记忆。”他说。

两人执手相握；阿拉斯泰尔又以他那外国人的做派，吻了吻对方的面颊。上马之际，一阵骤雨将他们裹住，四野的景色尽数抹去，两个人便被隔在了一个只属于他们自己的世界里。

“我们是赤身人哪，”约翰逊说，“人人都得登自己的基列的拉末，只愿你的和我的，原是同一座就好了。”

说罢他拨转马头，缓缓向南，走进雨里去了。

## X Snowbound at the Sleeping Deer / 十、雪困睡鹿客栈
===Original===
The whole staff of the Sleeping Deer were around the door when my lady Norreys, making dainty grimaces at the weather, tripped over the yards of snow-powdered cobbles between the step of her coach and the comfortable warmth of the inn. The landlord, ill-favoured and old, was there with his bow, and the landlady, handsome and not yet forty, with her curtsey, and in the gallery which ran round the stone-flagged hall the chambermaid tribe of Dollys and Peggys clustered to regard the newcomer, for pretty young ladies of quality did not lie every night at a moorland hostelry. But the lady would not tarry to warm her toes by the great fire or to taste the landlady’s cordials. A fire had been bespoke in her bedchamber and there she retired to drink tea, which her woman, Mrs. Peckover, made with the secret airs of a plotter in the sanctum beside the bar. The two servants from Weston attended the coach in the innyard. Mr. Edom Lowrie comforted himself with a pot of warm ale, while Mr. Samuel Johnson, finding a good fire in the parlour, removed his shoes, and toasted at the ribs his great worsted stocking soles.

Twenty minutes later, when the bustle had subsided, two unassuming travellers appeared below the signboard on which might be seen the fresh-painted gaudy lineaments of a couching fallow deer. The snow was now falling thick, and the wind had risen so that the air was one wild scurry and smother. Midwinter marched straight for the sanctum, and finding it empty but for Mrs. Peckover, continued down a narrow passage, smelling of onions, to a little room which he entered unbidden. There sat the landlord with horn spectacles on his nose, making a splice of a trout rod. At the sight of Midwinter he stood to attention, letting all his paraphernalia of twine and wax and tweezers slip to the floor.

“I have brought a friend,” said Midwinter. “See that you entreat him well and do his biddings as if they were my own. For myself I want a horse, friend Tappet, for snow or no I must sleep in the next shire.”

===Chinese===
诺里斯夫人驾到时，睡鹿客栈（the Sleeping Deer）阖店人等已聚在门边；夫人对着这般天气做出娇怯的怪相，提裙碎步，踏过马车踏板与客栈暖意之间那几码薄雪轻覆的鹅卵石。相貌寒碜的老店主持躬相迎，姿容端正、未满四十的老板娘屈膝为礼；环着石板大厅的回廊上，多莉、佩姬一族（Dollys and Peggys）的客房侍女们挤作一团，争睹这位新客——有身份的年轻漂亮小姐，可不是夜夜都会光临荒原客栈的。但夫人不肯停留，既不去大火炉边暖暖脚，也不尝老板娘奉上的甜酒。她的卧室早已吩咐生好炉火，她便径自回房用茶——茶是她的贴身女仆佩克欧弗太太（Mrs. Peckover）在账台旁的小内间里沏的，那副神秘做派，俨然一个密谋党人。从韦斯顿跟来的两名仆人在客栈院子里照看马车。以东·洛里先生捧着一壶热麦酒自得其乐，塞缪尔·约翰逊先生则在客厅里寻得一炉好火，脱下鞋来，把那双大号的毛线袜底凑在炉条火间烘烤。

二十分钟后，喧腾平息下来，两个不起眼的行客出现在招牌底下——招牌上新漆着一只蜷卧黇鹿的浓艳轮廓。此刻雪已下得浓密，风也刮了起来，满天是一片狂乱的纷飞迷蒙。仲冬径直朝那小内间走去，见里头只有佩克欧弗太太一人，便继续沿一条弥漫着洋葱味的窄过道往前，径入一间小屋，也不通传。店主正坐在里面，鼻上架着角质眼镜，给一杆鳟鱼钓竿做拼接。一见仲冬，他霍然立正，麻线、蜡块、小镊子等一应家什尽数滑落在地。

「我带来一位朋友，」仲冬说。「务必好生款待，他的吩咐，你就当是我的吩咐。至于我自己，我要一匹马，朋友塔佩特——下雪也罢，不下也罢，今夜我必须睡在邻郡。」

===Original===
So as Alastair was changing into his own clothes, which the landlord fetched for him from Edom, he saw from his window in the last faint daylight a square cloakless figure swing from the yard at a canter and turn south with the gale behind it.

The young man had now secured all his belongings, some having come with Edom by grace of the charcoal-burner and the rest from Squire Thicknesse’s manor in the lady’s charge. As he dressed, his mind was busy on his old problem, and he had sadly to confess that though he had covered much country in recent days he had got little new light. More than once he had tried to set Midwinter’s mind to work on it, but, beyond his advice to come to Brightwell, he had shown no interest. Why should he, Alastair reflected, since his creed forswore all common loyalties? But as he had plodded up and down the foothills that day his thoughts had been running chiefly on the lady’s husband whom she believed to be now with the Prince, but who most certainly was, or was about to be, in the vicinity of Brightwell. For what purpose? To receive a letter from Edom﻿—a continuing correspondence, sent by Kyd, and charged with the most desperate import to the Prince﻿—a correspondence which should be without delay in the Prince’s hands. What did Sir John Norreys in the business? Why did Kyd send the letters by Brightwell, which was not the nearest road to Lancashire?

As he came downstairs he noticed a map hanging on a panel between prints of the new gardens at Chatsworth and the old Marquis of Granby. It was a Dutch thing, drawn by Timothy Hooge a hundred years before, and it showed all the southern part of the Peak country, with fragments of Yorkshire, Notts and Staffordshire adjoining. It was hard to read, for it had been pasted on a wooden board and then highly varnished, but the main roads were strongly marked in a purplish red. He saw the road from the northwest descend the valleys to Derby and so to London, the road from Manchester and Lancashire which the Prince’s army would travel. With some trouble he found Brightwell and to his surprise saw the road which passed it marked with equal vigour, as if it vied with the other in importance. A moment’s reflection told him the reason. It was the main way from the West. By this road must come the levies from Wales if they were to join the Prince before he reached Derby and the flat country. By this road, too, must all messages come from West England so soon as the army left Manchester. More, the Hanoverian forces were gathering in Nottinghamshire. If they sought to cut in in the Prince’s rear they would march this way.﻿ ﻿… Brightwell was suddenly revealed as a point of strategy, a ganglion; if treachery were abroad, here it would roost.

===Chinese===
于是，趁阿拉斯泰尔换回自己衣裳的当口——衣裳是店主从以东处取来的——他从窗口望见，在最后一线微弱的天光里，一个方正面阔、不披外氅的身影跃马出院，催马小跑，借着身后狂风之势转向南去。

此时年轻人已把自己的一应行囊尽数收拢：一部分是承烧炭人之便随以东带来的，其余的则由夫人作主，从西克尼斯乡绅的庄园取回。他一面穿衣，心里一面盘算着那桩老难题，却不得不黯然承认：连日来他跑过多少乡土，换来的新光亮却少得可怜。他不止一次想请仲冬动动心思，可除了劝他到布莱特韦尔去，那人对此毫无兴致。也难怪，阿拉斯泰尔暗想，他的信条本就弃绝一切寻常的忠义。不过，那天他在山麓间上上下下跋涉时，想得最多的还是夫人的丈夫——夫人以为他如今在王子身边，可他分明已经、或即将身在布莱特韦尔左近。所为何来？为的是收以东递来的一封信——那是一环接一环的通信，由基德寄发，字字关乎王子最险恶的要害——这些信件本该刻不容缓地送到王子手中才是。约翰·诺里斯爵士在这桩事里做何营生？基德为何要绕道布莱特韦尔递信？那可不是去兰开夏最近的路径。

下楼时，他注意到壁板上一幅地图，挂在查茨沃斯新园囿与老格兰比侯爵两幅版画之间。那是荷兰人的手笔，出自百年前的蒂莫西·胡赫（Timothy Hooge）所绘，画尽了皮克地区南部全境，连带毗邻的约克郡、诺茨与斯塔福德郡的边角。图不易辨读——它裱在木板上，又罩了厚厚的清漆——但几条大道都用紫红色重重描出。他看见西北来的大道沿山谷下行，通到德比，再通往伦敦；那是从曼彻斯特与兰开夏来的路，王子的大军将取道于此。他费了些力气找到布莱特韦尔，惊讶地发现经过那里的道路描得同样遒劲，仿佛要与前者竞比轻重。略一思索，他便明白了其中缘由。那是西部来的干道。威尔士的募兵若要赶在王子抵达德比和平坦乡野之前与之会师，必走此路。王子的大军一旦离开曼彻斯特，西英格兰来的所有消息也必经此路传来。更有甚者，汉诺威军正在诺丁汉郡集结。他们若想抄王子的后路，行军必取此道。……布莱特韦尔蓦然显形为一处战略要点，一处神经中枢；若有奸宄出没，必栖于此。
===Original===
He walked into the kitchen, for he had an odd fancy about the horseman whom he had seen ride away a little before Lady Norreys’ arrival﻿—an incredible suspicion which he wished to lay. A kitchen wench was busy at the fire, and on a settle a stableman sat drinking beer while a second stamped the snow from his boots at the back door. The appearance of a dapper gentleman in buckled shoes and a well-powdered wig so startled the beer-drinker that he spilled half his mug on the floor. Alastair ordered fresh supplies for all three and drank his on the seat beside the others. Had they been in the yard all afternoon? They had, and had prophesied snow since before breakfast, though Master wouldn’t have it so and had sent the wagons to Marlock, where they would be storm-stayed.﻿ ﻿… Yes. A rider had come down the valley and had put up his horse for the better part of an hour. He had been indoors most of the time﻿—couldn’t say why. A tall fellow, Bill said. No, not very powerful﻿—lean shoulders﻿—pale face﻿—big nose. Young, too﻿—Tom reckoned not more than twenty-five.﻿ ﻿… Alastair left them with an easier mind, for the worst of his suspicions had been disproved. The back he had seen from the ridge-top posting up the dale had had a disquieting resemblance to Kyd’s.

In the parlour he found Mr. Johnson stretching his great bulk before a leaping fire and expanding in the warmth of it. The windows had not been shuttered, so the wild night was in visible contrast to the snug hearth. A small girl of five or six years, the landlady’s child, had strayed into the room, and, fascinated by a strange gentleman, had remained to talk. She now sat on one of Johnson’s bony knees, while he told her a fairy tale in a portentous hollow voice. He told of a dragon, a virtuous dragon in reality a prince, who lived in a Derbyshire cave, and of how the little girl stumbled on the cave, found the dragon, realised his true character, and lived with him for a year and a day, which was the prescribed magical time if he were to be a prince again. He was just describing the tiny bed she had in the rock opposite the dragon’s lair, which lair was like a dry millpond, and the child was punctuating the narrative with squeals of excitement, when Alastair entered. Thereupon the narrator became self-conscious, the story hastened to a lame conclusion, and the small girl climbed from his knee and with many backward glances sidled out of the room.

“You find me childishly employed, sir,” said Johnson, “but I dearly love a little miss and I think my company has charms for them. I rejoiced to hear from the Scotch serving-man, who by the way is a worthy fellow, that you were expected to meet us at this place. We are fortunate in winning here thus early, for presently the snow will so conglobulate that the road will be impossible for coach and horses.﻿ ﻿… You have not yet dined, sir? No more have I or the Scotchman, and my lady has retired to her chamber. Our hostess promised that the meal should not be long delayed, and I have bidden the Scotchman to share it, for though his condition is humble he has becoming manners and a just mind. I do not defend the sitting down of servants and masters as a quotidian occurrence, but customs abate their rigidity on a journey.”

===Chinese===
他踱进厨房，心里惦着那个骑马人——就在诺里斯夫人到达前不久，他望见那人驰马而去——他起了一个古怪的念头，一桩难以置信的疑心，想把它放下。一个厨下丫头正忙着照看炉火，高背长凳上坐着个马夫在喝啤酒，另一个正在后门口跺掉靴上的雪。这位穿带扣鞋、敷粉假发一丝不苟的利落绅士一露面，喝啤酒的那个吓了一跳，半杯酒泼在了地上。阿拉斯泰尔给三人都要了新的，挨着他们在长凳上坐下同饮。他们整个下午都在院子里么？正是，而且早饭前就预言要下雪，只是东家不肯依，把大车都打发去了马洛克（Marlock），这一去准得让风雪困在半道。……是啊。有个骑马的顺着山谷下来，把马寄放了足足大半个钟头。人大半时候都待在屋里——说不上为什么。是个高个子，比尔说。不，不算壮实——肩膀瘦削——脸白白的——鼻子大。人也年轻——汤姆估摸不超过二十五岁。……阿拉斯泰尔心里松快了些，离开他们，因为他最坏的疑心已被推翻。他从山脊顶上望见的那策马沿谷而上的背影，与基德的背影像得让人不安。

在客厅里，他见到约翰逊先生正对着腾腾炉火舒展那庞大的身躯，在暖意里洋洋舒张。窗子还没上窗板，于是狂野的夜色与融融的炉边恰成看得见的对照。一个五六岁的小姑娘——老板娘的孩子——误走进了这间屋子，被这位陌生的绅士迷住了，便留下来攀谈。此刻她正坐在约翰逊那瘦骨嶙峋的一侧膝头上，听他用一种郑重其事的瓮声瓮气讲童话。他讲一条龙，一条有德的龙——其实是位王子——住在德比郡一处山洞里；讲小姑娘如何误撞进洞，遇见了龙，认出了他的真身，又如何同他一起住了一年零一天——若要他重新变回王子，法术规定的时限正是如此。他正说到她在龙窟对面的岩壁里有了一张小床——那龙窟好比一口干涸的磨坊池——孩子兴奋的尖叫不时给故事打着拍子，阿拉斯泰尔就在这时走了进来。于是讲故事的人顿时局促起来，故事匆匆落了个蹩脚的收场，小姑娘从他膝上爬下来，一步三回头地蹭出了房间。

「先生，您见我干着孩子气的事了，」约翰逊说，「可我打心眼里喜欢小姑娘，而且我想，我这陪伴对她们自有其魅力。听那位苏格兰仆人说您将在此地与我们相会，我真是一喜——顺便说一句，他是个正派人。我们运气好，到得这样早，因为不多时雪便会如此凝团成块，道路将不容车马通行了。……先生，您还没用晚餐吧？我同那苏格兰人也都没有，夫人已回房歇息了。老板娘答应饭不会耽搁多久，我已请那苏格兰人同席——他身份虽微，举止却得体，心地也公正。主仆同席若算日日惯例，我并不辩护；只是旅途之上，礼俗自会放宽它的刚性。」

===Original===
To Johnson’s delight a maid entered at that moment for the purpose of laying the table. She lit a half-dozen of candles, and closed and barred the heavy shutters so that the only evidence of the storm that remained was the shaking of the window frames, the rumbling in the chimney and the constant fine hissing at the back of the fire where the snow descended. This distant reminder gave an edge to the delicate comfort of the place, and as fragrant odours were wafted from the kitchen through the open door Johnson’s spirits rose and his dull eyes brightened like children’s at the sight of sweets.

“Of all the good gifts of a beneficent Providence to men,” he cried, “I think that none excels a well-appointed inn, and I call it a gift, for our fallible mortal nature is not capable unaided of devising so rare a thing. Behold me, Captain Maclean. My wealth is less than a crown and, unless I beg my way, I see not how I can return to Chastlecote. I am dependent upon my dear young lady for the expense of this journey, which she chose to command. Therefore I do not feel justified in ordering what my fancy dictates. Yet so strongly am I delighted by this place that I propose to spend this my last crown on a bowl of bishop to supplement the coming meal, which from its odour should be worthy of it. Like Ariadne in her desertion I find help in Bacchus.”

“Nay, sir, I am the host,” said Alastair. “Last night I slept by a tinkler’s fire and dined off a tinkler’s stew. Tonight we shall have the best the house affords. The food, I take it, is at the discretion of the landlady, but the wine shall be at yours.”

“Oh brave we!” cried Johnson. “Let us have in the landlord forthwith, for, Captain Maclean, sir, I would be indeed a churl if I scrupled to assent to your good fellowship.”

===Chinese===
令约翰逊欢喜的是，恰在此时一个女仆进来摆桌开饭。她点起半打蜡烛，关上并闩好厚重的窗板，于是风暴留存的唯一凭据，只剩窗框的震颤、烟囱里的轰鸣，还有炉膛后部雪花落进处那一阵持续而细微的嘶嘶声。这遥远的提醒，反倒给此地的精致舒适添了一分锋芒；香气从厨房穿过敞开的门飘送过来，约翰逊兴致高涨，那双平日晦暗的眼睛亮了起来，像孩子们见了糖果。

「仁慈的天意赐给世人的种种美物之中，」他高声道，「我想没有一样胜过一家陈设妥帖的客栈；我称之为天赐，因为我们这易谬的凡人天性，若无外助，断然造不出如此妙品。您瞧我，麦克莱恩上尉。我的全部家财不足一枚克朗，除非一路行乞，我看不出自己如何回得了查斯特科特。这趟行程的花销，全仰仗我亲爱的小姐——是她执意要走的。因此，任性点菜，我自觉无此资格。然而此地让我心花怒放，竟至决意把这最后一枚克朗花掉，换一盅主教酒（bishop），给即将上桌的晚餐助兴——这顿饭单凭香气，就配得上这酒。正如遭弃的阿里阿德涅（Ariadne），我在巴克科斯（Bacchus）处寻得了救助。」

「不，先生，做东的是我，」阿拉斯泰尔说。「昨夜我睡在补锅匠的火边，吃的是补锅匠的炖菜。今晚咱们要这家客栈拿得出的最好的。饭菜嘛，我想由老板娘做主，酒却归您做主。」

「妙极，痛快！」约翰逊叫道。「快把店主叫进来，因为，麦克莱恩上尉先生，若对您的这番情谊还要忸怩推却，我真是天下第一鄙夫了。」
===Original===
He rang the bell violently and, when the landlord was fetched, entered upon a learned disquisition on wines, with the well-thumbed cellar-book of the inn as his text. “Claret we shall not drink, though our host recommends his binns and it is the favourite drink of gentlemen in your country, sir. In winter weather it is too thin, and, even when well warmed, too cold. Nay, at its best it is but a liquor for boys.”

“And for men?” Alastair asked.

“For men port, and for heroes brandy.”

“Then brandy be it.”

“Nay, sir,” he said solemnly. “Brandy on the unheroic, such as I confess myself to be, produces too soon and certainly the effect of drunkenness. Drunkenness I love not, for I am a man accustomed to self-examination, and I am conscious when I am drunk, and that consciousness is painful. Others know not when they are drunk or sober. I know a man, a very worthy bookseller, who is so habitually and equally drunk that even his intimates cannot perceive that he is more sober at one time than another. Besides, my dear lady may summon us to a hand at cartes or to drink tea with her.”

===Chinese===
他猛力摇铃，店主唤到之后，他便以客栈那本翻得起了毛边的酒窖账簿为经文，发表起一篇关于酒的渊博宏论。「红葡萄酒（Claret）我们就不喝了，虽然店主东夸他的窖藏，而那也是贵国绅士们最喜爱的杯中之物，先生。冬季天时，它太单薄；即便烫得滚热，也太寒凉。不，它登峰造极，也不过是一种男孩儿喝的酒。」

「那么男人喝什么？」阿拉斯泰尔问。

「男人喝波特酒，英雄喝白兰地。」

「那就白兰地吧。」

「不，先生，」他肃然道。「白兰地之于非英雄之辈——我自认便是此辈——太快也太必然地生出醉意。醉态我是不爱的，因为我是惯于自省的人，我醉时心里明白，而那份明白是痛苦的。旁人醉了还是醒着，自己并不知道。我认得一个人，一位非常可敬的书商，他醉得那样恒常而均匀，连至交也看不出他何时光醒些、何时更醉些。再者，我们亲爱的小姐说不定要召我们去打几局牌，或是同她用茶。」

===Original===
Eventually he ordered a bottle of port, one of old madeira and one of brown sherry, that he might try all three before deciding by which he should abide. Presently Edom was summoned, and on his heels came dinner. It proved to be an excellent meal to which Mr. Johnson applied himself with a serious resolution. There was thick hare soup, with all the woods and pastures in its fragrance, and a big dressed pike, caught that morning in the inn stew-pond. This the two Scots did not touch, but Mr. Johnson ate of it largely, using his fingers, because, as he said, he was shortsighted and afraid of bones. Then came roast hill mutton, which he highly commended. “Yesterday,” he declared, “we also dined upon mutton﻿—mutton ill-fed, ill-killed, ill-kept and ill-dressed. This is as nutty as venison.” But he reserved his highest commendations for a veal pie, made with plums, which he averred was his favourite delicacy. With the cheese and wheaten cakes which followed he sampled the three bottles and decided for the port. Alastair and Edom were by comparison spare eaters, and had watched with admiration the gallant trencher-work of their companion. For liquor they drank a light rum punch of Alastair’s compounding, while Mr. Johnson consumed, in addition to divers glasses of sherry and Madeira, two bottles of rich dark port, dropping a lump of sugar into each glass and stirring it with the butt of a fork.

And all the while he talked, wisely, shrewdly, truculently, and with a gusto comparable to that which he displayed in the business of eating.

“You slept hard last night?” he asked of Alastair. “How came you here?”

===Chinese===
最后他点了一瓶波特、一瓶陈年马德拉、一瓶褐色雪利，好在定于一尊之前把三样都试上一试。不多时以东被唤了来，晚餐紧跟着他上了桌。这顿饭果然精美，约翰逊先生以严肃的决心向它进击。有浓浓的野兔汤，香气里尽是树林与牧场；有一条烹调停当的大狗鱼，是当天早晨从客栈鱼塘里捕的。这道鱼两个苏格兰人都没动，约翰逊先生却大快朵颐，而且下手去抓，因为据他说，他眼神不济，怕鱼刺。接着上来烤山羊肉，他交口称赏。「昨日，」他宣称，「我们吃的也是羊肉——喂得不好、宰得不好、存得不好、烹得不好的羊肉。今日这肉香醇得赛过鹿肉。」但他最高的赞誉，留给了一张加李子的小牛肉馅饼，他断言那是他平生最爱的美味。随后的干酪与麦饼上来时，他把三瓶酒都品了一遍，敲定了波特。相形之下，阿拉斯泰尔和以东吃得节省，只带着钦佩旁观同伴那豪迈的饕餮功夫。喝的酒，是阿拉斯泰尔亲手调的一味淡朗姆潘趣；约翰逊先生则在若干杯雪利与马德拉之外，又饮尽了两瓶浓黑的红波特，每杯丢进一块方糖，用叉柄搅上一搅。

而这中间他一直说着话——睿智、精明、峻切，那股兴头，同他吃饭时的劲头不相上下。

「昨夜睡得艰苦吧？」他问阿拉斯泰尔。「您怎么到这里的？」
===Original===
“On foot. For ten days I have been in an older world with a man who is a kind of king there.” He spoke for a little of Midwinter, but Johnson was unimpressed.

“I think I have heard these boasts before, sir. When a man decries civility and exalts barbarism, it is because he is ill fitted to excel in good society. So when one praises rusticity it is because he is denied the joys of town. A man may be tired of the country, but when he is tired of London he is tired of life.”

“Yet the taste can be defended,” said Alastair. “A lover of natural beauty will be impatient of too long a sojourn in town, and if he would indulge his fancy he must leave the highway.”

Mr. Johnson raised his head and puffed out his cheeks.

“No, sir, I do not assent to this fashionable cant of natural beauty, nor will I rave like a green girl over scenery. One part of the earth is very much as another to me, provided it support life. The most beautiful garden is that which produces most fruits, and the fairest stream that which is fullest of fish. As for mountains﻿—”

===Chinese===
「徒步来的。这十天里，我一直待在一个更古老的世界里，同一个人在一起——在那世界里，他形同王者。」他略略说了些仲冬的事，约翰逊却不为所动。

「先生，这类自夸我想早有所闻。一个人贬抑文雅、推崇野蛮，必是因为他在上流社会里难占鳌头。同样，一个人称颂田园，必是因为城市的欢乐与他无缘。人可以厌倦乡村，可谁若厌倦了伦敦，他便是厌倦了人生。」

「然而这趣味也未尝不可辩护，」阿拉斯泰尔说。「钟爱自然之美的人，在城里住得太久便要心浮气躁；他要纵容这份痴念，就得离开大道。」

约翰逊先生昂起头，鼓起了腮帮。

「不，先生，这种自然之美的时髦滥调我不附和，我也绝不会像黄毛丫头似的对着风景发痴。世上这一处地方与那一处，在我眼里并无二致，只要它能养人。最美的园子是结果最多的园子，最好的河是鱼最满的河。至于山——」

===Original===
The food and the wine had flushed Mr. Johnson’s face, and his uncouth gestures had become more violent. Now with a wheel of his right hand he swept two glasses to the floor and narrowly missed Edom’s head.

“Mountains!” he cried, “I deny any grandeur in the spectacle. There is more emotion for me in a furlong of Cheapside than in the contemplation of mere elevated bodies.”

Edom, with an eye on the port, was whispering to Alastair that they would soon be contemplating another elevated body, when there came a knocking and the landlady entered.

“Her ladyship’s services to you, sirs,” she announced, “and she expects Mr. Johnson to wait upon her after the next half-hour, and she begs him to bring also the gentleman recently arrived with whom she believes she has the honour of an acquaintance.” The landlady, having got the message by heart, delivered it with the speed and monotony of a bellman. Mr. Johnson rose to his feet and bowed.

“Our service to my lady,” he said, “and we will obey her commands. *Our* service, mark you,” and he inclined towards Alastair. The summons seemed to have turned his thoughts from wine, for he refused the bottle when it was passed to him.

===Chinese===
酒足饭饱，约翰逊先生的脸红了，他那粗豪的手势也愈发猛烈起来。此刻他右手一抡，把两只杯子扫落在地，险些扫中以东的脑袋。

「山！」他叫道，「我不承认这景象里有什么壮美。奇普赛德街一弗隆的路，给我的感动也比冥想这些区区隆起之物来得多。」

以东眼睛盯着那波特酒，正凑在阿拉斯泰尔耳边说，用不了多久，他们就要瞻仰另一具隆起之物了——这时有人敲门，老板娘走了进来。

「二位先生，夫人向你们致意，」她传话道，「她请约翰逊先生过半个钟头后到她房里去，还恳请他把新到的这位绅士一并带去——她相信她有缘与这位绅士相识。」老板娘把话背得烂熟，用更夫报时般的飞快与单调一口气说完。约翰逊先生起身一躬。

「向夫人致我们的敬意，」他说，「我们谨遵她的吩咐。注意，是我们（*Our*）的敬意。」他朝阿拉斯泰尔俯了俯身。这一声传召似乎把他的心思从酒上转开了，酒瓶递过来时，他竟辞谢了。
===Original===
“The dear child is refreshed, it would seem,” he said. “She found this morning’s journey irksome, for she has little patience. Reading she cannot abide, and besides the light was poor.”

“Is madam possessed of many accomplishments?” Alastair asked, because it was clear that the other expected him to speak on the subject.

“Why no, sir. It is not right for a gentlewoman to be trained like a performing ape. Adventitious accomplishments may be possessed by any rank, but one can always distinguish the born gentlewoman.”

Then he repented.

“But I would not have you think that she is of dull wits. Nay, she is the most qualitied lady I have ever seen. She has an admirable quick mind which she puts honestly to yours. I have had rare discussions with her. Reflect, sir; she has lived always in the broad sunshine of life, and has had no spur to form her wits save her own fancy. A good mind in such a one is a greater credit than with those who are witty for a livelihood. ’Twill serve her well in matrimony, for no woman is the worse for sense and knowledge. For the present, being not three weeks married, her mind is in a happy confusion.”

===Chinese===
「亲爱的孩子看来歇过劲儿来了，」他说。「今晨的行程让她腻烦，因为她没什么耐性。读书她是断然受不住的，再说车里光线又差。」

「夫人多有才艺么？」阿拉斯泰尔问，因为对方显然等着他就这个话题开口。

「并不多，先生。淑女不该被调教得像只会把戏的猿猴。附庸得来的才艺，哪个阶层的人都可能有，可天生的淑女，人家一望便知。」

说着他又后悔了。

「可我不要您以为她资质愚钝。不，她是我平生所见最富才具的女子。她有一副令人钦佩的敏悟心思，与人交锋从不藏奸。我与她有过稀世的妙谈。想想吧，先生；她一直活在人生的朗朗晴日里，除了自己的痴念，别无马刺来砥砺心智。这样一个人的好头脑，比那些靠机智谋生之人的更可称道。日后于婚娶必有大用，因为女子有见识有学问，只有好处，绝无坏处。眼下呢，成婚还不满三礼拜，她的心思正处在一场欢天喜地的迷乱里。」

===Original===
He smiled tenderly as he spoke, like a father speaking of a child.

“She is happy, I think,” he said, and repeated the phrase three times. “You have seen her,” he turned to Alastair. “You can confirm my belief that she is happy?”

“She is most deeply in love,” was the reply.

“And transmutes it into happiness,” said Johnson, and repeated with a rolling voice some lines of poetry, beating time with his hand,

> “Love various minds does variously inspire;
 

 It stirs in gentle bosoms gentle fire
 

 Like that of incense on the altar laid.”

“There,” said he, “Dryden drew from a profundity which Pope could not reach. But it is time for us to be waiting on my lady.” He hoisted himself from his chair, brushed the crumbs from his waistcoat, straightened his rusty cravat, and opened the door with a bow to the others. He was in the best of spirits.

===Chinese===
他说这话时笑容温存，像父亲说到自己的孩子。

「她是幸福的，我想。」他说，把这句话重复了三遍。「您见过她，」他转向阿拉斯泰尔。「您能证实我的信念——她是幸福的么？」

「她正深深地爱着。」对方答道。

「并且把爱化作幸福，」约翰逊说，随即用洪亮的嗓音吟诵起几句诗来，以手击节：

> 「爱情以百般心肠燃起百般火焰；
 
 　它在温柔胸怀里搅起温柔的火，
 
 　恰似馨香在祭坛上焚起。」

「瞧，」他说，「德莱顿（Dryden）从蒲柏（Pope）不能及的深处汲出了这几句。不过，我们该去侍候我们的小姐了。」他从椅子里把自己拔了出来，掸掉马甲上的面包屑，扶正那生锈的旧领巾，向其他人一躬身，开了门。他正处在兴致的高处。
===Original===
The landlady was waiting to show the two upstairs, Edom having meantime retired to smoke a pipe in the bar. As they ascended, the gale was still pounding on the roof and an unshuttered lattice showed a thick drift of snow on the outer sill, but over the tumult came the echo of a clear voice singing. To Alastair’s surprise it was a song he knew, the very song that Midwinter had played two nights before. “Diana and her darling crew” sang the voice, and as the door opened it was Diana herself that seemed to the young man to be walking to meet him. *Vera incessu patuit Dea.*

Mrs. Peckover had dressed her hair, which the coach journey had disarranged, but to Alastair’s eye her air was childlike, as contrasted with the hooped and furbelowed ladies of the French court. Her skirts were straight and unmodish, so that her limbs moved freely, and the slim young neck was encircled with her only jewel﻿—a string of pearls. The homely inn chamber, which till a few hours before had been but the Brown Room, was now to him a hall in a palace, a glade in the greenwood, or wherever else walk princesses and nymphs.

She gave him her hand and then dropped into a chair, looking at him earnestly from under her long eyelashes.

===Chinese===
老板娘候着，引二人上楼；以东已趁这工夫退到店堂里抽烟斗去了。两人拾级而上时，狂风还在擂打着屋顶，一扇未上板的花格窗露出外窗台上厚厚的积雪，可就在这一片喧嚣之上，飘来一个清亮嗓音的歌声。让阿拉斯泰尔吃惊的是，那歌他认得——正是仲冬两夜之前奏的那支曲子。「狄安娜与她宠爱的侍从」，歌声唱道；房门开处，在年轻人看来，迎面走来迎接他的，正是狄安娜本人。*Vera incessu patuit Dea*——步履之间，真神自显。

佩克欧弗太太已替她重新做好头发——一路车马把它弄乱了——可在阿拉斯泰尔眼里，她通身的气韵仍是孩子气的，同法兰西宫廷那些束着裙箍、缀满荷叶边的贵妇全然两样。她的裙裾笔直，不入时流，四肢因此活动自如；那纤细的年轻颈项上，只环着她唯一的饰物——一串珍珠。这间家常的客栈卧房，几个钟头之前还不过是「棕室」一间，此刻在他眼里，已成了宫殿中的殿堂、绿林中的空地，成了公主与仙娥漫步的任何所在。

她向他伸出手来，随即坐进椅中，从长长的睫毛底下恳切地望着他。

===Original===
“I thought that b-by this time you would be in L-Lancashire, Captain Maclean.”

“So also did I,” and he told her the story of Gypsy Ben and his morning’s hunt. “There is business I have had news of in these parts, a riddle I must unravel before I can ride north with a quiet mind. The enemy musters in Nottinghamshire, and I must carry word of his dispositions.”

Her brown eyes had kindled. “Ben is a rogue then! By Heaven, sir, I will have him stript and whipt from Thames to Severn. Never fear but my vengeance shall reach him. Oh, I am heartily glad to know the truth, for though I have used him much I have had my misgivings. He carried letters for me to my dear Sir John.” She stopped suddenly. “That is why the replies are delayed. Oh, the faithless scoundrel! I can love a foe but I do abhor all traitors.﻿ ﻿… Do you say the enemy musters in Nottingham?” The anger in her voice had been replaced by eagerness at this new thought.

===Chinese===
「我还想，这、这时辰你总该到兰开夏了，麦克莱恩上尉。」

「我也这样想。」于是他把吉普赛本的事和他那场晨间围猎讲给她听。「我在此地接得了消息，有一桩哑谜，须得解开了，我才能安心策马北上。敌军正在诺丁汉郡集结，我必须把他们的部署带回去。」

她那双褐色的眼睛燃了起来。「这么说，本是个恶棍！皇天在上，先生，我要叫人剥光他的衣服，从泰晤士河一路鞭打到塞文河。放心，我的报复自会追上他。噢，知道真相我打心底里高兴，因为虽说一直用他，我心里到底存着疑影。他替我给我亲爱的约翰爵士捎信来着。」她猛地停住。「难怪回信迟迟不来。噢，这没信义的恶棍！仇敌我都能爱，叛徒我却深恶痛绝。……你说敌军集结在诺丁汉？」话音里的怒火，已被这个新念头换成了急切。
===Original===
“So it is reported, and, as I read it, he may march by this very road if he hopes to take the Prince’s flank. You at Brightwell may have the war in your garden.”

Her eyes glistened. “If only Sir John were here! There is the chance of a famous exploit. You are a soldier, sir. Show me, for I love the gossip of war.”

On the hearthstone with a charred stick he drew roughly the two roads from the north. “Here or hereabouts will lie the decision,” he said. “Cumberland cannot suffer the Prince to approach nearer London without a battle. If you hear of us south of Derby undefeated, then you may know, my lady, that honesty has won.”

She cried out, twining her hands.

===Chinese===
「传闻如此。依我推断，他若想攻王子的侧翼，走的说不定正是这条路。你们布莱特韦尔，怕是要把战事摆进自家花园里了。」

她目光灼灼。「约翰爵士要是在这儿就好了！正有一场成名功业的机会。您是行伍中人，先生。给我讲讲，我最爱听战争的谈屑。」

他在炉石上用一根烧焦的木棍，粗粗画出两条北来的大道。「决胜之地，就在此处或此处左近，」他说。「坎伯兰绝不会坐视王子再向伦敦逼近一步而不决战。夫人，您若听闻我军到了德比以南仍未落败，便可知道，正道已经赢了。」

她绞着双手，喊出声来。

===Original===
“Tell me more, sir. I had thought to pass the evening playing Pope Joan with my Puffin, but you are here to teach me a better pastime. Instruct me, for I am desperate ignorant.”

Alastair repeated once again his creed in which during the past days he had come the more firmly to believe. There must be a victory in England, but in the then condition of Wales and the West a very little victory would suffice to turn the scale. The danger lay in doubting counsels in the Prince’s own circle. Boldness, and still boldness, was the only wisdom. To be cautious was to be rash; to creep soberly south with a careful eye to communications was to run a deadly peril; to cut loose and march incontinent for London was safe and prudent. “Therefore I must get quickly to the Prince’s side,” he said, “for he has many doubting Thomases around him, and few with experience of war.”

“He has my Sir John,” she said proudly. “Sir John is young, and has not seen such service as you, but he is of the same bold spirit. I know his views, for he has told them me, and they are yours.”

===Chinese===
「再给我讲讲，先生。我原打算今晚同我的老风箱玩教皇琼牌的，不想您来了，教我一样更好的消遣。开导我吧，我无知得很。」

阿拉斯泰尔又一次申说他的信条——这几日里，他对此信得愈发坚定了。在英格兰必须打一场胜仗；但以眼下威尔士与西部之势，一场小小的胜利便足以扭转乾坤。危险在于王子身边疑虑重重的谋议。唯有大胆，大胆，再大胆，方是唯一的明智。谨慎即是鲁莽；小心翼翼盯着交通线、稳稳当当地向南爬行，即是自蹈死地；甩开一切、马不停蹄直扑伦敦，才是安全与持重。「所以我必须尽快赶到王子身边，」他说，「他周围多疑的人太多，懂战争的人太少。」

「他还有我的约翰爵士呢，」她骄傲地说。「约翰爵士年轻，见识的战阵不如您多，可他同样是副大胆的脾性。他的见解我都知道，他都告诉过我——同您的一模一样。」
===Original===
“There are too many halfhearted, and there is also rank treason about. Your Gypsy Ben is the type of thousands.”

She clenched her hands and held them high. “How I l-loathe it! Oh, if I thought I could betray the Cause I should hang myself. If I thought that one I loved could be a traitor I should d-die.” There was such emotion in her voice that the echo of it alarmed her and she changed her tone.

“Puffin,” she cried, “are you honest on our side? I have sometimes doubted you.”

“Madam,” Mr. Johnson replied in the same bantering voice, “I can promise that at any rate I will not betray you. Being neither soldier nor statesman, I am not yet called to play an overt part in the quarrel, but I am a Prince’s man inasmuch as I believe in the divine origin of the Christian state and therefore in the divine right of monarchs to govern. I am no grey rat from Hanover.”

===Chinese===
「三心二意的人太多，四下里还有明目张胆的叛卖。你那位吉普赛本，就是成千上万人的标本。」

她攥紧双手，高高举起。「我多么憎、憎恶这个！噢，若想到自己会背叛大业，我会亲手吊死自己；若想到我爱的哪个人会当叛徒，我会立、立刻死去。」她的声音里情感那样激越，连它的回响都惊了她自己，于是换了语气。

「老风箱，」她叫道，「你在我们这一边么，当真心么？我有时可是疑过你的。」

「夫人，」约翰逊先生用同样的打趣语声答道，「我至少可以应许您：我绝不背叛您。我既非军人，亦非政客，尚未奉召在这场争端里扮演公开的角色；但我信基督教国家有其神圣起源，因而信君临天下者有其神授之权——就凭这个，我是王子的人。我可不是汉诺威来的灰耗子。」

===Original===
“Yet,” she said, with a chiding finger, “I have heard you say that a Tory was a creature generated between a non-juring parson and one’s grandmother.”

“Nay, my dear lady,” he cried, “such heresy was never mine. I only quoted it as a pernicious opinion of another, and I quoted too my answer that ‘the Devil, as the first foe to constituted authority, was the first Whig.’ ”

At this juncture Mrs. Peckover appeared with a kettle of boiling water and the rest of the equipment of tea, which the girl dispensed out of the coarse inn earthenware and sweetened with the coarse sugar which Mr. Johnson had used for his port. While the latter drank his dish noisily, she looked curiously at Alastair.

“You are no politician, Captain Maclean, and doubtless have no concern with the arguments with which our gentlemen soothe their consciences. You do not seek wealth or power﻿—of that I am certain. What are the bonds that join you to the Prince?”

===Chinese===
「可是，」她说着，竖起一根嗔怪的指头，「我可听你说过，托利党人是拒宣誓的牧师同人家的祖母生出来的孽种。」

「岂有此理，我亲爱的夫人，」他叫道，「那等异端邪说从来不是我的。我只是引述旁人的谬论，还引了我的答语——『魔鬼既是既定权威的头一个仇敌，便是头一个辉格党人。』」

正在这当口，佩克欧弗太太提着一壶滚水和其余的茶具出现了；姑娘用客栈的粗陶分茶，加的粗糖，正是约翰逊先生方才调波特酒用的那种。趁后者大声啜着茶，她好奇地打量阿拉斯泰尔。

「您不是政客，麦克莱恩上尉，想必也不关心我们这些先生用来抚慰良心的那些议论。您不求财，也不求权——这一点我确信。那么，是什么纽带把您系在王子身上呢？」
===Original===
“I am a plain soldier,” he said, “and but fulfil my orders.”

“Nay, but you do not answer me. You do more than obey your orders; you are an enthusiast, as Sir John is﻿—as I am﻿—as that dull Puffin is not. I am curious to know the reason of your faith.”

Alastair, looking into the fire, found himself constrained to reply.

“I am of the old religion,” he said, “and loyalty to my king is one of its articles.”

She nodded. “I am a daughter of another church, which has also that teaching.”

===Chinese===
「我是一介武夫，」他说，「不过是奉命行事。」

「不，您没有回答我。您做的远不止奉命行事；您是一位热忱之士，约翰爵士是——我也是——那位乏味的老风箱不是。我很好奇您信仰的缘由。」

阿拉斯泰尔望着炉火，觉得自己不能不答了。

「我奉的是旧教，」他说，「而效忠吾王，正是旧教信条中的一条。」

她点了点头。「我是另一个教会之女，那教会也有这条教义。」

===Original===
“Also I am of the Highlands, and I love the ancient ways. My clan has fought for them and lost, and it is in my blood to fight still and risk the losing.”

Her eyes encouraged him, and he found himself telling the tale of Clan Gillian﻿—the centuries-long feud with Clan Diarmaid, the shrinking of its lands in Mull and Morvern, the forays with Montrose and Dundee, the sounding record of its sons in the wars of Europe. He told of the old tower of Glentarnit, with the loch lapping about it, and his father who had no other child but him; of the dreams of his youth in the hot heather; of that little ragged clan which looked to him as leader and provider; and into his voice there came the pathos and passion of long memories.

“I fight for that,” he said; “for the old things.”

It seemed that he had touched her. Her eyes were misty and with a child’s gesture she laid a hand on his sleeve and stroked it. The spell which had fallen on them was broken by Mr. Johnson.

===Chinese===
「何况我出身高地，钟爱古老的道路。我的氏族为它们战斗过，也败落过；再战一场、再冒一次败落之险，本就溶在我的血里。」

她的眼睛鼓励着他，他不知不觉讲起了吉利安氏族的故事——与迪尔迈德氏族数百年的世仇，在马尔与莫尔文日削月割的领地，随蒙特罗斯与邓迪出征的劫掠，其子弟在欧洲历次大战中赫赫可闻的声名。他讲到格伦塔尼特的老塔，湖水轻轻拍着塔基；讲到他的父亲，除他之外别无子嗣；讲到少年时代在灼热石楠丛中的梦；讲到那支衣衫褴褛的小小氏族，仰望他做领袖与供养人；他的嗓音里，渐渐渗入悠长记忆的悲怆与炽热。

「我为的就是这个，」他说；「为那些古老的事物。」

看来他触动了她。她眼中蒙起水雾，孩子气地把一只手搭在他袖上，轻轻抚着。笼罩两人的那道魔咒，被约翰逊先生打破了。
===Original===
“I conceive,” he said, “that the power of the Scottish chief is no less than Homeric, and his position more desirable than that of any grandee in England. He may be poor, but he has high duties and exacts a fine reverence. When I was a child my father put into my hands Martin’s book on the Western Isles, and ever since I have desired to visit them and behold the patriarchal life with my own eyes.”

“Your Highlanders are good soldiers?” she asked.

“They are the spear-point of the Prince’s strength,” said Alastair.

“It is a strange time,” said Johnson, “which sees enlisted on the same side many superfine gentlemen of France, certain sophisticated politicians of England, and these simple, brave, ignorant clansmen.”

“There is one bond which unites them all,” she cried with enthusiasm, “which places my Sir John and the humblest Scotch peasant on an equality. They have the honesty to see their duty and the courage to follow it. What can stand against loyalty? It is the faith that moves mountains.”

===Chinese===
「依我看，」他说，「苏格兰族长的权柄不亚于荷马时代，其地位之可羡，胜过英格兰任何贵胄。他或许贫穷，却有崇高的职分，收受着可观的敬意。我小时候，父亲把马丁那部写西部群岛的书放进我手里；从那时起，我便一直想亲往一游，亲眼看看那种宗法生活。」

「你们高地人是好兵么？」她问。

「他们是王子兵锋的矛尖。」阿拉斯泰尔说。

「这是个奇怪的时代，」约翰逊说，「法兰西的一众衣冠上等的绅士、英格兰的若干老于世故的政客，同这些淳朴、勇敢、无知的氏族子弟，竟会应募在同一面旗下。」

「有一条纽带把他们统统连在一起，」她热切地喊道，「这条纽带，让我的约翰爵士与最卑微的苏格兰农夫平起平坐。他们认得清本分的诚实，追得上本分的勇气。忠诚面前，何物能挡？这便是移山的信念。」

===Original===
“Amen, my dear lady,” said Johnson, and Alastair with a sudden impulse seized her hand and carried it to his lips.

===Chinese===
「阿门，我亲爱的夫人。」约翰逊说。阿拉斯泰尔则凭一阵突来的冲动，捉起她的手，送到唇边。

---
===Original===
The next morning dawned as silent as midnight. The wind had died, the snowfall had ceased, and the world lay choked, six-foot drifts in the road, twenty foot in the dells, and, with it all, patches of hilltop as bare as a man’s hand. The shepherds were out with the first light digging sheep from the wreaths, and the cows after milking never left the byres. No traveller appeared on the road, for a coach was a manifest impossibility, and a horse little better. Alastair and Johnson breakfasted at leisure, and presently the elder of the Weston servants brought word of the condition of the highway. This was borne by Mrs. Peckover to her mistress, who summoned Mr. Johnson to her to discuss the situation. The landlord was unhopeful. Unless he could put six horses to it the coach would not get to Brightwell, though a squad of men went ahead to clear the drifts. The extra four horses he could not provide since his wagons were all at Marlock and the two riding horses were useless for coach work. The best plan would be to send to Brightwell for the requisite horses, and this should be done later in the day, if no further snow fell. The lady pouted, but settled herself comfortably at cartes with her maid.

She inquired after Alastair’s plans, and was told that he would make a shift to travel, since his errand brooked no delay. Thereafter he found the landlord and drew him aside. “You were bidden by our friend to take orders from me,” he said. “I have but the one. I stay on here, but you will let it be known that I have gone﻿—this day after noon. You will give me a retired room with a key, forbid it to chambermaids, serve me with your own hand, and show me some way of private entry. It is important that I be thought to have left the countryside.”

The man did as he was told and Alastair spent the morning with Mr. Johnson, who suffered from a grievous melancholy after the exhilaration of the night before. At first he had turned the pages of the only book in the inn, an ancient devotional work entitled *A Shove for a Heavy-Sterned Christian*. But presently he flung it from him and sat sidelong in a chair with his shoulders humped, his eye dull and languid, and his left leg twitching like a man with the palsy. His voice was sharp-pitched, as if it came from a body in pain.

===Chinese===
次日清晨，静得如同午夜。风已止息，雪也已停，整个世界噎得透不过气来：路上雪积六英尺，洼地深达二十英尺，饶是如此，山顶上仍有一块块光秃得像手掌的地方。牧人们天一亮就出门，从雪堆里刨羊；奶牛挤完奶，再没出过牛棚。路上不见一个行客，因为马车显然不可能通行，骑马也好不到哪里去。阿拉斯泰尔和约翰逊从容用了早餐，不多时，韦斯顿仆人中年长的那位来报路况。这话由佩克欧弗太太传给夫人，夫人便把约翰逊先生请了去商议局面。店主不抱什么希望。除非套上六匹马，否则马车到不了布莱特韦尔，纵使派一队人先去铲雪也不中用。多出来的那四匹马他拿不出——他的大车全在马洛克，两匹坐骑又干不了拉车的活。上策是遣人去布莱特韦尔借必要的马，若不再下雪，今日下半天便可去办。夫人噘起了嘴，只好同女仆舒舒服服打起牌来。

她问起阿拉斯泰尔的打算，得到的回答是：他要想法子上路，因为他的差事刻不容缓。随后他寻到店主，把人拉到一边。「咱们那位朋友吩咐过，要你听我差遣，」他说。「我只有一道令。我留在这儿不走了，但你要放出话去，说我已经走了——就在今日午后。你给我一间僻静的房间，配上钥匙，不许侍女进出，饭食你亲手来送，再指给我一条私下进出的路径。要紧的是让人相信，我已经离开此地了。」

店主照办了。阿拉斯泰尔便陪约翰逊先生消磨了一上午——昨夜的亢奋过后，那人正陷于一场沉沉的忧郁。起初他翻着店里唯一的一本书，一部古旧的灵修著作，题作《给重臀基督徒的一推》（A Shove for a Heavy-Sterned Christian）。可不多时他便把它摔在一边，侧身坐进椅子，肩膀耸起，眼神呆滞倦怠，左腿抽动，像害了麻痹症的人。他嗓音发尖，仿佛发自一具正疼痛的身体。

===Original===
“I am subject to such fits,” he told Alastair. “They come when my mind is unemployed and when I have pampered my body with over-rich food. Now I suffer from both causes. Nay, sir, do not commiserate me. Each of us must live his life on the terms on which it is given him. Others have some perpetual weakness of mind or some agonising pain. I have these black moods when I see only the littleness of life and the terrors of death.”

Lady Norreys had written a letter to her husband’s great-uncle at Brightwell, and armed with it Alastair set out a little before midday. He had dressed himself in the frieze and leather with which Midwinter had provided him, for it was as good a garb as a kilt for winter snows. The direction was simple. He had but to follow the valley, for Brightwell was at its head, before the road began to climb to the watershed.

To one who had shot hinds on steeper hills in wilder winters the journey was child’s play. He made his road by the barer ridges, and circumvented the hollows or crossed them where matted furze or hazel made a foundation. He found that the higher he moved up the vale the less deep became the fall, and the shallower the wreaths, as if the force of the wind had been abated by the loftier mountains. Brightwell lay in a circle of woods on whose darkness the snow had left only a powder; before it ran the upper streams of a little river; behind it the dale became a ravine and high round-shouldered hills crowded in on it.

===Chinese===
「我常犯这样的毛病，」他告诉阿拉斯泰尔。「心里闲着无事、身子又被过丰的饮食娇纵了的时候，它们就来了。如今两样都占了。不，先生，不要怜悯我。人人的命，都得按上天开出的条件去活。旁人有终身的智力孱弱，有钻心的剧痛；我有的是这些黑色的心境——在那里面，我只看得见生命之渺小与死亡之恐怖。」

诺里斯夫人已给布莱特韦尔她丈夫的叔祖写好一封信，阿拉斯泰尔揣着它，将近正午时上了路。他穿的是仲冬给他置办的粗呢皮革行头，御起冬雪来，同苏格兰短裙一样合用。路线简单：只消顺着山谷走，布莱特韦尔就在谷头，在道路开始爬上分水岭之前。

对曾在更荒的冬天、更陡的山上猎过母鹿的人，这趟路不过儿戏。他拣光秃的山脊开路，洼地则绕行过去，或从荆豆、榛树盘结成根基的地方踏越。他发觉越往谷里高处走，积雪越浅，雪堆越薄，仿佛风势已被更高的山岭削弱了。布莱特韦尔卧在一圈树林之中，雪只在幽暗的林子上覆了薄薄一层粉；宅前流着一条小河的上源；宅后，山谷收作峡隙，高耸的圆肩山岭四面逼来。
===Original===
A thin column of smoke rose from a chimney into the bitter windless noon, so the place was inhabited. But the gates of the main entrance were shut﻿—massive gates flanked by stone pillars bearing a cognisance of three mullets on a chief﻿—and the snow of the avenue was a virgin sheet of white. Alastair entered the park by a gap in the wall, crossed the snow-filled river, and came by way of a hornbeam avenue to the back parts of the house. There he found signs of humanity. The courtyard was trampled into slush, and tracks led out from it to the woody hills. But nevertheless an air of death sat on the place, as if this life it bore witness to was only a sudden start in a long slumber. With his spirits heavily depressed he made his way to what seemed to be the door, and entered a lesser courtyard, where he was at once attacked by two noisy dogs.

As he drove them off, half thankful for their cheerful violence, an old man, dressed in black like a butler, appeared. He had a thin peevish face, and eyes that squinted so terribly that it was impossible to guess the direction of his gaze. He received the letter without a word and disappeared. After a considerable lapse of time he returned and bade Alastair follow him through a labyrinth of passages, till they reached a high old panelled hall, darkened by lozenged heraldic windows, and most feebly warmed by a little fire of damp faggots. There he was left alone a second time, while he had leisure to observe the immense dusty groining and the antlers and horns, black as bog oak, on the walls. Then suddenly a woman stood before him.

She was tall as a grenadier and beaked like a falcon, and to defend her against the morning cold she wore what seemed to be a military coat and a turban. Her voice was surprisingly deep and large.

“You are the messenger from the Sleeping Deer? My lady Norreys lies there storm-stayed, because of the snow and asks for horses? You travelled that road yourself. Would six horses bring a coach through?”

===Chinese===
一缕细细的炊烟升上苦寒无风的正午天空，可见宅中有人。但正门的大门紧闭——门极厚重，两侧石柱上镌着纹章：纹章带上三枚星徽——林荫道上的雪还是一匹未经践踏的白。阿拉斯泰尔从墙上一处豁口进了园地，蹚过填满积雪的小河，循一条鹅耳枥林荫道走到宅子后部。在那里他见到了人的痕迹。庭院被踩成了泥雪浆，一道道蹄印脚印从院里通向林木繁茂的山冈。然而一股死气仍笼罩着这地方，仿佛它所见证的这点生机，不过是长眠中的一次突然惊动。他带着沉沉下压的心境，走向似乎是门户的地方，进到一个小些的庭院，立刻被两只狂吠的狗扑上来攻击。

他把狗赶开，心里倒有几分感激它们这番热闹的凶恶；这时出现了一个老人，一身黑衣，像是个管家。他生着一张瘦削乖戾的脸，眼睛斜视得厉害，教人猜不出他目光落向何方。他一言不发地收下信，消失了。过了好长一段工夫他才回来，引阿拉斯泰尔穿过迷宫般的走廊，直到一座高大的旧式镶板厅堂——菱格纹章窗透不进光亮，一小堆湿柴几乎烘不出暖气。他在那里第二次被单独留下，得空打量那巨大而积尘的拱顶，以及墙上黑得像沼泽橡木的鹿角与兽角。然后，蓦地，一个女人立在他面前。

她高得像名掷弹兵，生着鹰隼似的钩喙；为抵御清晨的寒冷，她穿一件貌似军装的外衣，裹一顶头巾式帽。

「你是睡鹿客栈来的信差？诺里斯夫人被风雪困在那儿了——因为这场雪——要借马匹？那条路你自己走过。六匹马能把马车拉过来么？」

===Original===
Alastair, coarsening his accent as best he could, replied that with care six horses could get a coach to Brightwell.

“Then return at once and say that the horses will be there an hour before sunset.”

A new voice joined in, which came from an older woman, fat as the other was lean, who had waddled to her side.

“But, sister, bethink you we have not the animals.”

===Chinese===
阿拉斯泰尔尽量把口音放粗，回答说：只要当心，六匹马能把马车拉到布莱特韦尔。

「那就即刻回去传话：马匹将在日落前一个钟头备齐在那里。」

一个新的声音插了进来，出自一个更老的女人——一个胖子，同那位的瘦恰成对照——她已摇摇摆摆挪到了她身旁。

「可是，姐姐，你想一想，咱们没有这些牲口呀。」
===Original===
The first speaker turned fiercely. “The animals must and shall be found. We cannot have our new cousin moping in a public hostel on her first visit to us. For shame, Caroline.”

“Back with you,” she turned to Alastair. “Bennet will give you a glass of ale, but see you do not dally over it.”

The buttery ale was not such as to invite dalliance, and like the whole place smacked either of narrow means or narrow souls. Even the kitchen, of which he had a glimpse, was comfortless. To warm his blood Alastair trotted across the park, and as he ran with his head low almost butted into a horseman who was riding on one of the paths that converged on the back courtyard. He pulled himself up in time, warned by the rider’s cry, and saw pass him a gentleman in a heavy fawn riding-coat, whose hat was pulled down over his brows and showed little of his face. Two sharp eyes flashed on him and then lifted, and a sharp nose, red with the weather, projected over the high coat collar.

Alastair stared after him and reached certain conclusions. That was the nose he had seen by the light of Edom’s lantern the night he spent with Kyd at the inn. That was the back he had observed yesterday afternoon riding away from the Sleeping Deer. Thirdly and most important﻿—and though his evidence was scanty he had no doubt on the matter﻿—the gentleman was Sir John Norreys. My lady when she reached Brightwell would find her husband.

===Chinese===
先说话的那位猛地转过身来。「牲口必须找到，也一定找得到。新亲头一回上门，我们不能让她在公共客栈里愁闷。不知羞，卡罗琳。」

「回去吧，」她转向阿拉斯泰尔。「贝内特会给你一杯麦酒，可不许你赖着磨蹭。」

酒房的麦酒本也不诱人逗留，同整个宅子一样，要么透着寒酸，要么透着小气。连厨房——他瞥了一眼——也不见一点舒适。为暖起血气，阿拉斯泰尔小跑着穿过园地；他低着头跑，几乎一头撞上一位骑马的人——那人正走在几条汇向后院的小径之一上。骑者一声喊，他赶忙收步，只见一位绅士从他身边过去，穿一件厚重的浅黄褐色骑装，帽檐压到眉际，看不大见脸。两只锐利的眼睛朝他一闪，随即抬起；一只冻红了的尖鼻子，从高竖的领口上探出来。

阿拉斯泰尔目送着他，得出了几点结论。那正是他在客店同基德过夜那晚、就着以东灯笼的光看见过的鼻子。那正是他昨日午后望见从睡鹿客栈驰走的背影。第三点，也是最要紧的一点——虽然凭据寥寥，他对此却毫不怀疑——这位绅士就是约翰·诺里斯爵士。夫人到得布莱特韦尔，将见到她的丈夫。

## XII The Hut in the Oak Shaw / 十二、橡树丛中的小屋

===Original===

The sinking at the heart disappeared long before Alastair reached his attic, and was replaced by a violent heat of anger. He lit a candle, for the dark irked him, and sat on his bed with his face as scarlet as if it had been buffeted. He felt his temples throb and a hot dryness at the back of his throat. For the moment thoughts of the dire peril to the Cause were swallowed up in natural fury at a rogue.

Blind fool that he had been! All the steps were now bitterly clear in his bedraggled Odyssey. At Cornbury Kyd had been sowing tares in my lord’s mind﻿—not in partnership with the Duchess Kitty, of that he was assured﻿—he did not believe that that vivacious lady, Whig as she might be, was a partner of his villainy. From the first encounter at the roadside inn the man had dogged him; perhaps that meeting had been premeditated. The scene at Flambury, the accusing mummer in Squire Thicknesse’s Justice Room, the well-informed warrant, Ben the Gypsy and his treachery﻿—all were the doing of the pawky Lammermuir laird. General Oglethorpe would use his services but prevent his getting his reward; but there were others less scrupulous, and anyhow these services spelled death to the Prince’s fortunes.﻿ ﻿… A second Grosvenor fortune would be achieved! No, by God, it should not, if Alastair Maclean were left another six months alive!

Sir John Norreys was the man’s tool, and the news from the West passed through him to Kingston and Wade, and Ligonier and Cumberland, and Mr. Pelham in London. Mr. Pelham doubtless had taken steps. He would arrest the levy in the West before it had grown dangerous; and the fines and forfeitures of broken loyalists would go to enrich the Exchequer and Mr. Nicholas Kyd of Greyhouses.﻿ ﻿… He had lost his dislike of Sir John. That huckstering baronet was only an instrument in the hand of a cleverer knave.

===Chinese===

早在回到自己的阁楼之前，阿拉斯泰尔心头的悸沉便已消失，代之而起的是一股怒火的燥热。他点起一支蜡烛——黑暗叫他烦躁——坐在床沿，一张脸红得像被人掴打过似的。他觉出太阳穴突突直跳，喉咙后头一阵火辣辣的干渴。此刻，大业凶险的种种思虑，尽数被对一个好贼的天然怒火吞没了。

他真是个瞎了眼的傻瓜！这趟狼狈不堪的奥德赛（Odyssey），如今每一步都清清楚楚、苦涩刺目。在科恩伯里时，基德便已在勋爵心里播下了稗子——却不是同基蒂公爵夫人合谋，这一点他敢断言——他不信那位鲜活爽利的夫人，纵然是个辉格党，肯与他那桩奸恶的营生为伍。从路旁客栈的初遇起，这人便一路盯着他；说不定那场相遇，本就是事先安排好的。弗兰伯里的那一出，西克尼斯乡绅审理室里指认他的扮戏戏子，那纸消息灵通的拘票，吉普赛本和他的背叛——桩桩件件，都是拉默缪尔那个狡黠领主的手笔。奥格尔索普将军要用他的效力，却不容他领功受赏；可还有些人没这般讲究，而无论如何，这效力对王子殿下的大业都是死路一条。……又一份格罗夫纳式的家业，眼看就要攒成了！不，上帝在上，只要阿拉斯泰尔·麦克莱恩还有六个月好活，这事就成不了！

约翰·诺里斯爵士不过是这人的工具，西边来的消息经他一手，传到金斯顿与韦德那里，传到利戈尼耶（Ligonier）与坎伯兰那里，再传到伦敦的佩勒姆先生（Mr. Pelham）那里。佩勒姆先生想必已有布置。他会在西边的义军养成气候之前先行一网拿尽；而倾家败业的忠义之士所纳的罚金、所抄没的家产，便去充实国库，充实格雷豪斯的尼古拉斯·基德先生。……他对约翰爵士的嫌恶已经消了。那个市侩的从男爵，不过是攥在一个更精明的恶棍手里的一件家什。

===Original===

But why was Kyd here, when he had sent Edom to Brightwell with the news that he was not to be looked for before the close of the month? He did not believe that Edom had lied, so either there was a deeper game afoot, or Kyd had changed his plans. He thought the latter, for even rogues were the sport of circumstance. Some news had reached him of surpassing importance and he had posted all that way to see Oglethorpe, who, as a former Jacobite, would be the more readily believed by the Government when he acted against his former friends.

It stood to reason that Kyd would visit Brightwell, to see Norreys, to instruct his servant﻿—some errand or other, even if he returned next day to the South. Brightwell was the Philippi of the campaign, the place of meetings, or why had Norreys been sent there? Even now the laird’s ruddy visage and the baronet’s lean jaw might be close together in some damnable machination.﻿ ﻿… And the lady, the poor lady. At the thought of her Alastair clenched his hands, and shut his eyes tight to kill the pain in them. That poor nymph, that dainty innocence in such a den of satyrs!

And then, oddly enough, his mood changed to a happier one as the picture of Claudia Norreys brightened on the screen of his memory. Please God, she was cut off now forever from the man she called husband. Her eyes must soon be opened, and he pictured her loathing, her horror of disgust. There were other thoughts at the back of his mind, which he choked down, for this was no time for pretty fancies. But it comforted him to think that he was fighting for the happiness of the girl who sang “Diana.”

===Chinese===

可是，基德既已打发以东到布莱特韦尔传话，说自己月底之前不必指望归来，此刻却又在这里作甚？他不信以东撒了谎，那么，要么是另有一局更深的棋在走，要么便是基德改了计划。他认定是后者，因为即便是恶棍，也免不了被时运播弄。想必是有天大的要紧消息传到他手里，他才驿马兼程赶那一路来见奥格尔索普——此人身为从前的雅各布党，一旦反戈对付旧日的朋友，政府只会愈发信得过他。

照情理推想，基德必要来布莱特韦尔一走，见见诺里斯，吩咐他的仆人——总有这样那样一桩差事，纵然他次日便折回南边去。布莱特韦尔是这场战事的腓力比（Philippi），是各方会合之地——不然何必把诺里斯打发到那里去？说不定此时此刻，那领主的赤红面孔和那从男爵的瘦长下颌，正凑在一处密谋着什么可咒的勾当。……还有那位夫人，那位可怜的夫人。一想到她，阿拉斯泰尔便攥紧双拳，死死阖上眼睛，好掐灭眼底的疼痛。那可怜的林中仙子，那娇柔的无辜者，竟落在这样一个羊怪成群的巢穴里！

说也奇怪，随后他的心境竟转晴了，因为克劳迪娅·诺里斯的身影，在他记忆的幕布上明亮起来。托上帝的福，她如今总算永远脱离了她称作丈夫的那个男人。她的双眼不久必将睁开，他想象着她的憎厌，她那深恶痛绝的惊骇。他心底还藏着别样的念头，都被他强自按了下去——眼下不是想入非非的时候。但一想到自己是为那位唱过《狄安娜》的姑娘的幸福而战，他便得了安慰。

===Original===

He slept little and at dawn was up and dressed in his frieze and leather, his coarse stockings and his hobnailed shoes. The frost was passing, and a mild south wind blew up the vale, softening the snow crust and sending runnels of water down the hollows and eaves of the great drifts. Alastair found the landlord breakfasting in the dog-kennel he called his room.

“I am going to Brightwell,” he told him, “and may be absent for days. Expect me back when you see me. Keep my room locked, and leave the key as before in the crack below the broken axle-hole of the mill.” Then he stepped out-of-doors, where the milkers were just opening the byres, and soon was on the hillside with his face to the High Peak.

He crossed the high road and looked at the tracks. There was one fresh and clear, that of a man in heavy boots plodding towards the inn. There were faint hoof marks also, but they seemed to be old. He reflected that the thaw could not have begun till after midnight, and that if Kyd had ridden this road his horse’s track would have shown no more than the others of yesterday.

The sun was well above the horizon when he reached the park wall of Brightwell and entered the demesne by his usual gap. It was a morning like early spring, when the whole world was full of melting snows, running waters and light breezes. His plan was to go to the wood which overhung the kitchen yard and gave a prospect of the house and all its environs. There he would watch till noon, in the hope that either Kyd would appear or one of Lady Norreys’ party. If the former, he would follow him and have the interview for which his soul longed; if the latter, then he would find a way of getting speech and learning the nature of the household. If nothing happened by noon, he would contrive to make his way into the kitchen as before, and trust to his wits to find an errand.

===Chinese===

他睡得极少，天一亮便起身，穿上那身粗呢与皮革，粗毛线袜，和那双钉了平头钉的乡下鞋。霜冻正在退去，一股温和的南风顺谷吹上来，融软了雪壳，融雪的水顺着大雪堆的凹处和檐边淌成一道道细流。阿拉斯泰尔寻见店主时，他正在他唤作房间的那间狗窝里用早餐。

“我要去布莱特韦尔，”他告诉店主，“也许要离开好几天。几时见着我人，几时再等我回来。把我那间屋子锁好，钥匙还照旧搁在磨坊破轴孔下面的裂缝里。”说罢他跨出门去，挤奶工们正打开牛栏，不多时他已上了山坡，脸朝着皮克高地。

他跨过大路，察看路上的痕迹。有一道印子新而清晰，是个穿重靴的汉子一步一步蹭向客栈去的。另有几处浅淡的蹄印，却像是旧的。他心想，解冻不到午夜之后不会开始，倘若基德骑马走过这条路，他的马蹄印便不会比昨天留下的那些更显新。

待他抵达布莱特韦尔的园墙，从惯走的那处缺口进入庄园时，太阳已升得老高。这是个宛如早春的早晨，整个世界满是消融的积雪、奔流的水声和轻软的风。他的计划是到那片俯临厨房院子的树林去，从那里能望见宅子和它四围的动静。他要在那里守到正午，盼着基德露面，或是诺里斯夫人一行中的什么人出现。若是前者，他便跟踪上去，求那场他灵魂渴慕的会面；若是后者，他便设法接上话，探明这一家人的底细。倘若到正午还毫无动静，他就照旧设法混进厨房去，凭机智寻个由头。

===Original===

He saw no one as he forded the now turbulent stream and climbed the farther slope to the wood of hazels and ashes which clung like an eyebrow to the edge of a bare grey bluff, beneath which were the roofs of the rearmost outbuildings. But as he entered the wood he received a shock. Suddenly he had the consciousness that he was being observed, which comes as from a special sense to those who have lived much in peril of their lives in lonely places. He cowered like a rabbit, and seemed to detect very faint and far-off movements in the undergrowth which were too harsh and sudden for a wild animal. Then they ceased, and the oppression passed. He threaded his way through the undergrowth to his old lair beside a stone, where a tangle of fern hid his head, and there he sat him down to wait.

It was a very wet anchorage. The frozen ground beneath him was melting into slush, rivulets descended from the branches, vagrant winds blew avalanches of melting snow like hail in his face. He grew cold and stiff, and there was no such drama on the stage before him as might have caused him to forget his icy stall. He saw in every detail the morning awakening of a Derbyshire manor. A man with his head tied up in a stocking wheeled barrow-loads of chopped logs from the wood-hovel; another brought milk pails from the byres; while two stable-boys led out to water various horses, among which Alastair recognised those once ridden by Mr. Johnson and Edom. The butler Bennet, wearing a kind of dingy smock, shuffled out-of-doors and cried shrilly for someone who failed to appear. Then came a long spell of quiet﻿—breakfast, thought Alastair. It was broken by a stout fellow in boots, whom he had not seen before, coming from the direction of the kitchen, shouting the name of “Peter.” Peter proved to be one of the stable-boys, who, having been goaded by a flight of oaths into activity, produced in a space of five minutes a horse saddled and bridled and tolerably well groomed. This the man in boots led round to the front of the house, and presently, out from the shelter of the leafless avenue, appeared Sir John Norreys, in a hurry as usual and heading for the bridle-path to Dovedale.

This told Alastair two things. First, that in all likelihood Mr. Kyd had never been to Brightwell, or had left earlier, otherwise Sir John would scarcely have fled his company.
